# Machine Learning for Drug Discovery: Methods, Applications, and Future Directions

## 1 Introduction and Background

### 1.1 The Traditional Drug Discovery Pipeline

The traditional drug discovery and development pipeline represents one of the most complex, resource-intensive, and sequential scientific endeavors undertaken by modern medicine. Bringing a new therapeutic agent from initial concept to patient bedside typically spans more than a decade and demands billions of dollars in investment, with no guarantee of ultimate success [1; 2]. Understanding the individual stages of this pipeline is essential for appreciating both the magnitude of the challenge and the transformative potential that machine learning approaches offer.

**Target Identification and Validation**

The pipeline begins with target identification, where researchers seek to identify biological molecules—most commonly proteins such as enzymes, receptors, ion channels, or transporters—whose modulation is hypothesized to produce a therapeutically beneficial effect in a disease of interest. This stage draws heavily on advances in genomics, proteomics, transcriptomics, and systems biology, employing techniques such as genome-wide association studies (GWAS), gene expression profiling, and network-based analyses to nominate candidate targets [3]. Target validation then follows, requiring experimental and computational evidence that the proposed target is causally linked to the disease and that its modulation is both safe and efficacious. This phase is inherently uncertain; a large proportion of drug failures in later clinical stages can be traced back to insufficient target validation, underscoring the foundational importance of this step.

**Hit Discovery**

Once a validated target has been established, the next stage involves hit discovery—the identification of small molecules or biological entities that demonstrate measurable binding or modulatory activity against the target. Traditionally, this is accomplished through high-throughput screening (HTS) of large chemical libraries, which may contain millions of compounds, or through fragment-based drug discovery and virtual screening approaches [4]. The output of this stage is a collection of "hit" compounds that show promising initial activity but require extensive further optimization before they can be considered viable drug candidates.

**Lead Optimization**

Hit compounds are then subjected to lead optimization, a process in which medicinal chemists systematically modify molecular structures to improve potency, selectivity, pharmacokinetic properties, and safety profiles while reducing liabilities such as toxicity or poor metabolic stability [5; 6]. This stage is particularly resource-intensive, often consuming the majority of the preclinical research budget, and involves iterative cycles of synthesis, biological testing, and structural refinement guided by structure-activity relationship (SAR) analyses. The optimization of multiple molecular properties simultaneously—including absorption, distribution, metabolism, excretion, and toxicity (ADMET) profiles—poses a formidable multi-objective challenge that requires sophisticated experimental and computational tools.

**Preclinical Testing**

Candidate compounds that survive lead optimization are advanced to preclinical testing, which encompasses a comprehensive series of in vitro and in vivo studies designed to characterize the safety, pharmacokinetics, pharmacodynamics, and mechanism of action of the candidate. These studies are conducted in cell-based assays and animal models to generate the data package required to support an Investigational New Drug (IND) application to regulatory authorities. Preclinical testing is not only costly and time-consuming but also raises important questions about the translational relevance of animal models to human disease, contributing to subsequent failures in clinical development.

**Clinical Trials**

Clinical development is organized into three successive phases, each with distinct objectives and increasing scale. Phase I trials primarily assess safety and tolerability in a small number of healthy volunteers or patients, often including dose-escalation studies to determine the maximum tolerated dose [7]. Phase II trials evaluate preliminary efficacy and further characterize safety in a larger patient population, while Phase III trials involve large-scale, randomized, controlled studies designed to confirm efficacy and monitor adverse events across diverse patient populations. The sequential nature of these phases means that failures at any stage result in the loss of all preceding investment, contributing to the extraordinarily high overall cost of drug development [8]. Notably, clinical trial design itself is a complex undertaking requiring careful attention to patient enrollment, endpoint selection, and protocol optimization [9; 10].

**Regulatory Approval and Post-Market Surveillance**

Drugs that successfully complete Phase III trials must then navigate a rigorous regulatory review process conducted by agencies such as the U.S. Food and Drug Administration (FDA) or the European Medicines Agency (EMA). Regulatory submissions require comprehensive documentation of all preclinical and clinical data, chemistry, manufacturing, and controls (CMC) information, and risk management plans [11]. Following approval, post-market surveillance (Phase IV) continues to monitor long-term safety and effectiveness in the broader patient population.

**The Sequential and Resource-Intensive Nature of the Pipeline**

Collectively, these stages form a highly sequential pipeline in which each phase builds upon the outputs of its predecessor, creating critical dependencies and bottleneck points that amplify the consequences of failure at any individual stage [12]. The attrition of candidate compounds across these stages is dramatic; it has been estimated that only a tiny fraction of initially identified hits ultimately achieve regulatory approval. This sequential, resource-intensive architecture, combined with the biological complexity of human disease and the challenge of translating preclinical findings into clinical outcomes, defines the fundamental challenge that modern computational and machine learning approaches seek to address [1; 13]. As the following section elaborates, these compounding inefficiencies—spanning enormous financial costs, protracted timelines, high failure rates, and limited translational success—provide the central motivation for integrating machine learning and artificial intelligence throughout the drug discovery process.

### 1.2 Challenges in Traditional Drug Discovery: Cost, Time, and Attrition

The traditional drug discovery pipeline is widely recognized as one of the most resource-intensive and high-risk endeavors in modern science and industry. As described in the preceding section, the pipeline encompasses a series of highly sequential and interdependent stages—from target identification and hit discovery through lead optimization, preclinical testing, and multi-phase clinical trials—each of which introduces its own distinct challenges and failure modes. Bringing a single new drug to market typically requires an investment exceeding \$2–3 billion and spans a timeline of more than a decade [12; 14]. These staggering costs arise from the cumulative expenses of each pipeline stage, all of which demand substantial human, financial, and infrastructural resources [13].

A particularly acute challenge in this pipeline is the extraordinarily high attrition rate. The vast majority of candidate compounds that enter preclinical development never successfully complete clinical trials, and only a small fraction of those that do enter clinical testing ultimately receive regulatory approval [1]. Clinical failure rates have been attributed to a complex interplay of scientific and translational obstacles, including inadequate efficacy in human populations, unforeseen toxicity, poor pharmacokinetic and pharmacodynamic profiles, and the fundamental difficulty of translating findings from animal models to human disease [15]. These translational gaps are compounded by the limited predictive power of early-stage in vitro and in vivo assays, which often fail to capture the full complexity of human biology.

The attrition problem is further exacerbated by the inherent complexity of the chemical space that must be explored during drug design. It has been estimated that the space of drug-like molecules spans between 10²³ and 10⁶⁰ candidates [16], making exhaustive experimental screening practically infeasible. Conventional high-throughput screening campaigns, while valuable, are costly and time-consuming, and typically yield only a small number of viable lead compounds from vast compound libraries.

From a systemic perspective, the pharmaceutical industry has also faced a productivity crisis characterized by stagnant numbers of new drug approvals despite continuously escalating R&D expenditures [17]. Investigations into clinical trial meta-data have further revealed that innovation in drug discovery is constrained by network effects such as preferential attachment and local network dynamics, which bias exploration toward previously validated drug targets and limit the search for genuinely novel therapeutics [8]. Indeed, analyses suggest that only a small fraction of discovered compounds or drug candidates successfully transition from basic science into clinical research, and the average translational lag from initial discovery to first clinical application has been estimated at over 14 years [18].

These converging challenges—enormous financial costs, protracted timelines, high failure rates, and limited translational success—define the central motivation for integrating computational and artificial intelligence-driven strategies throughout the drug discovery process. As the following section traces, the field has a rich history of progressively more sophisticated computational approaches, from early QSAR models and molecular docking to modern deep learning frameworks, each generation building on the insights and infrastructure of its predecessors in an effort to reduce attrition, prioritize the most promising candidates earlier, and ultimately deliver effective therapies to patients more rapidly and at lower cost [19; 15]. The inadequacy of existing methods has made it clear that new machine learning strategies must be developed and integrated into the pipeline [20], and the historical trajectory reviewed next illuminates both how far the field has come and the foundations upon which contemporary approaches now build.

### 1.3 Historical Milestones in Computational Drug Discovery

The history of computational drug discovery spans several decades, marked by a series of transformative milestones that progressively reshaped how scientists identify, design, and optimize therapeutic candidates. Understanding this trajectory is essential for appreciating how modern machine learning methods emerged from a rich intellectual foundation built by generations of computational chemists, pharmacologists, and informaticians—and how they now offer the transformative potential to address the profound challenges of cost, attrition, and translational failure that have long burdened the pharmaceutical industry.

## Early Foundations: QSAR and the Hansch Era

The formal origins of computational approaches in drug discovery can be traced to the early 1960s, when Corwin Hansch and Toshio Fujita introduced the concept of quantitative structure-activity relationships (QSAR). Their pioneering work established that biological activity could be mathematically correlated with physicochemical properties of molecules—such as lipophilicity, electronic character, and steric parameters—using linear free energy relationships. This foundational insight, that molecular structure encodes biological function in a learnable, quantitative manner, became the cornerstone upon which all subsequent computational drug discovery methodologies were built [21; 22].

The Hansch-Fujita approach gave rise to a broad tradition of QSAR modeling, where researchers sought to derive predictive equations relating structural descriptors to bioactivity endpoints. Free-Wilson analysis, introduced around the same period, provided an alternative fragment-based additive model for relating substituent patterns to activity. Throughout the 1970s and 1980s, the QSAR paradigm expanded significantly, incorporating a growing arsenal of molecular descriptors—topological indices, quantum-chemical parameters, and connectivity indices—enabling increasingly sophisticated models of structure-activity relationships. These early QSAR methods, though limited by the computational resources of the era and the simplicity of linear regression frameworks, established the fundamental principle that machine learning-style predictive modeling could guide medicinal chemistry decisions [23].

## Molecular Docking and Structure-Based Methods

A parallel and equally transformative thread in the history of computational drug discovery was the emergence of structure-based drug design (SBDD), made possible by advances in X-ray crystallography and nuclear magnetic resonance spectroscopy that began yielding high-resolution three-dimensional structures of proteins in the 1970s and 1980s. The availability of protein structural data catalyzed the development of molecular docking algorithms—computational methods designed to predict the preferred binding orientation of a small molecule ligand within a protein active site.

Early docking programs such as DOCK, developed by Kuntz and colleagues at UCSF in the early 1980s, represented a conceptual breakthrough: for the first time, it became feasible to computationally screen libraries of molecules for their geometric complementarity to a known receptor structure. Subsequent generations of docking software—including AutoDock, FlexX, GOLD, and Glide—progressively incorporated more sophisticated treatments of molecular flexibility, solvation effects, and thermodynamic scoring, significantly improving the accuracy of binding pose prediction and virtual screening enrichment [24; 25].

The development of scoring functions—mathematical models that estimate the free energy of binding from structural features of a docked complex—became a particularly active area of research. Classical physics-based scoring functions drew on molecular mechanics force fields and empirical parameterizations of hydrogen bonding, hydrophobic contacts, and entropic penalties. Knowledge-based scoring functions derived statistical potentials from databases of crystallographic complexes, capturing average interaction preferences without explicit physical assumptions. These scoring approaches, while imperfect in their accuracy, demonstrated that computational methods could provide practically useful guidance for prioritizing compounds in drug discovery campaigns [26].

## The Rise of Cheminformatics and Molecular Fingerprints

The 1990s witnessed the coalescence of a new discipline—cheminformatics—dedicated to the application of informatics techniques to chemical data. Central to this development was the invention and proliferation of molecular fingerprint representations: compact binary or integer encodings that capture the presence or absence of structural fragments, subgraphs, or pharmacophoric features within a molecule. Daylight fingerprints, introduced in the early 1990s, encoded circular neighborhoods around each atom, while MACCS keys encoded the presence of 166 predefined structural keys. Extended Connectivity Fingerprints (ECFP), developed by circular algorithm variants, became among the most widely used representations for similarity searching and QSAR modeling.

These fingerprint-based representations enabled efficient computation of molecular similarity using metrics such as the Tanimoto coefficient, facilitating large-scale virtual screening by ligand-based methods. Pharmacophore modeling—the abstraction of essential interaction features (hydrogen bond donors and acceptors, hydrophobic regions, charged groups) required for biological activity—emerged as another powerful cheminformatics tool for virtual screening and lead hopping [22; 27].

The growth of chemical databases during this period—including the Cambridge Structural Database, the Protein Data Bank, and commercial repositories such as the MDL Drug Data Report—provided increasingly rich datasets for training and validating computational models. The development of relational database systems and cheminformatics toolkits such as Daylight Chemical Information Systems and later RDKit and OpenBabel democratized access to computational chemistry methods, enabling broader adoption across academia and industry. This progressive accumulation of structured chemical and biological data laid the groundwork for the data-driven machine learning revolution that would follow.

## Computer-Aided Drug Design and the Integration of Multiple Methods

By the late 1990s and early 2000s, the field had matured sufficiently that integrated computer-aided drug design (CADD) workflows began to emerge, combining ligand-based and structure-based methods within coherent drug discovery pipelines. Homology modeling extended SBDD to targets lacking experimental structures by building protein models from evolutionarily related templates. Pharmacophore models derived from known actives could be overlaid with docking results to enhance screening selectivity. Free energy perturbation and thermodynamic integration calculations, though computationally expensive, offered rigorous estimates of relative binding affinities for lead optimization.

The application of high-throughput virtual screening—computationally screening millions of compounds against protein targets using docking or similarity-based methods—became increasingly common as both computational resources and compound libraries grew. This era also saw the formalization of drug-likeness criteria such as Lipinski's Rule of Five, published in 1997, which codified empirical filters for oral bioavailability based on simple physicochemical descriptors, providing practical heuristics for prioritizing compounds in virtual screening campaigns [22]. These integrated CADD workflows represented an important precursor to the fully ML-driven pipelines that would emerge in subsequent years, establishing the conceptual architecture of multi-stage computational screening that modern approaches have inherited and extended.

## Machine Learning Enters the Scene: From Classical to Modern Methods

The early 2000s marked the beginning of more systematic applications of machine learning to drug discovery problems. Support vector machines (SVMs), random forests, and neural networks were applied to QSAR modeling, demonstrating advantages over classical linear regression in handling nonlinear structure-activity relationships and high-dimensional descriptor spaces. Comparative evaluations across large benchmark datasets began to establish which methods performed best for different property prediction tasks, though the results were often dataset-dependent and no single algorithm emerged as universally superior [23; 21; 28].

The advent of deep learning in the early 2010s represented a qualitative shift in the computational drug discovery landscape. The success of deep neural networks in image recognition, natural language processing, and speech recognition demonstrated that hierarchical learned representations could dramatically outperform hand-crafted features in complex prediction tasks. Researchers quickly recognized the potential of deep learning for molecular property prediction, particularly with the development of graph neural networks that could operate directly on molecular graph representations—capturing structural information without the information loss inherent in fixed-length fingerprint encodings [27; 22].

Convolutional neural networks applied to molecular representations, deep architectures for SMILES string processing, and early graph convolutional networks for property prediction all appeared within a few years of each other in the mid-2010s, collectively establishing that deep learning could provide substantial improvements over classical methods for molecular property prediction, virtual screening, and drug-target interaction modeling [29; 27]. This wave of deep learning innovation coincided with, and was substantially enabled by, the very drivers of data abundance, computational infrastructure, and architectural breakthroughs that characterize the modern AI-in-drug-discovery landscape discussed in this survey.

## From Predictive Modeling to Generative Design

Perhaps the most transformative development of the late 2010s was the emergence of deep generative models capable of designing entirely new molecules with desired properties—a paradigm shift from the reactive task of predicting properties of existing molecules to the proactive task of generating novel chemical matter. Variational autoencoders, generative adversarial networks, and recurrent neural network-based generative models demonstrated that deep learning could navigate the vast chemical space of drug-like molecules to propose candidates with tailored profiles of activity, selectivity, and drug-likeness [29; 30].

Reinforcement learning frameworks further enriched the generative design paradigm by enabling goal-directed optimization of molecular properties, allowing models to iteratively refine generated structures toward multi-objective targets encompassing binding affinity, ADMET properties, and synthetic accessibility [31]. The coupling of generative models with molecular docking evaluations as reward signals represented a particularly impactful methodological advance, closing the loop between molecular generation and structure-based activity estimation [32]. These generative capabilities directly addressed one of the core challenges identified earlier—the intractability of exhaustively exploring the vast chemical space of drug-like molecules—by learning to navigate and exploit that space intelligently.

## The AlphaFold Revolution and Structure-Based Discovery at Scale

The release of AlphaFold2 by DeepMind in 2021 marked what many regard as the single most impactful event in computational structural biology in decades. By demonstrating that protein three-dimensional structure could be predicted from amino acid sequence with accuracy rivaling experimental crystallography, AlphaFold2 addressed one of the most fundamental bottlenecks in structure-based drug design: the availability of target protein structures. The rapid expansion of the AlphaFold Protein Structure Database to encompass virtually all known protein sequences dramatically expanded the universe of druggable targets accessible to computational methods, enabling structure-based drug discovery campaigns against previously intractable targets [33].

Concurrent with AlphaFold's rise, deep learning-based docking and binding affinity prediction methods underwent rapid development, replacing classical physics-based scoring functions with learned models capable of exploiting rich structural features of protein-ligand complexes. Equivariant neural network architectures respecting the rotational and translational symmetries of three-dimensional molecular structures emerged as a particularly powerful framework for this task [33]. Together, these structural advances began to deliver on the long-standing promise of structure-based drug design at a scale and accuracy previously unachievable through experimental or classical computational means alone.

## Toward Foundation Models and Autonomous Discovery

The most recent chapter in the history of computational drug discovery has been defined by the emergence of large-scale foundation models—pre-trained on billions of molecular structures, protein sequences, and biomedical texts—and the vision of autonomous, AI-driven closed-loop drug discovery pipelines. These developments represent the culmination of decades of progress in computational methods, database construction, and machine learning theory, and they set the stage for the comprehensive machine learning approaches reviewed throughout this survey [27; 34]. In this sense, the historical arc of the field directly connects to the contemporary landscape of AI-driven pharmaceutical research—characterized by exploding biomedical data, powerful computing infrastructure, transformative deep learning architectures, and growing industrial investment—that defines the context for the methods and applications examined in the sections that follow.

In retrospect, the history of computational drug discovery traces a remarkably coherent arc: from the linear regression QSAR models of the 1960s, through the geometric complementarity algorithms of early molecular docking, through the cheminformatics revolution of the 1990s, to the deep learning-enabled predictive, generative, and structural methods of today. Each era built upon the insights and infrastructure of its predecessors, and the field's current sophistication reflects the accumulated contributions of an extraordinarily diverse community of scientists working at the intersection of chemistry, biology, physics, computer science, and medicine. The machine learning methods examined in subsequent sections of this survey are the direct intellectual descendants of this rich historical tradition, and their continued development promises to address the enduring challenges of drug discovery with ever-greater efficiency, accuracy, and scope.

### 1.4 The Rise of Machine Learning and Artificial Intelligence in Pharmaceutical Research

The past decade has witnessed a remarkable emergence and rapid growth of machine learning (ML) and artificial intelligence (AI) technologies within pharmaceutical research and drug discovery, fundamentally reshaping how the field approaches the complex challenges of identifying and developing new therapeutics [27; 19]. As the historical trajectory outlined in the preceding section makes clear, this transformation did not emerge in a vacuum—it represents the culmination of decades of incremental progress in computational methods, database construction, and algorithmic theory. Yet the pace and character of change over the past ten years represent a genuine discontinuity, driven by a confluence of several powerful and mutually reinforcing factors that have collectively created fertile ground for AI-driven approaches to gain traction across drug discovery workflows.

**The Explosion of Biomedical Data**

One of the most fundamental drivers underpinning the rise of AI in pharmaceutical research is the unprecedented explosion of biomedical data generated across multiple scientific domains. High-throughput experimental technologies, including genomic sequencing platforms, proteomics assays, high-content imaging systems, and large-scale phenotypic screening campaigns, have produced vast repositories of molecular, biological, and clinical information at a scale that far exceeds the capacity of traditional manual analysis [35]. Large-scale molecular databases cataloging compound structures, biological activities, protein sequences, interaction networks, and clinical outcomes have become increasingly accessible to the research community [36]. This data abundance provides the essential raw material upon which modern ML models depend, enabling the training of complex, high-capacity models that can learn intricate structure-property relationships and biological patterns from millions of annotated examples [27; 22].

The curation of large-scale, publicly accessible chemical databases, bioactivity repositories, and multi-omics datasets has been particularly transformative. Researchers now have access to databases containing billions of drug-like molecules alongside pre-computed molecular descriptors, fingerprints, and structural properties [37]. The availability of such resources has dramatically lowered the barriers to developing and benchmarking new ML models, accelerating the pace of methodological innovation and enabling direct comparisons across competing approaches—addressing, in a meaningful way, the data scarcity that historically constrained the generalizability of earlier QSAR and cheminformatics methods.

**Advances in Computing Infrastructure**

The rise of AI in drug discovery has been inextricably linked to transformative advances in computing infrastructure, particularly high-performance computing (HPC) and the emergence of exascale resources. Training sophisticated deep learning models on large molecular datasets demands enormous computational power, memory capacity, and efficient parallel processing capabilities that were simply unavailable in earlier eras [38]. The maturation of GPU-accelerated computing, combined with increasingly accessible cloud-based infrastructure, has enabled researchers to train models with hundreds of millions or even billions of parameters within practical timeframes.

Exascale computing resources have further expanded the scope of what is computationally feasible in AI-driven drug discovery, enabling large-scale virtual screening campaigns and the integration of physics-based molecular simulation with ML scoring functions at unprecedented throughput [13; 39]. These infrastructure advances have made it possible to screen billions of molecules computationally within days rather than years, dramatically compressing the timescales associated with early-stage drug discovery [39]—directly confronting the cost and timeline challenges that have historically burdened pharmaceutical R&D. The convergence of AI and HPC has therefore been a critical enabler, allowing both model development and large-scale application deployment at scales that were previously unimaginable [38].

**Breakthroughs in Deep Learning Architectures**

Perhaps the most intellectually significant driver of AI's rise in pharmaceutical research has been the series of landmark breakthroughs in deep learning architectures over the past decade [19; 1]. The development and refinement of convolutional neural networks, recurrent neural networks, attention mechanisms, graph neural networks, transformer architectures, and generative models such as variational autoencoders, generative adversarial networks, and diffusion models has provided researchers with a powerful and flexible toolkit for modeling complex molecular data [40; 41].

Graph neural networks, in particular, have proven exceptionally well-suited to molecular data, as the natural graph-theoretic representation of molecular structures as atoms and bonds maps directly onto the node-and-edge formalism of these architectures [42]. This architectural alignment between molecular structure and graph-based deep learning has spurred rapid methodological development in property prediction, drug-target interaction modeling, and de novo molecular generation—extending and substantially superseding the fixed-length fingerprint representations that defined the cheminformatics era. Simultaneously, transformer-based architectures and large language models have demonstrated remarkable capacity to learn from sequential molecular representations such as SMILES strings, treating chemistry as a kind of molecular language amenable to the same self-supervised learning approaches that have revolutionized natural language processing [43; 44].

The generalizability and representational power of these modern architectures have also enabled multi-task learning frameworks that simultaneously optimize across multiple molecular properties, improving data efficiency and predictive accuracy in settings where labeled experimental data is scarce [45]. The practical impact of these architectural innovations is evidenced by the growing number of AI-designed compounds entering preclinical and clinical development pipelines [1], and sets the stage for the landmark AI milestones in drug discovery discussed in the following section.

**Growing Availability of Large-Scale Molecular Databases and Open Science Ecosystems**

The development of open-access molecular databases, standardized benchmarking platforms, and open-source ML toolkits has created a collaborative scientific ecosystem that has dramatically accelerated the adoption of AI in pharmaceutical research [46]. Platforms such as TorchDrug provide researchers with standardized implementations of state-of-the-art methods alongside curated benchmark datasets, enabling rigorous and reproducible comparisons across the diverse methods landscape [46]. The emergence of this open science infrastructure has lowered the entry barrier for computational researchers, fostered cross-disciplinary collaboration, and promoted the rapid dissemination of methodological innovations—building upon the tradition of database curation and shared toolkits that began with repositories like the Cambridge Structural Database and early cheminformatics software.

**Increasing Industrial Investment and Academic Interest**

The convergence of data, computation, and algorithmic advances has attracted substantial investment from both the pharmaceutical industry and the broader technology sector, further accelerating the pace of AI adoption in drug discovery [20; 15]. Major pharmaceutical companies have established dedicated AI research units and entered into high-profile partnerships with AI-focused biotechnology companies [47]. Simultaneously, academic research groups have contributed a torrent of methodological innovations spanning molecular representation learning, generative chemistry, structure-based design, and multi-omics data integration [19; 48].

This industrial-academic synergy has been essential in translating methodological advances from theoretical demonstrations into practically impactful tools. The result is a rapidly maturing field in which AI is no longer viewed merely as a promising experimental approach but increasingly as an indispensable component of modern pharmaceutical research workflows [1; 20]. The collective momentum generated by these converging drivers—data abundance, computational power, architectural innovation, open science infrastructure, and industrial investment—has firmly established machine learning and AI as transformative forces in the pursuit of new therapeutics, and directly underpins the landmark real-world successes examined in the section that follows.

### 1.5 Landmark Successes and Proof-of-Concept Applications

The past decade has witnessed a series of landmark successes that have moved machine learning and AI in drug discovery from theoretical promise to tangible, real-world impact. Building on the confluence of data abundance, computational advances, and architectural breakthroughs described in the previous section, these milestones span early proof-of-concept demonstrations, high-profile industry collaborations, and, most recently, AI-generated molecules advancing into clinical development—collectively validating the transformative potential of data-driven approaches across the pharmaceutical pipeline.

**Early AI-Assisted Compound Identification**

Among the earliest and most compelling demonstrations of AI's utility in drug discovery were applications in virtual screening and compound prioritization. Deep neural networks trained on large-scale bioactivity datasets were shown to substantially outperform traditional docking and fingerprint-based methods in identifying active compounds against disease-relevant targets. For instance, large-scale ligand-based virtual screening campaigns leveraging deep neural networks trained on over 220 million data points across millions of molecules demonstrated the feasibility of screening one billion compounds from databases such as ZINC for potential inhibitors, illustrating both the scalability and predictive power of deep learning in early-stage drug discovery [49]. These efforts established that AI models could serve as high-throughput filters capable of dramatically reducing the experimental search space while maintaining or improving hit rates compared to conventional computational methods [50].

In parallel, proof-of-concept frameworks combining AI models with experimental validation were developed to demonstrate the iterative power of closed-loop discovery. One notable example is the AIMEE framework, which integrated deep learning-based property prediction with enzymological experiments to identify inhibitors against the SARS-CoV-2 3CL protease. Starting from a bioactive chemical library, two rounds of AI-guided experimentation identified six novel inhibitors with a hit rate of approximately 29%, with four compounds exhibiting IC50 values below 3 μM—a striking demonstration of how AI can accelerate the identification of bioactive compounds while also providing interpretable insights into the chemical features driving activity [51].

**Drug Repurposing Campaigns: COVID-19 as a Catalyst**

The COVID-19 pandemic served as an unprecedented catalyst for demonstrating AI's value in drug repurposing—the identification of new therapeutic uses for existing, approved compounds. Given the urgency of the global health crisis and the time and cost constraints of de novo drug development, repurposing offered a compelling near-term strategy, and AI provided powerful tools to accelerate it. Multiple research groups rapidly deployed graph neural networks, knowledge graph embeddings, and deep learning frameworks to prioritize repurposable drug candidates against SARS-CoV-2 targets.

A prominent example is the CoV-KGE methodology, which constructed a comprehensive biomedical knowledge graph encompassing 15 million edges spanning drugs, diseases, genes, pathways, and expression data derived from over 24 million PubMed publications. Network-based deep learning applied to this knowledge graph identified 41 repurposable drug candidates—including indomethacin, toremifene, and niclosamide—whose predicted therapeutic associations with COVID-19 were subsequently validated by transcriptomic and proteomic data from SARS-CoV-2-infected human cells as well as data from ongoing clinical trials [52]. Similarly, graph neural network-based drug repurposing pipelines integrating virus-host protein interaction networks, pathway data, and drug properties prioritized candidate drugs and highlighted potential synergistic drug combinations for COVID-19, with top candidates including widely used therapeutics such as Azithromycin and Aspirin, later validated by genetic profiles and in vitro experimental efficacy data [53].

The rapid mobilization of AI and high-performance computing (HPC) resources during the pandemic further demonstrated the infrastructure readiness for AI-driven drug discovery at scale. Large-scale data releases aggregating over 4.2 billion molecules with pre-computed molecular fingerprints, 2D images, and 3D molecular descriptors were made publicly available to accelerate community-wide ML model development and virtual screening efforts [37]. Hybrid workflows combining physics-based molecular simulations with machine learning scoring functions were deployed on supercomputing infrastructure to screen inhibitors against multiple SARS-CoV-2 target proteins at previously infeasible throughput levels [13; 54]. These pandemic-era campaigns collectively demonstrated that AI-driven drug repurposing could generate actionable leads within weeks rather than years—a paradigm shift relative to conventional timelines.

Beyond COVID-19, machine-learning-driven drug repurposing approaches have been systematically applied to other complex diseases, leveraging multi-omics integration, sequence-based representations, and graph-based deep learning to uncover non-obvious therapeutic connections across approved drug databases [55; 56].

**AI-Assisted Generative Design and Molecules Entering Clinical Trials**

One of the most compelling milestones in AI-driven drug discovery is the entry of AI-generated molecules into clinical trials—a development that has transformed AI from a predictive tool into an active participant in the medicinal chemistry design cycle. Generative models including variational autoencoders, reinforcement learning frameworks, and graph-based generative architectures have been used to design novel chemical entities with desired pharmacological profiles, moving beyond virtual screening to propose entirely new molecular structures.

Reinforcement learning-based and autoencoder-based generative models have been applied to design novel candidates targeting SARS-CoV-2 viral proteins, optimizing simultaneously for drug-likeness, synthetic accessibility, and antiviral activity, illustrating the multi-objective design capabilities of these frameworks [57]. Multi-task graph generative models have been proposed to bridge the historically problematic gap between target-based in vitro activity and cell-based in vivo efficacy, designing compounds with both target inhibitory and cell-active properties and demonstrating that generative AI can directly address one of the most persistent failure modes in the drug discovery pipeline [58].

The broader pharmaceutical industry has also reported that AI-enabled generative chemistry, machine learning, and multi-property optimization have enabled several compounds to enter clinical trials, marking the maturation of generative AI from a research curiosity to a component of active drug development programs [1]. Active learning-coupled generative workflows—such as variational autoencoders combined with iterative active learning using drug-likeness, synthesizability, molecular similarity, and docking score metrics—have demonstrated the ability to generate chemically viable, high-affinity molecules with novel scaffolds for clinically validated targets such as CDK2 and KRAS, further establishing the practical potential of generative AI in lead identification and optimization [59].

**High-Profile Industry Collaborations and Ecosystem Maturation**

The success of these technical demonstrations has been accompanied by a rapid maturation of the AI drug discovery ecosystem, including high-profile industry collaborations between pharmaceutical companies and dedicated AI-driven biotech firms. These partnerships have brought together large proprietary bioactivity datasets, domain expertise in medicinal chemistry, and state-of-the-art AI capabilities, resulting in outcomes such as significantly reduced molecule discovery timelines—with some reports citing tenfold reductions—and the creation of the largest molecule toxicity databases to date [20]. Such collaborations have also resulted in the development of over 200 drug safety predictive models and have attracted substantial investment from technology giants, further accelerating the adoption of AI across the pharmaceutical R&D value chain [20; 15].

These landmark achievements collectively illustrate that machine learning and AI have crossed a critical threshold—from proof-of-concept demonstrations in academic settings to validated contributors in real-world pharmaceutical discovery programs. At the same time, they underscore the importance of addressing remaining challenges in data quality, reproducibility, and experimental validation to fully realize the promise of AI-driven drug discovery [27; 60]. With this context of demonstrated real-world impact in mind, the following section provides a systematic taxonomy of the major methodological categories that underpin these successes and that form the core focus of this survey.

### 1.6 Overview of Machine Learning Approaches in Drug Discovery

Machine learning has emerged as a transformative force across virtually every stage of the drug discovery pipeline, offering computational strategies that complement and accelerate traditional experimental approaches. The landmark demonstrations described in the preceding section—spanning virtual screening campaigns, AI-assisted drug repurposing during the COVID-19 pandemic, and AI-generated molecules advancing into clinical trials—reflect the breadth of methodological advances that now underpin AI-driven pharmaceutical research. The diversity of ML methods being applied mirrors the multifaceted nature of drug discovery itself, spanning molecular property prediction, de novo molecular design, drug-target interaction modeling, structure-based design, and knowledge graph reasoning. Below, we provide a concise taxonomy of these major methodological categories, setting the stage for the detailed technical discussions in subsequent sections—and directly anticipating the organizational structure of this survey introduced in the following section.

**Predictive Modeling for Molecular Property Prediction.** A foundational application of machine learning in drug discovery is the prediction of key molecular properties—including binding affinity, solubility, toxicity, and ADMET profiles—from molecular representations. Early approaches relied on fixed molecular fingerprints and classical machine learning algorithms, but recent advances have introduced graph neural networks (GNNs) and transformer-based architectures that learn representations directly from molecular graphs and SMILES strings [61; 22]. These models enable rapid virtual screening of large compound libraries, significantly reducing the cost and time associated with experimental assays [28; 62]. As illustrated by the AI-assisted virtual screening campaigns reviewed in the preceding section, predictive models of this kind have already demonstrated their practical utility in real-world discovery programs.

**Deep Generative Models for De Novo Drug Design.** Beyond property prediction, generative models have opened the possibility of designing entirely novel drug-like molecules with desired pharmacological properties. Approaches spanning variational autoencoders (VAEs), generative adversarial networks (GANs), recurrent neural networks (RNNs), normalizing flows, and diffusion models have been employed to explore vast chemical spaces and propose candidate molecules [40; 63]. These models can be conditioned on target properties or protein pocket geometries to perform goal-directed generation and lead optimization [30; 64]. The entry of AI-generated molecules into clinical trials, highlighted in the preceding section, stands as perhaps the most compelling validation of this methodological direction.

**Graph-Based Methods for Drug-Target and Drug-Drug Interaction Prediction.** Graph machine learning has proven particularly powerful for modeling the complex relational structure of biological and chemical systems. GNN-based architectures have been extensively applied to drug-target interaction (DTI) prediction, drug-target binding affinity (DTA) estimation, and drug-drug interaction (DDI) prediction, leveraging graph representations of molecules and proteins to capture structural and functional relationships [65; 66]. Multi-view and attention-based fusion strategies further enhance these predictions by integrating heterogeneous molecular descriptors and protein representations [67; 68].

**Structure-Based Approaches Leveraging Protein Structure Prediction.** The advent of highly accurate protein structure prediction tools such as AlphaFold has catalyzed a renaissance in structure-based drug design (SBDD). Deep learning models now enable enhanced molecular docking, binding site identification, protein-ligand affinity scoring, and de novo generation of molecules tailored to specific protein pockets [41; 69]. Geometric deep learning frameworks, which respect the SE(3) and E(3) symmetries of 3D molecular systems, have emerged as a principled approach for modeling protein-ligand complexes and guiding the generation of stereospecific, high-affinity candidates [70]. These structure-based capabilities are particularly relevant to the hybrid physics- and ML-based workflows that were deployed at scale during the pandemic-era campaigns described in the preceding section.

**Knowledge Graph Reasoning and Multi-Source Data Integration.** Knowledge graphs (KGs) provide a powerful framework for integrating heterogeneous biomedical data—including drug, gene, disease, and pathway information—to support drug repurposing, DDI prediction, and target identification [36; 42]. Knowledge graph embedding methods, combined with graph neural networks and multi-hop reasoning, enable inference over rich relational data and provide interpretable predictions that can guide experimental follow-up [71; 72]. The CoV-KGE and related drug repurposing frameworks highlighted in the preceding section exemplify the practical power of this methodological family.

**Self-Supervised and Pretraining Strategies.** A cross-cutting theme across all these methodological categories is the adoption of self-supervised and pretraining paradigms to address the chronic scarcity of labeled molecular data. By pretraining on large-scale unlabeled molecular databases and subsequently fine-tuning on task-specific datasets, models such as MPG and KPGT achieve strong generalization across diverse drug discovery benchmarks [73; 74]. Multi-modal pretraining strategies that combine molecular graphs, SMILES strings, images, and biological assay data further enrich molecular representations [75; 76].

Together, these six methodological categories constitute a rich and rapidly evolving toolkit for AI-driven drug discovery, and they directly map onto the organizational structure of this survey as described in the following section. Standardized platforms such as TorchDrug [46] have begun to unify these diverse methods under common benchmarking and evaluation frameworks, facilitating reproducible comparisons and accelerating the adoption of machine learning across the pharmaceutical research community. The following sections of this survey provide comprehensive technical reviews of each of these major methodological directions, examining their architectures, benchmark performance, and practical applications across the drug discovery pipeline.

### 1.7 Scope, Organization, and Objectives of This Survey

## 1.7 Scope, Organization, and Objectives of This Survey

The rapid proliferation of machine learning methods applied to drug discovery has generated a rich but sprawling literature that can be challenging for both newcomers and experienced researchers to navigate. Building upon the methodological taxonomy introduced in the preceding section—which identified predictive modeling, generative design, graph-based interaction prediction, structure-based approaches, knowledge graph reasoning, and self-supervised pretraining as the major pillars of the field—this survey aims to provide a comprehensive, structured, and up-to-date overview spanning the full breadth of these techniques and their applications across the drug discovery pipeline, from molecular representation learning and property prediction to generative molecular design, drug-target interaction prediction, and structure-based drug design.

### Scope and Coverage

This survey covers machine learning methods applied to small molecule drug discovery, with selective coverage of protein-centric tasks where they directly interface with ligand-based drug design. Our scope encompasses:

- **Molecular representation learning**, including string-based, fingerprint-based, graph-based, and 3D representations, as well as self-supervised and contrastive pretraining strategies [27; 42].
- **Molecular property prediction** and virtual screening, covering graph neural networks, transformer-based models, multi-task learning, uncertainty quantification, and few-shot learning [22; 77].
- **Molecular generation and lead optimization**, including variational autoencoders, generative adversarial networks, diffusion models, reinforcement learning, and fragment-based approaches [2; 64].
- **Drug-target interaction, drug-drug interaction, and knowledge graph reasoning**, covering graph-based methods, attention mechanisms, multimodal fusion, and biomedical knowledge graph embeddings [42; 36].
- **Structure-based drug design and protein structure prediction**, including AlphaFold integration, machine learning-enhanced docking, binding site identification, and 3D generative models [41].
- **Cross-cutting challenges and future directions**, encompassing data scarcity, out-of-distribution generalization, reproducibility, explainability, federated learning, large language models, quantum machine learning, and autonomous closed-loop discovery [1; 15; 78].

We deliberately focus on methods that have demonstrated practical utility or strong theoretical foundations in the drug discovery context. While we acknowledge adjacent fields such as protein structure prediction and computational chemistry, we include them only insofar as they directly enable or enhance ligand-centric drug discovery tasks.

### Literature Selection Criteria

The papers reviewed in this survey were selected based on the following criteria: (1) direct relevance to machine learning or artificial intelligence applied to drug discovery tasks; (2) methodological novelty or significant empirical contribution; (3) coverage of benchmark evaluations on established datasets; and (4) representative coverage of diverse subfields and methodological families. We also include benchmark platforms and evaluation frameworks that are essential for understanding the state of model comparison in the field [46; 25]. We prioritize peer-reviewed publications but also include high-impact preprints where they represent important methodological advances. Our review spans foundational works that established key paradigms as well as recent developments reflecting the current frontier of the field.

### Organizational Structure

The remainder of this survey is organized as follows, with each section corresponding directly to one of the major methodological categories identified in the preceding taxonomy. **Section 2** reviews molecular representations and pretraining strategies, covering the diverse ways molecules can be encoded for machine learning models. **Section 3** examines methods for molecular property prediction and virtual screening, including graph neural networks, transformer-based models, and uncertainty-aware approaches [79]. **Section 4** surveys deep generative models for de novo molecular design and lead optimization, spanning VAEs, GANs, diffusion models, reinforcement learning, and multi-objective optimization strategies [80; 59]. **Section 5** addresses drug-target interaction and drug-drug interaction prediction, including knowledge graph reasoning and multimodal data integration [44]. **Section 6** covers structure-based drug design, including protein structure prediction, machine learning-enhanced docking, binding site identification, and 3D generative models [41; 81]. **Section 7** addresses open challenges and future directions, including interpretability [78; 82], federated learning, causality, large language models, quantum machine learning, and autonomous closed-loop discovery systems.

### Objectives and Intended Audience

The primary objectives of this survey are: (1) to provide a unified and structured reference for researchers entering the field of machine learning for drug discovery; (2) to highlight methodological connections and distinctions across the diverse subfields surveyed above; (3) to critically assess the state of benchmarking and evaluation practices [46]; (4) to identify persistent open challenges and promising research directions; and (5) to offer a resource that bridges the perspectives of machine learning researchers and pharmaceutical scientists.

This survey is intended for a broad audience including machine learning researchers seeking to apply their expertise to drug discovery, computational chemists and bioinformaticians looking to adopt modern deep learning approaches, and pharmaceutical scientists interested in understanding the capabilities and limitations of AI-driven methods [72; 27]. By synthesizing the methodological landscape introduced in this opening chapter into a coherent and detailed technical treatment, we hope this survey will serve not only as a comprehensive reference but also as a practical guide for navigating the rapidly evolving landscape of machine learning in drug discovery, stimulating further methodological innovation and cross-disciplinary collaboration in this exciting and consequential field.

## 2 Molecular Representations and Pretraining Strategies

### 2.1 String-Based Molecular Representations

String-based molecular representations encode chemical structures as linear sequences of characters, providing a compact and human-readable format that is naturally compatible with sequence modeling architectures. Among these representations, the Simplified Molecular Input Line Entry System (SMILES) is the most widely adopted, encoding atoms, bonds, branching points, and ring structures using a defined grammar of ASCII characters. A SMILES string traverses the molecular graph in a depth-first manner, representing atoms as elemental symbols, bonds as single characters (e.g., `=` for double bonds), and ring closures as numeric indices. While SMILES is compact and expressive, it suffers from a critical limitation: small perturbations to a string—such as reordering atoms or altering a single character—can yield an invalid molecule or a chemically distinct structure, making validity a persistent concern in generative modeling and string manipulation tasks.

To address the fragility of SMILES, the SELFIES (Self-Referencing Embedded Strings) representation was introduced as a robust alternative in which every possible string corresponds to a valid molecule by construction. SELFIES employs a context-sensitive grammar with derivation rules that guarantee chemical validity regardless of the string content, making it particularly attractive for generative models that sample or mutate strings in latent space [2]. This robustness property has led to increased adoption in variational autoencoder (VAE) and reinforcement learning (RL) frameworks for molecular generation, where the absence of validity constraints simplifies training and exploration.

Beyond SMILES and SELFIES, the IUPAC International Chemical Identifier (InChI) provides a hierarchical, canonical string representation designed for unambiguous chemical identification. InChI encodes molecular structure in layered components covering the molecular formula, connectivity, hydrogen positions, stereochemistry, and isotopic information. While InChI is primarily used for database indexing and chemical structure standardization rather than generative modeling, it offers deterministic canonicalization that eliminates the ambiguity present in SMILES, where a single molecule can have multiple valid string representations. IUPAC systematic names, while human-readable and chemically informative, are rarely used directly as machine learning inputs due to their verbosity and irregular grammatical structure, although recent large language model (LLM) approaches have explored parsing and generating IUPAC names as part of multimodal molecular understanding [2].

The tokenization of string-based representations is a fundamental preprocessing step that determines how sequence models process molecular information. For SMILES, tokenization strategies range from character-level tokenization—treating each ASCII character as an individual token—to atom-level tokenization that groups multi-character atomic symbols (e.g., `Cl`, `Br`) and bond descriptors as single tokens, reducing vocabulary size and improving alignment between tokens and chemically meaningful units. For SELFIES, tokenization naturally aligns with the semantic units of the grammar, where each token corresponds to a complete derivation rule rather than an individual character, producing shorter sequences with more informative tokens.

Sequence models including recurrent neural networks (RNNs), long short-term memory networks (LSTMs), and gated recurrent units (GRUs) were among the first deep learning architectures applied to SMILES-based molecular generation and property prediction [83; 2]. These models treat SMILES strings as sequential data and learn to predict the next token given prior context, enabling autoregressive generation of novel molecules. Transfer learning strategies, where RNN-based models are pretrained on large general chemical databases such as ChEMBL or ZINC and subsequently fine-tuned on focused datasets of biologically active compounds, have demonstrated the ability to generate libraries enriched in drug-like scaffolds relevant to specific targets [2].

More recently, Transformer-based chemical language models have largely superseded RNNs for string-based molecular representation learning, owing to their capacity for parallelized training and long-range dependency modeling through multi-head self-attention. Models such as ChemBERTa, SMILES-BERT, and MolBERT adapt the BERT pretraining paradigm to molecular SMILES strings, employing masked token prediction objectives to learn contextual embeddings of molecular substructures from large unlabeled chemical corpora. These pretrained representations can be fine-tuned on downstream tasks including molecular property prediction, virtual screening, and ADMET modeling, often outperforming classical fingerprint-based baselines [2]. The integration of positional encodings, attention masking to enforce chemical grammar constraints, and domain-specific vocabulary construction are key design choices that distinguish chemical language models from their natural language processing counterparts. In this way, string-based representations serve as a natural bridge between the classical fingerprint and descriptor methods discussed previously and the more structurally expressive graph-based representations introduced in the following subsection.

A notable challenge for all string-based representations in generative modeling is the disconnect between string-level validity and molecular novelty or synthesizability [84]. Generative models operating in SMILES space may produce syntactically valid strings corresponding to chemically unusual or synthetically inaccessible structures, motivating complementary validity filters, retrosynthetic accessibility scores, and multi-objective optimization frameworks that incorporate synthesizability constraints alongside property optimization. Despite these challenges, the simplicity, wide toolchain support, and compatibility of string representations with the powerful ecosystem of sequence modeling architectures continue to make SMILES and SELFIES central representations in machine learning for drug discovery.

### 2.2 Molecular Fingerprints and Descriptor-Based Representations

## 2.1 Molecular Fingerprints and Physicochemical Descriptors

Molecular fingerprints and descriptor-based representations constitute one of the most foundational and widely employed paradigms for encoding chemical information in machine learning-driven drug discovery workflows. These representations translate the structural and physicochemical characteristics of molecules into fixed-length numerical vectors—either binary bit vectors or integer count vectors—that can be readily consumed by classical and modern machine learning algorithms alike. As the earliest and most established class of molecular representations, they provide an important baseline against which the more expressive string-based and graph-based representations discussed in subsequent subsections can be evaluated.

### Classical Fingerprint Methods

Among the most historically significant fingerprint approaches are the Daylight fingerprints, which encode molecular substructure paths of varying lengths as hashed bit strings, and the MACCS keys, which represent a fixed set of 166 predefined structural features corresponding to specific molecular fragments and substructural patterns. These fragment-based representations provided early cheminformatics tools with a computationally tractable means of encoding molecular structure for similarity search and virtual screening applications [19].

The Morgan algorithm, upon which the widely used Extended Connectivity Fingerprints (ECFP) are based, introduced a circular fingerprinting strategy that iteratively propagates atomic environment information outward from each atom up to a specified radius. ECFPs, particularly ECFP4 and ECFP6, encode the local chemical neighborhood of every atom by hashing atomic features—including atomic number, charge, degree, and hydrogen count—along with their circular neighborhoods, producing fixed-length binary or count vectors. This design allows ECFPs to capture rich local structural motifs without requiring explicit substructure enumeration, making them exceptionally effective for ligand-based virtual screening and quantitative structure-activity relationship (QSAR) modeling [19].

### Physicochemical Descriptors

Beyond topological fingerprints, physicochemical descriptors provide an alternative class of molecular representations that encode global molecular properties such as molecular weight, lipophilicity (logP), polar surface area (PSA), hydrogen bond donor and acceptor counts, rotatable bond counts, and ionization constants. Tools such as RDKit, MOE, and Dragon compute hundreds to thousands of such descriptors, capturing diverse chemical and physical characteristics relevant to ADMET profiling, drug-likeness assessment, and lead optimization [1; 19]. Lipinski's Rule of Five and its derivatives, for instance, are formulated directly in terms of physicochemical descriptors, underscoring their central role in early drug-likeness filtering.

### k-mer and Fragment-Based Fingerprints

Fragment-based fingerprints extend classical approaches by decomposing molecules into chemically meaningful substructures such as rings, functional groups, pharmacophoric features, and scaffold elements. k-mer fingerprints, analogously to those used in genomic sequence analysis, encode the occurrence frequencies of molecular substructure fragments of varying sizes, providing richer structural resolution than single-bit hashing approaches. These fragment-level encodings have proven particularly valuable in focused library generation and scaffold hopping, where preserving core structural features while modifying peripheral substituents is critical. Notably, such fragment-based perspectives anticipate the motif-level and hierarchical graph constructions discussed in Section 2.3, where chemically meaningful substructures are explicitly encoded as higher-level nodes in molecular graphs.

### Applications in Similarity Search and Virtual Screening

Molecular fingerprints have been central to ligand-based virtual screening pipelines, where the Tanimoto coefficient computed between ECFP fingerprints enables rapid similarity-based retrieval of structurally related compounds from large compound libraries. Their fixed-length vector nature makes them highly scalable for high-throughput screening campaigns against libraries containing millions to billions of compounds [4; 50]. The computational efficiency of fingerprint-based similarity search, combined with their broad applicability across diverse target classes, has cemented their role as standard tools in early-stage hit identification.

### Complementary Use Alongside Learned Representations

Despite the emergence of deep learning-based molecular representations—including the string-based chemical language models and graph neural networks discussed in the following subsections—molecular fingerprints and physicochemical descriptors remain valuable as complementary input features. Multi-modal and multi-task learning frameworks frequently incorporate Morgan fingerprints and descriptor vectors alongside learned graph or sequence embeddings to capture orthogonal structural information and improve predictive robustness [85; 1]. This hybrid strategy leverages the interpretability and domain expertise encoded in classical fingerprints with the representational power of learned neural embeddings, yielding models with improved generalization across diverse molecular property prediction benchmarks.

In summary, molecular fingerprints and physicochemical descriptors provide a mature, interpretable, and computationally efficient foundation for molecular representation in drug discovery. While the string-based and graph-based representations introduced in the following subsections offer greater structural expressiveness and end-to-end trainability, the complementary strengths of classical fingerprints—including their computational efficiency, interpretability, and broad toolchain support—ensure their enduring relevance within modern, integrated machine learning pipelines for pharmaceutical research.

### 2.3 Graph-Based Molecular Representations

## 2.3 Graph-Based Molecular Representations

While molecular fingerprints and physicochemical descriptors encode chemical information into fixed-length vectors through predefined rules or hashing schemes, they inherently compress or discard explicit structural connectivity information. Molecules possess an inherently graph-structured nature: atoms can be naturally represented as nodes and chemical bonds as edges, giving rise to a 2D topological molecular graph. This formulation is a particularly expressive and chemically intuitive representation, as it preserves explicit information about atomic connectivity, bond types, and local chemical environments—information that is often lost or compressed in fingerprint-based representations. As we progress toward increasingly geometry-aware models, the molecular graph serves as the natural topological substrate upon which 3D spatial information can subsequently be layered.

### Atom and Bond Featurization

The expressiveness of a molecular graph depends critically on how atoms and bonds are featurized. Atom (node) features typically include atomic number, hybridization state, formal charge, degree (number of bonded neighbors), aromaticity, and whether the atom is part of a ring. Bond (edge) features usually encode bond type (single, double, triple, aromatic), stereochemistry (e.g., *E*/*Z* configuration), and conjugation status. These discrete chemical attributes are converted into continuous feature vectors that serve as inputs to graph neural network (GNN) layers. The richness of featurization directly impacts the model's ability to distinguish chemically distinct molecular environments. In property prediction tasks such as binding affinity, solubility, ADMET, and toxicity prediction, graph-based models leveraging such featurizations have been shown to capture subtle structure-activity relationships that would be obscured by coarser fingerprint encodings [22; 27].

### Message Passing Neural Networks

The dominant computational paradigm for learning on molecular graphs is the Message Passing Neural Network (MPNN) framework. In MPNNs, each atom iteratively aggregates feature information from its neighboring atoms and bonds over multiple rounds of message passing, producing node-level representations that encode the local chemical neighborhood at increasing radii with each successive layer. After a fixed number of message passing steps, a global readout function—such as sum pooling, mean pooling, or a learned attention-weighted aggregation—collapses all atom-level representations into a single molecular-level embedding, which is then passed to a prediction head for downstream tasks. This architecture allows the model to learn hierarchical structural features without any manual feature engineering and is naturally invariant to the ordering of atoms in the molecular graph. Graph convolutional networks (GCNs), graph attention networks (GATs), and their variants all follow this general paradigm but differ in how they aggregate neighborhood information—using uniform averaging, degree-normalized convolution, or learned attention coefficients, respectively. Graph attention mechanisms have proven particularly valuable for drug-target interaction tasks, as they allow the model to selectively weight the contributions of different neighboring atoms [67; 86].

### Line Graph Representations for Bond-Angle Relationships

A limitation of standard atom-centered MPNNs is that bond-angle and torsion information—geometric features that are critical for accurately modeling molecular conformations and quantum-chemical properties—are not naturally captured when messages are passed only between adjacent atom nodes. Line graph representations address this by constructing an auxiliary graph where bonds become nodes and pairs of adjacent bonds (sharing an atom) become edges. This reformulation enables the model to pass messages between connected bonds, effectively encoding bond-angle relationships in the molecular topology. Such representations serve as an important intermediate step toward the fully geometry-aware models discussed in the following section, capturing angular structural information even in the absence of explicit 3D coordinates.

### Motif-Level and Hierarchical Graph Constructions

Beyond atom-level graphs, there is growing recognition that molecules exhibit hierarchical chemical structure: functional groups, ring systems, pharmacophore motifs, and scaffolds are recurring building blocks that carry biological meaning. Hierarchical and motif-level graph representations augment the standard atomic graph with additional levels of abstraction. In these constructions, clusters of atoms forming chemically meaningful substructures (e.g., rings, functional groups) are identified—often using domain knowledge from cheminformatics—and represented as higher-level nodes in the graph. Edges between the atom-level and motif-level nodes then allow the model to propagate information across scales, enabling it to learn representations that are sensitive to both local atomic environments and global scaffold-level features. This hierarchical perspective is closely related to fragment-based drug design paradigms and provides a natural interface between learned representations and interpretable medicinal chemistry concepts, complementing the fragment-based fingerprint approaches introduced in the previous section [82].

### Heterogeneous Molecular Graphs

Standard molecular graphs treat all atoms and bonds homogeneously, but real molecules contain chemically distinct atom types and bond types that may benefit from type-specific message passing. Heterogeneous molecular graphs explicitly encode multiple node and edge types, employing type-specific transformation matrices during message passing. This allows the model to learn specialized representations for different chemical elements and bonding environments, improving performance on datasets with structurally diverse compound classes. Heterogeneous graph constructions are also naturally extended to protein-ligand complex graphs, where both protein residues and ligand atoms are represented as nodes of different types, and diverse interaction types (covalent bonds, hydrogen bonds, hydrophobic contacts, van der Waals interactions) are represented as different edge types—a formulation that has proven valuable for binding affinity prediction [24; 87].

### Advantages and Applications

The advantages of graph-based molecular representations over their fingerprint and string-based counterparts are substantial. Unlike fixed-length fingerprints, GNN-learned representations are differentiable and end-to-end trainable, allowing feature learning to be optimized directly for the prediction objective. Unlike SMILES strings, molecular graphs are invariant to atom ordering and do not suffer from the syntactic fragility of string representations. They naturally handle molecules of varying sizes without padding or truncation. These properties make GNNs the backbone of many state-of-the-art molecular property prediction systems across ADMET, toxicity, solubility, and binding affinity tasks [22; 27; 45]. Moreover, molecular graphs serve as the foundation for generative models—including graph variational autoencoders and graph-based reinforcement learning frameworks—that generate novel molecules directly in graph space, ensuring chemical validity by construction. Crucially, the 2D molecular graph also provides the topological scaffold upon which 3D coordinate information can be naturally incorporated, making it the conceptual bridge to the three-dimensional and geometry-aware representations explored in the following section. The graph formulation thus constitutes a foundational representation that bridges property prediction, generative modeling, and interaction modeling throughout the drug discovery pipeline.

### 2.4 Three-Dimensional and Geometry-Aware Molecular Representations

## 2.4 Three-Dimensional and Geometry-Aware Molecular Representations

While 2D molecular graphs and string-based representations excel at capturing the topology and connectivity of molecules, they inherently abstract away the three-dimensional geometric information that governs many critical physical and chemical properties. As introduced in Section 2.3, the 2D molecular graph provides the topological scaffold upon which 3D spatial information can be naturally layered; this section develops that geometric extension. The spatial arrangement of atoms—including bond lengths, bond angles, dihedral angles, and torsion angles—profoundly influences molecular reactivity, binding affinity, and pharmacological activity. Three-dimensional and geometry-aware molecular representations aim to explicitly encode this spatial information, enabling more accurate and physically meaningful machine learning models for drug discovery. These representations also serve as natural building blocks for the multi-modal and hybrid approaches discussed in the following section, where 3D geometric encodings are combined with 2D topological graphs and other modalities to achieve greater representational richness.

**Atomic Coordinate-Based and Point Cloud Representations.** The most direct approach to 3D molecular representation involves specifying the Cartesian coordinates of each atom, yielding a point cloud in three-dimensional space. Each atom is assigned a position vector along with associated features such as atomic number, partial charge, and hybridization state. This representation naturally captures the full spatial geometry of a molecule, including interatomic distances and angular relationships. However, raw coordinate representations are not invariant to global translations and rotations of the molecular frame, which are physically irrelevant transformations. Addressing this limitation has driven significant methodological innovation, as models must either learn invariance from data or incorporate explicit geometric constraints through architectural design [41].

**Coulomb Matrices and Distance-Based Representations.** An early and influential approach to encoding 3D geometry is the Coulomb matrix, which encodes pairwise interatomic interactions inspired by classical electrostatics. The off-diagonal elements represent the repulsive interaction between atom pairs as a function of their nuclear charges and interatomic distances, while diagonal elements encode atomic energies. The Coulomb matrix is invariant to global rotations and translations by construction and has been widely used as a baseline for quantum-chemical property prediction. Extensions include the bag-of-bonds representation and sorted eigenspectrum encodings that further improve invariance properties.
