Distance matrices, which directly tabulate all pairwise interatomic distances, provide complementary geometric information and are similarly translation- and rotation-invariant [22].

Beyond pairwise distances, angle matrices and higher-order geometric descriptors encode three-body and four-body interactions, capturing bond angles, dihedral angles, and torsion profiles. These descriptors are particularly important for modeling conformational flexibility and the energy landscapes of molecular systems. The inclusion of torsion angle information enables models to distinguish between stereoisomers and conformational states that share identical 2D topological representations—as discussed in Section 2.3 in the context of line graph representations for bond-angle relationships—but differ substantially in their biological activity.

**Conformer Ensembles.** A single 3D conformation of a molecule may not adequately represent its behavior in solution or within a protein binding site, where thermal fluctuations drive conformational sampling. Conformer ensembles—collections of low-energy 3D structures generated through force-field minimization or molecular dynamics simulation—provide a richer representation of the conformational space accessible to a molecule. Machine learning models that operate over conformer ensembles can aggregate representations across multiple conformations using pooling or attention mechanisms, yielding a more robust and physically meaningful molecular descriptor. This approach has proven particularly valuable for predicting properties that are sensitive to conformational dynamics, such as membrane permeability and binding affinity [41; 22].

**Molecular Surface Representations.** Molecular surfaces, including the van der Waals surface, solvent-accessible surface, and molecular electrostatic potential surface, encode the chemical and geometric features that govern intermolecular interactions, solubility, and receptor complementarity. Representing molecules as surfaces defined on Riemannian manifolds enables the application of differential geometry and intrinsic surface-based learning methods that respect the curved geometry of molecular boundaries. Surface-based representations have been applied to binding site characterization, protein-ligand interaction modeling, and the identification of druggable pockets [41].

**Equivariant Neural Network Architectures.** A central challenge in learning from 3D molecular representations is designing models whose outputs transform appropriately under rotations, reflections, and translations—collectively described by the Euclidean group E(3) or its subgroup SE(3). A scalar molecular property such as energy must be invariant to these transformations, while vector or tensor outputs such as forces or dipole moments must transform equivariantly. Equivariant neural networks achieve this by constructing message-passing operations that explicitly track the transformation behavior of all intermediate representations using spherical harmonics, irreducible representations of the rotation group, or geometric tensor product operations. Prominent architectures include SchNet, DimeNet, SphereNet, PaiNN, SEGNN, NequIP, and MACE, which progressively incorporate higher-order geometric information to achieve improved accuracy on quantum-chemical benchmarks and molecular property prediction tasks [41; 19].

These equivariant architectures offer several advantages over invariant baselines: by explicitly encoding directional information while respecting physical symmetries, they achieve superior data efficiency and generalization, particularly when training data are scarce. The incorporation of bond angle and torsion angle information within the message-passing framework—extending the line graph and motif-level constructions of Section 2.3 into three-dimensional space—allows equivariant GNNs to model the subtle geometric effects that distinguish pharmacologically active conformations from inactive ones [27].

**Integration with Drug Discovery Workflows and Multi-Modal Extensions.** Three-dimensional and geometry-aware representations have become indispensable in structure-based drug discovery, where the accurate modeling of protein-ligand interactions requires knowledge of the precise spatial arrangement of interacting atoms. Applications include binding affinity prediction, docking pose evaluation, de novo molecular generation within protein binding pockets, and the assessment of steric clashes and geometric complementarity [41]. The combination of accurate 3D conformer generation—now accessible through deep learning-based conformer prediction tools—with equivariant neural network models has substantially advanced the accuracy and physical interpretability of machine learning models for drug discovery, bridging the gap between abstract molecular topology and the three-dimensional reality of molecular recognition. Crucially, these 3D geometric representations do not operate in isolation: as explored in the following section, their combination with 2D topological graphs, molecular images, and language-based encodings within multi-modal frameworks yields hybrid representations that leverage the complementary strengths of each modality, further extending the expressive power of geometry-aware models across the full breadth of drug discovery tasks.

### 2.5 Multi-Modal and Hybrid Molecular Representations

## 2.5 Multi-Modal and Hybrid Molecular Representations

The representations surveyed in previous subsections—SMILES strings, molecular graphs, and three-dimensional geometry-aware encodings—each capture distinct and complementary aspects of molecular information. No single representation, however, encompasses the full richness required for the diverse tasks encountered in drug discovery. SMILES strings excel at encoding molecular connectivity in a compact, human-readable format; molecular graphs preserve topological structure and enable local chemical environment reasoning; three-dimensional representations encode geometric and conformational information critical for understanding protein-ligand interactions; and molecular images provide visual patterns interpretable through computer vision techniques. Beyond these structure-centric modalities, cellular morphology profiles derived from high-content imaging assays encode phenotypic information that bridges molecular structure and biological function, while textual descriptions and biomedical literature provide semantic chemical knowledge that complements numerical representations. Multi-modal and hybrid molecular representations seek to combine these complementary information sources to improve predictive performance, robustness, and generalizability across drug discovery tasks.

Fusion strategies for combining multiple modalities broadly fall into three categories: early fusion, late fusion, and attention-based fusion. Early fusion concatenates feature vectors from different modalities into a unified representation before being passed to a downstream model, which allows the model to learn cross-modal correlations from the outset but may suffer from the curse of dimensionality and modality dominance. Late fusion, by contrast, trains separate encoders for each modality and combines their predictions or final-layer representations, preserving modality-specific inductive biases but potentially missing cross-modal interactions. Attention-based fusion employs transformer-style attention mechanisms to dynamically weight and integrate features from multiple modalities, enabling the model to focus on the most informative aspects of each representation for a given prediction task. This strategy has proven particularly effective in molecular property prediction and drug-target interaction modeling, where different modalities contribute differentially depending on the property of interest.

Cross-modal alignment is a powerful paradigm for multi-modal molecular learning, seeking to align representations from different modalities in a shared embedding space so that molecules with similar chemical properties are close regardless of how they are represented. This has been explored through contrastive learning objectives applied across modality pairs such as molecular graphs and SMILES sequences, molecular images and graphs, and molecular structures with textual descriptions. Such alignment enables zero-shot transfer across modalities and improves the transferability of learned representations to downstream tasks with limited labeled data—a theme that connects naturally to the self-supervised and unsupervised pretraining strategies discussed in the following subsection.

The integration of molecular images with other representations has gained traction, particularly in the context of cell-based phenotypic screening. Cellular morphology profiles obtained from high-content imaging, when combined with molecular structure representations, can capture functional information about how compounds perturb biological systems in ways that structural features alone cannot. Similarly, hybrid approaches that combine 2D topological graphs with 3D geometric representations benefit from the complementary nature of these views: 2D graphs encode covalent bonding patterns efficiently, while 3D coordinates capture conformational preferences and spatial arrangements that influence binding affinity and selectivity. This synergy between graph topology and three-dimensional geometry directly extends the geometry-aware representations introduced in Section 2.4 into a multi-modal setting.

The integration of large language models (LLMs) has opened new avenues for multi-modal molecular representation by incorporating natural language descriptions of chemical and biological knowledge alongside structural encodings [27; 19]. LLM-based embeddings of molecular text, reaction descriptions, and biomedical literature can be fused with graph-based or sequence-based molecular encoders to create richer, knowledge-aware representations. This is particularly valuable for low-data regimes and novel target settings where leveraging prior chemical knowledge encoded in language can compensate for the lack of labeled experimental data [15].

Multi-modal approaches also address specific limitations of individual representations. SMILES-based representations, while powerful for sequence modeling, are sensitive to tokenization and may fail to generalize across chemically diverse scaffolds. Molecular graphs overcome some of these limitations but can miss long-range interactions captured by transformer attention over SMILES sequences. Three-dimensional representations provide geometric accuracy but require expensive conformer generation. By combining these modalities, hybrid models achieve greater robustness and generalization across the diverse chemical spaces encountered in drug discovery [27]. Importantly, the representational diversity of multi-modal approaches creates a natural foundation for self-supervised pretraining strategies—such as cross-modal contrastive learning and multimodal masked prediction—that can exploit large unlabeled molecular databases, as will be explored in Section 2.6.

Despite their promise, multi-modal molecular representation methods face challenges including the computational cost of processing multiple input modalities, the need for aligned multi-modal training data, and the difficulty of fairly attributing predictive performance improvements to specific modality combinations. Nevertheless, the growing availability of large-scale multi-modal molecular datasets and advances in efficient cross-modal fusion architectures suggest that multi-modal and hybrid representations will become increasingly central to machine learning-driven drug discovery workflows [19; 42].

### 2.6 Self-Supervised and Unsupervised Pretraining Strategies

## 2.6 Self-Supervised and Unsupervised Pretraining Strategies

The scarcity of labeled molecular data in drug discovery has motivated the development of self-supervised and unsupervised pretraining strategies that leverage large-scale unlabeled molecular databases to learn rich, transferable molecular representations. As noted in the concluding discussion of Section 2.5, the representational diversity inherent in multi-modal approaches creates a natural foundation for such pretraining methods—including cross-modal contrastive learning and multimodal masked prediction—that can exploit large unlabeled molecular databases without requiring expensive experimental annotations. These approaches aim to capture fundamental chemical and structural patterns, enabling pre-trained models to be efficiently fine-tuned for a wide range of downstream drug discovery tasks. The specific pretraining strategies reviewed here—spanning masked prediction, knowledge-guided objectives, generative autoencoders, and denoising-based learning—collectively form a complementary suite of techniques that set the stage for the contrastive learning paradigm explored in Section 2.7.

### Masked Atom, Bond, and Token Prediction

One of the most widely adopted self-supervised objectives is masked prediction, directly inspired by masked language modeling in natural language processing. For graph-based molecular representations, masked atom and bond prediction tasks require the model to reconstruct the identity or properties of randomly masked atoms and bonds from their molecular context. This node- and edge-level pretraining encourages the model to encode rich local chemical environments into learned representations. Analogously, for SMILES-based representations, masked token reconstruction objectives—where random tokens in the SMILES string are masked and the model is trained to predict them—allow sequence models to capture the grammar and semantics of chemical language. Models such as MolGNet, trained with graph-level and node-level self-supervised strategies on millions of unlabeled molecules, have demonstrated that such pretraining can produce expressive and interpretable molecular embeddings that substantially improve performance across diverse downstream tasks [73]. These masked prediction objectives operate at multiple granularities—atom, bond, and token levels—that directly parallel the node-level and graph-level predictive frameworks discussed in the following subsection, and anticipate the fragment-level contrastive objectives introduced in Section 2.7.

### Graph-Level and Node-Level Predictive Pretraining

Beyond masked prediction, several pretraining frameworks employ both graph-level and node-level self-supervised objectives. Node-level tasks encourage the model to capture fine-grained local structural information, while graph-level tasks ensure the model encodes global molecular properties. For instance, graph-level pretraining objectives may involve predicting molecular fingerprints or physicochemical descriptors from the learned graph representation, thereby injecting domain-relevant chemical knowledge into the pretrained model. Frameworks such as MPG combine both atom-level and molecular-level pretraining signals to produce representations that are both locally and globally informative [73]. Multi-task pretraining on graph-structured molecules has also shown promise, where simultaneously predicting multiple molecular properties during pretraining yields representations that capture complementary aspects of molecular structure and function. This multi-granularity perspective—operating at both the local atomic level and the global molecular level—provides a conceptual bridge to the fragment-level and attention-guided contrastive objectives discussed in Section 2.7, which similarly exploit structural hierarchy to enrich learned representations.

### Knowledge-Guided Pretraining

A particularly powerful direction in molecular pretraining is the incorporation of domain-specific chemical knowledge to guide the self-supervised learning objectives. Rather than relying solely on the molecular graph topology, knowledge-guided pretraining strategies exploit auxiliary information such as chemical reaction rules, molecular descriptors, SMILES-derived semantic information, and pharmacological properties to enrich the pretraining signal. The Knowledge-guided Pre-training of Graph Transformer (KPGT) framework addresses two critical limitations of prior self-supervised methods—ill-defined pretraining tasks and limited model capacity—by introducing a high-capacity Line Graph Transformer (LiGhT) that encodes chemical bond information alongside a knowledge-guided pretraining strategy that leverages molecular fingerprints and physicochemical descriptors as auxiliary supervision signals [74]. This approach enables the pretrained model to capture both structural and semantic molecular information from large collections of unlabeled molecules, yielding superior performance on downstream property prediction benchmarks. Notably, the chemical knowledge injected through such pretraining objectives—including molecular fingerprints such as ECFP—connects directly to the cheminformatics-based similarity measures exploited in Section 2.7 for faulty negative mitigation in contrastive learning.

### Generative Autoencoder Pretraining

Generative autoencoders, including variational autoencoders (VAEs) trained on SMILES strings or molecular graphs, represent another class of unsupervised pretraining approaches that learn continuous latent representations of molecules. By training the encoder-decoder architecture to reconstruct valid molecular structures, the model implicitly encodes the distribution of drug-like chemical space into a compact latent manifold. These latent spaces can subsequently be exploited for molecular property prediction, optimization, and generation tasks. Extensions to 3D molecular structures have been explored through deep generative models that jointly encode drug-like molecules and their conformations in a shared latent space, enabling interpolation and sampling of novel 3D structures [30]. Such generative pretraining with 3D conformer representations allows the model to capture geometric and conformational features that are critical for structure-based drug discovery tasks, extending the geometry-aware representations surveyed in Section 2.4 into the self-supervised pretraining regime.

### Denoising-Based Pretraining on 3D Conformers

Denoising-based pretraining has emerged as a powerful self-supervised strategy for learning from 3D molecular conformations. In this paradigm, random noise is added to the 3D atomic coordinates of molecular conformers, and the model is trained to predict and remove this noise—effectively learning the underlying distribution of molecular geometries. This denoising objective encourages the model to develop a strong understanding of valid molecular geometries, bond lengths, bond angles, and torsion angles. Denoising pretraining is closely related to score-based generative modeling and diffusion model pretraining, where the model learns to reverse a gradual noising process applied to molecular 3D structures. Such approaches have shown significant promise in learning geometry-aware molecular representations that improve downstream binding affinity prediction and structure-based drug design tasks. The denoising paradigm also bears a conceptual relationship to the augmentation-based contrastive frameworks discussed in Section 2.7, where structured perturbations of molecular graphs—such as atom masking and bond deletion—play an analogous role in generating informative self-supervised training signals.

### Impact and Broader Context

The self-supervised and unsupervised pretraining strategies reviewed here collectively address the fundamental challenge of labeled data scarcity in drug discovery by unlocking the informational content of large-scale unlabeled molecular databases. Pretraining on tens of millions of molecules—as demonstrated in MPG with 11 million unlabeled molecules—has been shown to produce representations that generalize broadly across molecular property prediction, drug-drug interaction, and drug-target interaction benchmarks [73]. Furthermore, multimodal pretraining frameworks that combine SMILES-based and graph-based self-supervised objectives have demonstrated that capturing complementary views of molecular information leads to more robust and accurate representations for downstream drug discovery tasks [75]. Taken together, these pretraining strategies—operating across sequence, graph, and 3D geometric modalities—produce strong molecular representations that serve as natural starting points for the contrastive learning paradigm explored in Section 2.7, where similar and dissimilar molecular pairs are explicitly contrasted to further sharpen the chemical structure of the learned latent space. As the field continues to scale pretraining datasets and develop increasingly sophisticated self-supervised objectives, these strategies are poised to become foundational components of modern AI-driven drug discovery workflows.

### 2.7 Contrastive Learning for Molecular Representations

## 2.7 Contrastive Learning for Molecular Representations

Building upon the self-supervised and unsupervised pretraining strategies discussed in the previous section, contrastive learning has emerged as a complementary and increasingly powerful paradigm for molecular representation learning. While masked prediction and denoising objectives train models to reconstruct or denoise molecular inputs, contrastive learning takes a different approach: it enables models to capture chemical similarity and functional group information by learning representations such that similar molecular views are pulled together in embedding space while dissimilar molecules are pushed apart, thereby encoding chemically meaningful structure into the latent space without requiring explicit labeled data. These contrastively pretrained representations naturally complement the supervised and multi-task pretraining strategies explored in the following section, as they provide strong initializations that can be further refined with task-specific labeled supervision.

### Molecular Graph Augmentation Strategies

A fundamental component of contrastive frameworks for molecular data is the design of appropriate augmentation strategies that generate semantically consistent views of the same molecule. Common augmentations applied to molecular graphs include atom masking, where a subset of atomic features are randomly hidden or replaced; bond deletion, where edges representing chemical bonds are removed from the molecular graph; and subgraph removal, where entire molecular fragments or motifs are excised to create partial views. These augmentations must be designed carefully to preserve the chemical identity of the molecule while introducing sufficient variation to prevent trivial solutions. Unlike natural image augmentations such as random cropping or color jitter, molecular augmentations must respect the discrete and combinatorial nature of chemical structures, ensuring that augmented views remain chemically valid and informative.

### Cross-Modal Contrastive Learning

A particularly powerful extension of contrastive learning for molecular data involves aligning representations across different molecular modalities. Cross-modal contrastive frameworks leverage the fact that different representations of the same molecule—such as its molecular graph, its 2D depiction as an image, its SMILES string, or its cellular morphology profile from high-content imaging experiments—capture complementary aspects of molecular identity. By contrasting graph-image pairs, for instance, models can learn representations that simultaneously encode both topological connectivity and visual structural features. Similarly, contrastive learning between molecular graph representations and cellular morphology profiles enables models to bridge the gap between chemical structure and phenotypic response, which is particularly valuable for activity-based molecular design. Such cross-modal alignment approaches have been shown to enrich molecular embeddings with information that is difficult to obtain from any single modality alone. This cross-modal perspective anticipates the multi-modal and multi-task pretraining strategies discussed in the following section, where combining diverse modalities and supervision signals yields further representational gains.

### Multilingual Molecular Contrastive Pretraining

Another direction in contrastive molecular pretraining involves treating different textual representations of molecules—such as SMILES, SELFIES, and IUPAC names—as parallel "languages" that describe the same underlying chemical entity. Multilingual contrastive pretraining frameworks train models to align representations derived from these different string-based encodings of the same molecule, effectively teaching the model that these disparate textual forms encode equivalent chemical information. This approach draws inspiration from multilingual natural language processing, where contrastive objectives have been used to align representations across languages. In the molecular domain, such multilingual pretraining can improve robustness to the choice of molecular representation and enable better generalization to novel chemical structures. This strategy is closely related to the chemical language model pretraining approaches discussed in the following section, where SMILES and SELFIES representations are exploited under supervised objectives.

### Fragment-Level and Attention-Guided Contrastive Objectives

Beyond molecule-level contrastive learning, fragment-level contrastive objectives provide a finer-grained supervision signal by operating at the level of molecular substructures and functional groups. Fragment-level contrastive learning encourages the model to learn representations that capture the chemical similarity of shared substructures across different molecules. For example, two molecules sharing a common pharmacophoric fragment should have similar fragment-level representations even if their global molecular structures differ substantially. This fragment-level perspective complements the node-level and graph-level pretraining objectives described in the previous section, providing yet another granularity at which self-supervised signals can be applied. Attention-guided contrastive learning further refines this by using attention weights computed over molecular graphs to identify and emphasize the most chemically relevant atoms and bonds when computing contrastive objectives, effectively focusing the pretraining signal on the most informative structural regions.

### Faulty Negative Mitigation Using Cheminformatics Similarity

A well-known challenge in contrastive learning for molecular data is the problem of false or "faulty" negatives—pairs of molecules that are treated as negatives in the contrastive objective but are in fact chemically similar or even biologically equivalent. In standard contrastive frameworks applied to natural images or text, random sampling of negatives is typically safe because the probability of sampling a semantically identical example is low. However, in molecular datasets, chemical space is highly structured, and many pairs of molecules share significant structural and pharmacological similarity. Training with such false negatives can degrade representation quality by pushing apart embeddings of chemically similar molecules. To address this, cheminformatics-based similarity measures—such as Tanimoto similarity computed over molecular fingerprints like ECFP [22]—can be used to identify and down-weight or exclude potential false negatives from the contrastive loss, resulting in more chemically consistent and reliable learned representations.

### Chemical Similarity and Functional Group Capture

The ultimate goal of contrastive pretraining for molecular representations is to produce embeddings in which geometric proximity in the learned latent space corresponds to chemical and biological similarity. Well-designed contrastive frameworks achieve this by ensuring that the augmentation strategies and contrastive objectives collectively capture the structural, electronic, and pharmacological features that govern molecular behavior. Functional groups, which are the primary determinants of chemical reactivity and biological activity, should be represented consistently across different molecular contexts. By combining diverse augmentation strategies, cross-modal alignment, fragment-level objectives, and robust negative sampling, contrastive pretraining enables molecular encoders to learn representations that are both chemically informative and transferable to a wide range of downstream drug discovery tasks, including virtual screening, property prediction, and lead optimization [42; 27]. These contrastively pretrained representations serve as strong foundations that can be further enhanced through the supervised and multi-task pretraining strategies explored in the following section, where explicit label information is leveraged to further specialize and refine molecular embeddings for targeted drug discovery applications.

### 2.8 Multi-Task and Supervised Pretraining Strategies

## 2.8 Multi-Task and Supervised Pretraining Strategies

Building upon the contrastive pretraining strategies discussed in the previous section—which leverage unlabeled molecular data to learn chemically meaningful representations—supervised and multi-task pretraining paradigms offer a complementary and powerful direction by explicitly incorporating rich label information into the pretraining process. Rather than relying solely on unlabeled data or self-supervised objectives, these methods leverage large-scale curated datasets with explicit labels—spanning quantum-chemical properties, biological assay outcomes, and physicochemical measurements—to pretrain foundation models that can subsequently be fine-tuned for diverse downstream drug discovery tasks. The representations learned through these supervised strategies provide strong initializations that naturally complement and extend the contrastively pretrained embeddings discussed in Section 2.7, and in turn lay the groundwork for the cross-modal distillation and 2D–3D knowledge transfer approaches explored in the following section.

**Pretraining on Quantum-Chemical Property Datasets.** One prominent direction involves pretraining molecular encoders on large quantum-chemical datasets such as QM9, which provides ground-truth labels for a rich set of molecular energies, electronic properties, dipole moments, and thermodynamic quantities computed at the density functional theory (DFT) level. Training on such datasets encourages the model to internalize physically meaningful structure-property relationships, including the effects of bond lengths, angles, and electronic distributions, thereby grounding learned representations in fundamental chemical physics. These representations are subsequently transferable to biological property prediction tasks, where labeled data is far more scarce. Knowledge distillation approaches have also leveraged quantum-chemical datasets to train efficient inference models, as illustrated by methods that transfer structural knowledge from graph Transformers to lightweight SMILES-based models [88], enabling fast high-throughput screening without sacrificing the rich chemical knowledge embedded during pretraining on quantum-chemical corpora. This distillation perspective directly anticipates the 2D–3D knowledge transfer strategies discussed in Section 2.9.

**Massive Multi-Task Biological Pretraining.** A complementary strategy pretrains molecular encoders simultaneously across hundreds or thousands of biological assay tasks, spanning target binding, cellular toxicity, metabolic stability, and pharmacokinetic endpoints. By training a single model to predict multiple heterogeneous property labels, multi-task pretraining encourages the learning of shared latent representations that generalize across different biological contexts. This approach is particularly valuable given that individual biological assay datasets are typically small and noisy; aggregating supervision signal across many tasks alleviates data sparsity and reduces overfitting. Multi-task learning strategies for molecular property prediction have demonstrated consistent improvements over single-task baselines across benchmarks such as MoleculeNet, the ADMET Benchmark Group, and the Therapeutic Data Commons [75]. The multi-task paradigm naturally complements multi-modal molecular representations, as diverse supervision signals benefit from diverse input modalities capturing complementary structural and functional information.

**Co-Supervised Pretraining of Pocket and Ligand Representations.** In the context of structure-based drug discovery, co-supervised pretraining strategies have been developed to simultaneously learn representations of protein binding pockets and small molecule ligands. Rather than pretraining ligand encoders in isolation, these approaches jointly optimize pocket and ligand encoders using binding interaction labels, thereby aligning the representation spaces of proteins and molecules in a shared embedding. This co-supervised paradigm improves binding affinity and drug-target interaction prediction by ensuring that the learned embeddings reflect the mutual complementarity of ligand and pocket features, such as shape complementarity, hydrophobicity, and hydrogen bonding capacity. The resulting pretrained models provide strong initialization for downstream docking score prediction and virtual screening tasks, and the shared pocket-ligand embedding spaces they produce are closely related to the cross-modal alignment objectives pursued in the 2D–3D knowledge transfer frameworks discussed in Section 2.9.

**Design of Large-Scale Multi-Task Datasets Combining Quantum and Biological Labels.** A particularly impactful development has been the construction of large-scale multi-task pretraining datasets that combine quantum-chemical and biological property labels for the same set of molecules. By unifying diverse label types—ranging from DFT-computed energies and HOMO-LUMO gaps to experimental binding affinities and toxicity measurements—these datasets enable foundation models to learn representations that are simultaneously grounded in physical chemistry and biologically relevant. Training on such heterogeneous multi-task datasets encourages the model to disentangle and integrate complementary aspects of molecular structure: electronic properties captured by quantum labels and pharmacological properties captured by biological labels. This joint supervision produces more robust and universally transferable representations compared to pretraining on either label type alone, and the resulting models serve as natural candidates for the cross-modal distillation pipelines explored in the following section.

**Supervised Pretraining with Chemical Language Models.** Supervised pretraining strategies have also been explored in the context of chemical language models based on SMILES and SELFIES representations—building directly on the multilingual molecular contrastive pretraining strategies discussed in Section 2.7, which treated different string-based encodings as parallel views of the same molecule. Rather than purely unsupervised masked token prediction objectives, some approaches augment language model pretraining with supervised property prediction tasks. For instance, SMILES Transformer demonstrates that pretraining sequence models on large molecular corpora followed by supervised fine-tuning yields strong generalization in low-data drug discovery settings [89]. Similarly, models such as SELFormer incorporate supervised fine-tuning on diverse molecular property prediction tasks after pretraining on large SELFIES corpora, demonstrating superior performance across solubility, toxicity, and activity prediction benchmarks [90]. MolFormer demonstrates that large-scale unsupervised pretraining on over one billion SMILES sequences, followed by supervised fine-tuning, yields representations competitive with or superior to graph neural network baselines across ten benchmark datasets [91].

**Integration with Multi-Modal Pretraining.** Multi-task supervised pretraining naturally complements multi-modal molecular representation frameworks, extending the cross-modal contrastive alignment strategies introduced in Section 2.7 by adding explicit supervised label information on top of the multi-modal input structure. By combining supervision from quantum-chemical, biological, and structural labels with multi-modal input representations spanning molecular graphs, SMILES strings, and 3D conformers, these strategies yield foundation models with broad chemical coverage and strong generalization. Integrated approaches combining graph and SMILES representations within multi-task frameworks have shown that exploiting complementary modality information under joint supervision substantially outperforms single-modality, single-task baselines on downstream drug property prediction benchmarks [92; 93].

**Summary.** Multi-task and supervised pretraining strategies represent an important complement to the self-supervised and contrastive approaches surveyed in preceding sections, bridging unlabeled pretraining objectives with the rich curated label information available in quantum-chemical, biological, and structural datasets. By leveraging this diverse supervision spanning quantum-chemical properties, large-scale biological assays, and protein-ligand interaction data, these paradigms enable the development of foundation models with transferable representations suitable for the full breadth of drug discovery tasks. The representations and knowledge encoded through these supervised pretraining strategies further motivate the cross-modal distillation and 2D–3D knowledge transfer methods examined in Section 2.9, where the goal of efficiently deploying geometry-aware molecular knowledge at scale provides a natural continuation of the themes developed here.

### 2.9 Cross-Modal Distillation and 2D–3D Knowledge Transfer

## 2.9 Cross-Modal Distillation and 2D–3D Knowledge Transfer

Cross-modal distillation and 2D–3D knowledge transfer represent a critical frontier in molecular representation learning, addressing a fundamental tension in computational drug discovery: three-dimensional molecular geometry encodes rich physical and chemical information essential for accurate property prediction, yet obtaining or computing reliable 3D conformers at scale remains computationally expensive and practically challenging. Building on the multi-task and supervised pretraining strategies discussed in the previous subsection, these approaches seek to distill the knowledge embedded in 3D geometric representations into more computationally tractable 2D or string-based models, enabling efficient downstream inference without sacrificing the predictive quality conferred by explicit geometric awareness.

**Knowledge Distillation from 3D Teacher to 2D Student Models.** A prominent class of methods achieves this by training 3D-aware models—such as equivariant graph neural networks or denoising diffusion models operating over atomic coordinates—and subsequently transferring their learned representations to 2D molecular graph encoders through knowledge distillation objectives. In this paradigm, the 3D model acts as a "teacher" network, generating soft targets or latent embeddings that supervise a "student" 2D encoder. Because the student model operates solely on 2D topological graphs during inference, it avoids the computational overhead of conformer generation while still benefiting from the geometric knowledge encoded by the teacher. This strategy has proven particularly valuable for large-scale virtual screening and high-throughput property prediction scenarios where millions of molecules must be processed rapidly, complementing the fast inference goals discussed in the context of supervised pretraining frameworks in Section 2.8.

**Dual-View and Multi-View Pretraining Frameworks.** Dual-view and multi-view pretraining frameworks offer a complementary perspective by explicitly exploiting the natural correspondence between 2D molecular graphs and their 3D conformations during pretraining. Rather than treating the two modalities independently, these frameworks encourage the model to develop representations that are consistent and mutually informative across both views. Contrastive or consistency-based objectives align the representations of the same molecule observed as a 2D graph and as a 3D conformer, ensuring that the 2D encoder captures implicit geometric information. This is conceptually related to the cross-modal contrastive learning strategies discussed in earlier subsections, but with a particular emphasis on transferring structural and conformational knowledge rather than simply aligning heterogeneous data modalities.

**Distillation from Graph Transformers to Sequence Models.** Knowledge distillation from graph Transformer architectures to lighter-weight SMILES-based sequence models represents another important direction within this landscape. Graph Transformers can naturally encode both local chemical environments and long-range molecular interactions through attention mechanisms operating on molecular graphs, and distilling this relational knowledge into SMILES-based models—which are more straightforwardly scalable through established language model infrastructure—enables flexible deployment across different computational settings. The resulting SMILES-based student models inherit a richer chemical understanding than would be achievable through direct training on sequence data alone, providing a natural bridge between the graph-based and sequence-based pretraining paradigms surveyed in preceding subsections.

**Addressing Data Scarcity and Enabling Practical Deployment.** Importantly, these cross-modal transfer strategies also address a practical bottleneck in molecular machine learning: the scarcity of labeled 3D structural data. While large-scale databases of 2D molecular structures and associated biological activity labels are widely available, experimentally determined or high-quality quantum-chemically computed 3D conformers are far more limited. By leveraging 3D information during pretraining or distillation—even if derived from approximate conformer generation tools—and then enabling inference from 2D representations alone, these methods effectively democratize access to geometry-aware molecular modeling. Frameworks leveraging such 2D–3D knowledge transfer have demonstrated strong performance improvements on molecular property prediction benchmarks, validating the principle that geometric priors learned in 3D space can be successfully internalized into efficient 2D encoders for practical drug discovery applications.

**Summary.** Cross-modal distillation and 2D–3D knowledge transfer constitute an important methodological bridge between the richness of geometric molecular representations and the computational efficiency required for real-world drug discovery workflows. Together with the multi-task and supervised pretraining strategies discussed in Section 2.8, these approaches form a comprehensive toolkit for developing transferable molecular foundation models. The performance and generalization capabilities of these models are ultimately assessed through the benchmarking and evaluation frameworks discussed in the following subsection, which provide the standardized protocols necessary to rigorously compare diverse representation learning strategies across the full breadth of drug discovery tasks.

### 2.10 Benchmarking and Evaluation of Molecular Representations

## 2.10 Benchmarking and Evaluation of Molecular Representations

The rapid proliferation of molecular representation learning methods and pretraining strategies surveyed throughout this chapter—from graph neural networks and geometric deep learning to cross-modal distillation and 2D–3D knowledge transfer—has created an urgent need for standardized benchmarking frameworks that enable rigorous, reproducible, and fair comparison across diverse approaches. A well-structured evaluation ecosystem is indispensable for tracking scientific progress, identifying genuine improvements over existing methods, and guiding future research directions in molecular machine learning. Without such frameworks, it becomes impossible to disentangle true architectural and representational advances from the confounding effects of implementation choices, data preprocessing decisions, and evaluation protocols.

**MoleculeNet and Core Benchmarking Platforms**

MoleculeNet has emerged as one of the most widely adopted benchmarking suites in the field, providing curated collections of molecular datasets spanning quantum mechanics, physical chemistry, biophysics, and physiology. Datasets within MoleculeNet include QM9 for quantum mechanical properties, ESOL for aqueous solubility, BACE and BBBP for biological activity and permeability, HIV for antiviral activity, and Tox21 and ToxCast for toxicity prediction. These datasets have become standard reference points against which new molecular representation methods—including the pretraining and distillation strategies discussed in preceding subsections—are routinely evaluated [94; 95; 96].

The QM9 dataset, comprising approximately 134,000 small organic molecules with up to nine heavy atoms (carbon, hydrogen, oxygen, nitrogen, and fluorine), provides 12 quantum chemical properties computed at the DFT level of theory, and has been extensively used to benchmark graph neural network architectures and geometric deep learning approaches for molecular property prediction [97; 98; 99; 95]. Many state-of-the-art methods, including heterogeneous graph neural networks, simplicial message passing frameworks, and dual-branched architectures, report results on QM9 as a primary benchmark [100; 101].

**Dataset Splitting Strategies**

A critical methodological consideration in benchmarking molecular representations is the choice of dataset splitting strategy. Random splits, while computationally convenient, tend to overestimate model performance by allowing structurally similar molecules to appear in both training and test sets. To assess genuine generalization to novel chemical scaffolds—a property of paramount importance for real-world drug discovery—scaffold-based splits, which partition molecules according to their Bemis-Murcko scaffolds such that molecules sharing the same core scaffold are assigned to the same partition, are widely recommended and have become the de facto standard for evaluating drug discovery-relevant predictive models [96; 102]. Time-based splits, which assign earlier compounds to training and later compounds to test sets in a manner that mimics prospective deployment, and cluster-based splits based on physicochemical property similarity, have also been explored as means of constructing more challenging and realistic evaluation scenarios.

The importance of splitting strategy is illustrated by numerous studies demonstrating that models achieving strong performance under random splits can exhibit significantly degraded performance under scaffold splits, underscoring that apparent advances in predictive accuracy can be largely attributable to data leakage rather than genuine representational improvements [103; 104]. This distinction is particularly relevant when comparing pretraining strategies of the kind discussed in Sections 2.8 and 2.9, since models pretrained on large unlabeled molecular databases may have incidental exposure to test scaffolds if splitting is not performed carefully across both pretraining and fine-tuning datasets.

**Evaluation Metrics**

The choice of evaluation metric depends fundamentally on the nature of the prediction task. For binary classification tasks—such as activity prediction, toxicity classification, and blood-brain barrier permeability—the area under the receiver operating characteristic curve (AUC-ROC) and the area under the precision-recall curve (AUPRC) are the most commonly reported metrics, with AUPRC being particularly informative for datasets exhibiting severe class imbalance. For regression tasks—including prediction of binding affinity, solubility, atomization energy, and HOMO-LUMO gaps—mean absolute error (MAE) and root mean squared error (RMSE) are standard metrics, with MAE often preferred for its robustness to outliers [99; 98; 105].

**Emerging Large-Scale Benchmarks and Challenges**

Beyond MoleculeNet and QM9, several newer and more demanding benchmarking frameworks have emerged to address limitations of earlier datasets and to better reflect the scale and diversity of chemical space that modern representation learning methods are designed to handle. The Open Graph Benchmark Large-Scale Challenge (OGB-LSC) introduced the PCQM4Mv2 dataset, comprising over 3.7 million small organic molecules with DFT-computed HOMO-LUMO gap values, posing a substantially more challenging benchmark for molecular graph learning at scale [106]. The Therapeutic Data Commons (TDC) provides a comprehensive collection of machine learning tasks spanning the entire drug discovery pipeline, including ADMET prediction, drug-target interaction, and clinical trial outcome prediction, with standardized evaluation protocols, leaderboards, and data splits designed to facilitate reproducible benchmarking.

The ADMET Benchmark Group within TDC specifically focuses on absorption, distribution, metabolism, excretion, and toxicity prediction tasks relevant to pharmaceutical development, providing standardized scaffold-split train/validation/test partitions and enabling direct comparison of diverse molecular representation approaches on clinically relevant endpoints. These emerging platforms reflect a broader movement toward more rigorous, standardized, and community-driven evaluation of molecular machine learning methods, and they provide the natural evaluation context within which the pretraining and knowledge transfer strategies discussed throughout this chapter are ultimately assessed.

**Challenges in Fair Comparison**

Despite the availability of these benchmarking resources, fairly comparing diverse molecular representation and pretraining approaches remains a significant methodological challenge. Variations in atom featurization schemes, bond feature sets, and hyperparameter tuning practices can substantially affect reported performance, making it difficult to isolate the contribution of architectural innovations from implementation details [107]. Studies have shown that the choice of atom representation alone can account for non-trivial performance differences across GNN architectures, potentially confounding conclusions drawn from benchmark comparisons [107].

Pretraining strategies introduce additional complications, since models pretrained on large-scale datasets such as ZINC or ChEMBL benefit from exposure to diverse chemical space that may not be equally available to all competing methods, creating uneven playing fields in downstream benchmark comparisons [96; 108; 104; 105]. Furthermore, the integration of 3D geometric information—which often yields substantial performance improvements on quantum chemistry tasks and which forms the basis of the cross-modal distillation strategies discussed in Section 2.9—is only feasible for molecules with available conformer data, creating asymmetries between 2D and 3D representation methods on benchmarks that include both small-molecule and larger drug-like compounds [109; 110; 103].

The field is increasingly recognizing that benchmark saturation—where multiple methods achieve performance approaching or exceeding human-defined baselines on established datasets—necessitates the development of new, more challenging evaluation scenarios that better reflect real-world drug discovery applications. These include out-of-distribution generalization benchmarks, low-data regimes that assess few-shot learning capabilities, and multi-task settings that require simultaneous optimization across chemically diverse property endpoints [111; 112]. Collectively, these ongoing efforts to standardize evaluation protocols, expand dataset diversity, and develop more realistic splitting strategies are essential for ensuring that the advances in molecular representation learning surveyed throughout this chapter translate into genuine improvements in practical drug discovery applications.

## 3 Molecular Property Prediction and Virtual Screening

### 3.1 Overview of Molecular Property Prediction in Drug Discovery

Molecular property prediction occupies a central position in the modern drug discovery pipeline, serving as a computational gateway through which vast chemical spaces are filtered to identify promising therapeutic candidates. The ability to accurately predict key physicochemical and biological properties—including binding affinity to target proteins, absorption, distribution, metabolism, excretion, and toxicity (ADMET) profiles, drug-likeness, aqueous solubility, and safety endpoints—directly determines the efficiency of early-stage drug discovery and the likelihood of clinical success [1].

The traditional drug discovery process is famously expensive and time-consuming, with billions of dollars and over a decade of effort typically required to bring a single new drug to market [36]. A major contributor to this inefficiency is the high attrition rate of candidate compounds, many of which fail due to unfavorable pharmacokinetic properties or unanticipated toxicity that could, in principle, have been identified earlier with more accurate predictive models. Molecular property prediction thus serves not merely as an academic exercise but as a practical strategy for reducing attrition, prioritizing synthesis efforts, and guiding lead optimization decisions.

Historically, the field of molecular property prediction was anchored in quantitative structure-activity relationship (QSAR) modeling, a paradigm dating back several decades that seeks to establish mathematical relationships between the structural features of molecules and their measured biological or physicochemical activities. Classical QSAR approaches relied heavily on hand-crafted molecular descriptors and fingerprints—such as Morgan/ECFP fingerprints, physicochemical descriptors, and fragment-based representations—combined with linear regression, support vector machines, or shallow neural networks [4]. While these methods provided interpretable and computationally tractable models, their reliance on fixed feature representations and linear assumptions limited their capacity to capture complex, nonlinear structure-property relationships in diverse chemical spaces.

The advent of modern machine learning, and in particular deep learning, has fundamentally transformed molecular property prediction. Graph neural networks (GNNs), which represent molecules as graphs of atoms and bonds and learn representations end-to-end from raw molecular structure, have supplanted fixed fingerprints as the dominant paradigm for many property prediction tasks [85]. As will be discussed in detail in the following subsection, GNNs operate directly on the native graph topology of molecules, enabling the adaptive learning of task-relevant structural features that fixed fingerprint representations are inherently unable to capture. Concurrently, transformer-based chemical language models pretrained on large molecular databases have demonstrated remarkable ability to transfer learned chemical knowledge to downstream property prediction tasks with limited labeled data. Multi-task learning frameworks that simultaneously predict multiple related properties have further improved data efficiency and generalization [45].

The integration of machine learning-based property prediction into virtual screening workflows has enabled the high-throughput evaluation of large compound libraries at a fraction of the cost of experimental assays [12; 13]. By combining predictive models for multiple properties—binding affinity, solubility, ADMET, and toxicity—in an integrated pipeline, researchers can rapidly triage billions of candidate molecules, focusing experimental resources on the most promising compounds. This shift from sequential, experimental-first workflows to computationally guided, prediction-first paradigms represents one of the most impactful transformations in contemporary pharmaceutical research [50].

In summary, molecular property prediction has evolved from rule-based and descriptor-driven QSAR approaches to sophisticated deep learning architectures capable of learning directly from molecular structure, enabling more accurate, scalable, and interpretable predictions that are increasingly essential to efficient and cost-effective drug discovery pipelines. The following subsection examines graph neural networks in depth, tracing their architectural development from graph convolutional networks and message-passing frameworks to attention-based and physics-informed variants, and surveying their impact across key property prediction benchmarks including ADMET profiling and virtual screening.

### 3.2 Graph Neural Networks for Molecular Property Prediction

### 3.2 Graph Neural Networks for Molecular Property Prediction

Graph neural networks (GNNs) have emerged as a powerful paradigm for molecular property prediction, offering a principled way to learn representations directly from the topological structure of molecules represented as atomic graphs. As introduced in the previous subsection, the limitations of classical QSAR methods and fixed molecular fingerprints motivated the development of architectures capable of end-to-end learning from raw molecular structure. Unlike fixed molecular fingerprints or descriptor-based representations, GNNs operate on the native graph structure of molecules, where atoms are represented as nodes and chemical bonds as edges, enabling the model to adaptively learn task-relevant structural features through end-to-end training [19].

The foundational architectures in this space include graph convolutional networks (GCNs), which generalize the convolution operation to graph-structured data by iteratively aggregating feature information from local neighborhoods. Building upon this, message-passing neural networks (MPNNs) provide a unifying framework in which nodes exchange "messages" with their neighbors over multiple rounds of propagation, progressively encoding richer structural context into node embeddings. This message-passing paradigm has demonstrated strong performance across a variety of molecular property benchmarks, including binding affinity estimation, aqueous solubility prediction, and toxicity classification [85].

Attention-based graph models represent a significant refinement over vanilla GCNs and MPNNs by introducing learnable attention coefficients that dynamically weight the contributions of different neighboring atoms during aggregation. Graph attention networks (GATs) and their variants allow the model to focus on chemically relevant atomic environments, improving both predictive performance and interpretability. Such attention mechanisms have been particularly valuable in drug-target interaction and ADMET prediction tasks, where specific functional groups or pharmacophoric features are responsible for biological activity [78]. Notably, the interpretability afforded by attention-based GNNs connects naturally to the transformer attention mechanisms discussed in the following subsection, where similar principles are leveraged in the context of chemical language models.

A key advantage of GNN-based approaches over fixed fingerprint representations is their ability to capture structural nuances that are difficult to encode in predefined bit vectors. Fixed fingerprints such as Morgan/ECFP fingerprints encode molecular substructures within a fixed-radius neighborhood, but their capacity to represent global topology, long-range atomic interactions, and context-dependent chemical environments is inherently limited. GNNs, by contrast, learn hierarchical representations that are sensitive to the broader molecular context, enabling them to distinguish chemically distinct molecules that may share similar fingerprint profiles.

Physics-informed GNN variants further enhance predictive accuracy by incorporating domain knowledge about molecular interactions directly into the network architecture. These models integrate physical constraints such as interatomic distances, bond angles, and electrostatic interactions as edge features or through geometry-aware message passing, improving predictions of properties that depend on three-dimensional molecular structure. Such physics-informed approaches have been applied to binding affinity prediction and quantum-chemical property estimation, achieving competitive results on established benchmark datasets [41].

The application of GNNs to ADMET property prediction has been particularly impactful, as these properties—covering absorption, distribution, metabolism, excretion, and toxicity—are critical determinants of drug candidate success or failure in clinical development [1]. Multi-task GNN frameworks that simultaneously predict multiple ADMET endpoints leverage shared molecular representations to improve generalization, especially in low-data regimes where individual assay datasets may contain only hundreds to thousands of labeled examples [19]. This multi-task learning strategy, which effectively addresses data scarcity through shared representation learning, anticipates the transfer learning and pretraining paradigms central to the transformer-based approaches examined in the following subsection.

GNNs have also been evaluated extensively on standard benchmarking platforms such as MoleculeNet and the Therapeutic Data Commons, where they have consistently outperformed traditional machine learning methods using fixed descriptors on tasks including HIV inhibition classification, blood-brain barrier penetration prediction, and hERG cardiotoxicity assessment [85]. However, challenges remain in ensuring robust generalization under scaffold-based data splits that more realistically simulate the distribution shift encountered when predicting properties of structurally novel scaffolds outside the training distribution [15]. As discussed in the following subsection, this challenge of generalization under limited labeled data is one that transformer-based chemical language models, through large-scale pretraining, are particularly well positioned to address.

Overall, GNNs represent a cornerstone of modern machine learning-based molecular property prediction, and their continued development—particularly through integration with attention mechanisms, physics-based priors, and pretraining on large-scale unlabeled molecular data—is driving significant advances in virtual screening and ADMET profiling workflows [78; 41]. The following subsection examines how transformer-based architectures and chemical language models complement and, in certain regimes, extend beyond the capabilities of GNNs, together forming the foundation of contemporary deep learning approaches to molecular property prediction.

### 3.3 Transformer-Based and Language Model Approaches for Property Prediction

### 3.3 Transformer-Based and Language Model Approaches for Property Prediction

Building upon the graph-based and attention-enhanced architectures discussed in the previous section, transformer models and large language models (LLMs) represent a complementary and increasingly dominant paradigm for molecular property prediction. The remarkable success of transformer architectures in natural language processing has catalyzed their adaptation for chemical applications, where molecular structures encoded as SMILES strings are treated analogously to sentences in a natural language corpus. This paradigm shift has enabled the development of powerful chemical language models that leverage self-supervised pretraining on vast unlabeled molecular datasets, learning rich contextual representations of molecular substructures that can subsequently be fine-tuned for diverse downstream property prediction tasks—including the very ADMET and binding affinity endpoints that GNN-based methods target.

The foundational idea behind transformer-based molecular models is to apply BERT-style masked token reconstruction or autoregressive language modeling pretraining to SMILES sequences, learning embeddings that encode both local chemical environments and global molecular context. Unlike fixed molecular fingerprints, which rely on predefined structural patterns, or graph neural networks, which operate on molecular topology, transformer models capture long-range dependencies within the SMILES string, enabling them to recognize complex functional group relationships and stereochemical configurations that influence molecular properties. Models such as those combining sequential token representations with 2D topology graphs and 3D conformer information have demonstrated that integrating multiple pretraining strategies consistently outperforms single-modality approaches [113].

A central advantage of transformer-based approaches is their ability to benefit from large-scale pretraining on millions of unlabeled molecules drawn from databases such as ChEMBL, ZINC, and PubChem, after which fine-tuning on small, task-specific labeled datasets yields substantially improved generalization. This transfer learning paradigm is particularly valuable in drug discovery, where experimental labeled data for novel targets and assays is often scarce—a challenge directly analogous to the low-data regimes that motivate multi-task GNN frameworks. The pretraining phase instills chemical knowledge—including valence rules, ring systems, and pharmacophoric patterns—into the model parameters, reducing the amount of task-specific supervision needed to achieve competitive predictive performance.

Domain-relevant auxiliary tasks have been explored to enhance the quality of pretrained representations. These include predicting physicochemical properties such as molecular weight and logP as auxiliary objectives during pretraining, masked atom type prediction in hybrid SMILES-graph models, and reaction-based pretraining that exposes the model to chemical transformation knowledge. Such auxiliary objectives ensure that learned representations are not merely syntactic but encode chemically meaningful information relevant to biological activity and ADMET properties, effectively bridging the gap between language model pretraining and the domain-specific requirements of drug discovery.

Multi-modal and attention-based architectures represent an important extension of pure transformer approaches, and they connect naturally to the multi-modal fusion strategies that will be examined in detail in the following section. By integrating SMILES-based transformer encoders with molecular graph branches or fingerprint features, these hybrid models capture complementary chemical information from different representational perspectives [79]. The attention mechanism in such architectures further provides interpretability by highlighting which molecular tokens or substructures most strongly influence a given property prediction, offering medicinal chemists actionable structure-activity relationship insights—an advantage shared with the GAT-based approaches described earlier.

Empirical benchmarking has demonstrated that transformer-based models and chemical language models achieve competitive or superior performance relative to traditional fingerprint-based methods and classical machine learning approaches on many molecular property prediction benchmarks. On QSAR and ADMET benchmark suites, large-scale evaluations have shown that the optimal method depends on the specific dataset and property type, with deep learning approaches including language models and graph neural networks excelling on ADMET tasks, while classical methods sometimes remain competitive on smaller QSAR datasets [28]. This nuanced picture underscores that the quality and quantity of training data remain critical determinants of model performance alongside architectural choices—a theme consistent with the generalization challenges under scaffold-based splits highlighted in the context of GNNs.

Unified Auto-ML frameworks such as Uni-QSAR have demonstrated that combining 1D sequential transformer encoders, 2D graph encoders, and 3D conformer-based models within an automated parallel workflow, without manual fine-tuning or model selection, achieves state-of-the-art performance across the majority of tasks in the Therapeutic Data Commons benchmark [113]. This result highlights the practical value of transformer-based and multi-modal pretraining strategies for building robust, generalizable molecular property predictors, and it naturally motivates the more systematic treatment of multi-modal and multi-task learning frameworks that follows in the next section.

Looking forward, the continued scaling of chemical language models, the development of more sophisticated domain-relevant pretraining objectives, and the integration of biological sequence transformers with chemical structure encoders are expected to further advance the state of the art in molecular property prediction, bringing transformer-based models closer to becoming the default tool of choice for computational drug discovery workflows.

### 3.4 Multi-Modal and Multi-Task Learning for Property Prediction

### 3.4 Multi-Modal and Multi-Task Learning for Molecular Property Prediction

Building naturally upon the transformer-based and language model approaches discussed in the previous section—where multi-modal pretraining strategies were shown to consistently outperform single-modality architectures—multi-modal and multi-task learning represent a broader and more systematic paradigm for molecular property prediction. By integrating complementary information from diverse molecular representations and leveraging shared structure across related prediction tasks, these methods address fundamental limitations of single-representation and single-task approaches, achieving superior predictive performance, reduced variance, and more robust generalization across the drug discovery pipeline.

#### Multi-Modal Fusion Approaches

Different molecular representations capture distinct and complementary aspects of chemical structure and function. SMILES strings encode sequential connectivity information, molecular graphs capture topological relationships between atoms and bonds, 2D molecular images preserve spatial arrangement and visual patterns, and physicochemical fingerprints summarize key chemical properties in compact vector form. No single representation is universally optimal, motivating the development of multi-modal architectures that fuse information from multiple views of the same molecule—a design principle already hinted at in the Uni-QSAR framework discussed previously, which combines 1D, 2D, and 3D encoders in a unified workflow.

Early multi-modal approaches employed simple concatenation or ensemble strategies to combine predictions from models trained on different representations. However, more sophisticated attention-based fusion mechanisms have shown substantially greater effectiveness. For instance, attention-based architectures have been proposed that simultaneously process structural data and chemical descriptor features, leveraging the complementary nature of these two information sources to improve bioactivity prediction [79]. In such architectures, attention mechanisms serve the dual purpose of improving predictive accuracy and providing interpretability, allowing researchers to identify which structural features contribute most significantly to predicted biological activity—an advantage that connects directly to the structure-activity relationship insights offered by GAT-based and transformer-based approaches described in earlier sections.

The integration of molecular fingerprints alongside end-to-end learned representations represents another prominent multi-modal strategy. Pre-defined molecular descriptors, such as Morgan fingerprints and physicochemical descriptors, often capture domain knowledge that purely data-driven models may not learn efficiently from limited labeled data. Multi-view architectures trained to leverage both differentiable and predefined molecular descriptors have demonstrated the complementary behavior of different descriptor schemes on clinically relevant benchmarks [86]. By training such architectures adversarially, these methods can effectively integrate the rich prior knowledge encoded in classical cheminformatics descriptors with the flexibility of learned deep representations.

Beyond pairwise fusion, some frameworks integrate structured knowledge from knowledge bases and unstructured knowledge from biomedical literature alongside molecular structure representations. The KEDD framework exemplifies this multimodal philosophy, integrating heterogeneous inputs—molecular structures, protein sequences, structured knowledge graphs, and biomedical text—through multi-head sparse attention and modality masking mechanisms that enable robust prediction even under missing modality conditions [44]. This unified framework achieves significant improvements over state-of-the-art methods across a wide range of drug discovery tasks, demonstrating the practical value of integrating diverse knowledge modalities for property prediction.

#### Multi-Task Learning Strategies

Multi-task learning (MTL) leverages the observation that related molecular properties share underlying structural determinants, enabling a single model trained on multiple tasks simultaneously to learn richer, more transferable representations than models trained on individual tasks in isolation. In drug discovery, many critical properties—including ADMET parameters, binding affinities, and toxicity endpoints—are governed by overlapping structural features, making them natural candidates for joint modeling. This motivation is closely aligned with the multi-task GNN frameworks discussed earlier in this chapter, and extends those ideas into the context of richer, multi-modal input spaces.

The key advantage of MTL in the context of molecular property prediction lies in its ability to improve generalization on tasks with limited labeled data by exploiting shared information from related tasks. This is particularly valuable in drug discovery, where obtaining experimental measurements is expensive and time-consuming, often resulting in sparse and heterogeneous datasets [22]. A model trained jointly on multiple ADMET endpoints can share learned representations across tasks, effectively augmenting the available training signal for each individual endpoint and reducing the risk of overfitting to small datasets.

Physical formula-enhanced multi-task learning represents a particularly innovative direction that incorporates domain knowledge directly into the multi-task framework. The PEMAL method demonstrates how embedding physical formulas governing relationships between pharmacokinetic parameters—such as volume of distribution, clearance, and half-life—into a multi-task neural network can facilitate effective knowledge sharing and target alignment among parameters [45]. Crucially, PEMAL significantly reduces data demand compared to standard graph neural networks, enhances robustness to noisy measurements, and demonstrates how physical constraints can serve as implicit regularizers in data-scarce drug discovery settings.

MTL has also shown effectiveness in predicting specific property classes. For ADMET prediction, joint modeling of absorption, distribution, metabolism, excretion, and toxicity properties allows the model to capture cross-property correlations that single-task models miss. Similarly, toxicity prediction benefits substantially from multi-task formulations that pool information across diverse toxicological assays conducted on overlapping chemical libraries, improving the ability to generalize to novel scaffolds [27].

#### Combining Multi-Modal and Multi-Task Paradigms

The most powerful property prediction frameworks often combine multi-modal fusion with multi-task objectives, simultaneously leveraging diverse molecular representations and multiple prediction targets. This combination is particularly effective because different representations may be differentially informative for different property endpoints—for example, 3D geometric features may be more important for binding affinity prediction while topological fingerprints may dominate for metabolic stability—and multi-task learning can implicitly learn which representations to emphasize for each task.

Large-scale multi-task pretraining on diverse molecular property datasets provides a strong initialization for downstream fine-tuning on specific tasks. By pretraining on thousands of bioassay measurements spanning diverse target families and chemical series, foundation models can learn broadly transferable molecular representations that perform well across property prediction benchmarks without extensive task-specific fine-tuning [27]. The combination of pretraining diversity, multi-modal input fusion, and multi-task supervision represents the current state of the art in molecular property prediction for drug discovery.

#### Benefits and Limitations

Empirical evidence consistently demonstrates that multi-modal and multi-task approaches outperform their single-representation and single-task counterparts across diverse molecular property prediction benchmarks. The improvements are most pronounced in low-data regimes and for structurally novel compounds, precisely the settings most relevant to practical drug discovery—and directly relevant to the generalization challenges under scaffold-based splits highlighted throughout this chapter. Additionally, multi-modal models tend to produce more calibrated uncertainty estimates, as disagreement across modality-specific sub-models can serve as a signal of prediction uncertainty [15]. This natural connection between multi-modal disagreement and predictive confidence motivates the more systematic treatment of uncertainty quantification that follows in the next section.

However, these methods also introduce new challenges. Multi-modal frameworks require careful handling of missing modalities, particularly when some representations—such as experimental 3D conformers or cellular morphology profiles—are not available for all compounds in a dataset. Multi-task models are sensitive to the choice of task combinations and can suffer from negative transfer when tasks are poorly related or when their label distributions are highly heterogeneous. Addressing these limitations through principled architecture design, loss weighting strategies, and selective task combination remains an active area of research in the field [27]. These challenges further underscore the importance of reliable uncertainty quantification methods, which provide the principled confidence estimates needed to deploy such complex, multi-modal and multi-task models responsibly within production-grade drug discovery pipelines.

### 3.5 Uncertainty Quantification and Reliable Property Prediction
