Uncertainty quantification (UQ) has emerged as a critical component of reliable molecular property prediction, bridging the gap between the multi-modal and multi-task learning frameworks discussed previously and the few-shot generalization challenges that arise when labeled data is scarce. In drug discovery pipelines where erroneous predictions can lead to costly experimental failures, machine learning models for predicting binding affinity, ADMET profiles, and toxicity are often deployed on chemical spaces far removed from their training distributions, making calibrated confidence estimation essential for trustworthy virtual screening decisions.

Several methodological families have been developed to address uncertainty in molecular property prediction. **Bayesian neural networks** place prior distributions over model weights, enabling posterior inference over predictions and yielding well-calibrated uncertainty estimates. While theoretically rigorous, full Bayesian inference is computationally expensive, prompting the adoption of approximate methods. **Monte Carlo (MC) dropout**, which treats dropout at inference time as a variational approximation to a Bayesian neural network, provides a practical and scalable alternative that has been widely adopted in molecular property prediction tasks. **Deep ensemble methods**, which train multiple independent models with different random initializations, have been shown to produce robust and well-calibrated predictive distributions by aggregating diverse hypotheses about the data-generating process.

**Evidential deep learning** offers a deterministic alternative by directly predicting parameters of a higher-order distribution (e.g., a Normal-Inverse-Gamma distribution), enabling simultaneous estimation of aleatoric and epistemic uncertainty in a single forward pass without sampling. This approach is particularly attractive for large-scale virtual screening where computational efficiency is paramount. **Conformal prediction** provides distribution-free coverage guarantees, constructing prediction intervals with rigorous statistical validity regardless of the underlying model architecture, making it increasingly popular for regulatory-compliant property prediction.

The role of UQ extends beyond simple confidence reporting. In active learning-based virtual screening workflows, uncertainty estimates are used to prioritize compounds for experimental testing, enabling sample-efficient exploration of chemical space [27]. Models that distinguish between epistemic uncertainty (arising from limited training data) and aleatoric uncertainty (inherent data noise) can guide practitioners in identifying regions of chemical space where additional data collection is most valuable. This distinction is especially consequential in contexts where multi-task and multi-modal models are employed, as the shared representations learned across tasks and modalities can amplify or mask different sources of uncertainty. This is particularly relevant in high-throughput screening campaigns targeting novel protein targets, where labeled data is scarce [114].

Out-of-distribution (OOD) detection is another key application of uncertainty quantification. Molecular property models trained on specific chemical scaffolds frequently encounter structurally dissimilar compounds during deployment. High epistemic uncertainty can serve as a signal that a queried molecule falls outside the applicability domain of the model, triggering human review rather than automated decision-making [15]. This concern is directly relevant to few-shot and zero-shot settings, where models must generalize to entirely new assay types or target families with limited grounding in task-specific training data—precisely the regime where OOD detection becomes most critical for safe deployment.

Integration of UQ methods into generative drug design workflows further highlights their importance. When uncertainty-aware scoring functions are coupled with generative models for molecular optimization, the optimization process can be steered toward regions of chemical space where predictions are reliable, avoiding overconfident extrapolation into unexplored territories [59]. This synergy between generative modeling and uncertainty-aware property prediction represents a promising direction for closed-loop autonomous drug discovery, and complements the few-shot learning paradigms discussed in the following section by providing principled measures of prediction reliability when training data is limited.

Despite these advances, several challenges remain. Many UQ methods yield poorly calibrated uncertainties in practice, particularly under distribution shift, motivating ongoing research into post-hoc calibration techniques and temperature scaling. Benchmark comparisons of UQ methods on standardized molecular property datasets using metrics such as expected calibration error (ECE), negative log-likelihood, and reliability diagrams remain limited. Furthermore, the computational overhead of ensemble methods and Bayesian approaches can be prohibitive for screening billion-scale compound libraries, necessitating efficient approximations. Addressing these limitations will be essential for deploying uncertainty-aware property prediction models as reliable components of production-grade drug discovery pipelines [1].

### 3.6 Few-Shot and Zero-Shot Learning for Molecular Properties

## 3.6 Few-Shot and Zero-Shot Learning for Molecular Property Prediction

A fundamental bottleneck in molecular property prediction is the scarcity of labeled experimental data, particularly for novel biological targets, rare disease indications, and newly emerging assay types. This challenge is intimately connected to the uncertainty quantification concerns raised in the preceding section: when labeled data is sparse, epistemic uncertainty is highest, and models must generalize far beyond their training distributions. Traditional supervised learning approaches require large quantities of annotated examples to achieve reliable performance, yet in pharmaceutical research it is common to encounter settings where only a handful of measurements are available for a given target or property. Few-shot and zero-shot learning paradigms have therefore attracted considerable attention as strategies to bridge this data gap and enable generalization to new molecular properties and targets with minimal experimental evidence.

Meta-learning, or "learning to learn," represents one of the most prominent frameworks for few-shot molecular property prediction. In this paradigm, models are trained across a distribution of related tasks—such as activity prediction across multiple assay types—so that they can rapidly adapt to a new task given only a small support set of labeled molecules. Model-Agnostic Meta-Learning (MAML) and its variants have been adapted for molecular graph representations, enabling graph neural network encoders to acquire task-agnostic initializations that facilitate fast fine-tuning on novel assays. Such frameworks leverage the structural similarity across molecular property tasks to extract transferable inductive biases, significantly reducing the labeled data required for a new target. This meta-learning strategy is directly relevant to the toxicity prediction and ADMET modeling challenges discussed in the following section, where rare toxicological endpoints frequently suffer from severe data scarcity.

Neural process-based models offer a complementary perspective by treating each assay as a stochastic process and explicitly modeling the uncertainty arising from limited observations. These approaches account for assay heterogeneity—the fact that different experimental protocols, cell lines, and measurement conditions produce systematically different activity distributions—by learning a probabilistic mapping from a context set of labeled examples to predictive distributions over query molecules. By conditioning predictions on the available support data in a flexible, non-parametric manner, neural process models provide well-calibrated uncertainty estimates alongside point predictions, which is particularly valuable in drug discovery where distinguishing reliable from unreliable predictions can guide experimental prioritization. This probabilistic treatment of assay heterogeneity complements the uncertainty quantification methods discussed previously, extending calibrated confidence estimation into regimes of extreme data scarcity.

Transfer learning and multi-task pretraining strategies also contribute substantially to few-shot generalization. By pretraining on large collections of molecular data across diverse property types—including quantum-chemical properties, bioactivity measurements, and ADMET endpoints—models can acquire rich, transferable molecular representations that require only limited task-specific fine-tuning. Approaches such as those leveraging self-supervised graph pretraining [73] and knowledge-guided pretraining frameworks [74] have demonstrated improved performance in low-data regimes by encoding structural and chemical priors from large-scale unlabeled molecular corpora, effectively augmenting the signal available from sparse labeled sets. These pretraining strategies align closely with the multi-task and multi-modal representation learning frameworks discussed in earlier sections, and they provide the representational foundations upon which the ADMET and toxicity prediction models examined in the following section are built.

The emergence of large language models (LLMs) has opened new avenues for zero-shot property prediction, where models are asked to make predictions about molecular properties for entirely unseen assay types without any task-specific training examples. By encoding vast chemical and biological knowledge acquired during pretraining on scientific literature and molecular databases, LLMs can leverage textual descriptions of assay conditions, target biology, and molecular structures to generate zero-shot predictions. Instruction-tuned models that integrate molecular graph representations with natural language can respond to natural language queries about molecular properties, enabling flexible, zero-shot generalization across a broad range of drug discovery tasks. Multi-modal approaches that fuse chemical language representations with graph-based encoders [92] further enhance zero-shot capabilities by combining complementary sources of structural and semantic information.

Despite promising results, significant challenges remain in few-shot and zero-shot molecular property prediction. Assay heterogeneity means that task distributions used during meta-training may not faithfully reflect the diversity of assays encountered at deployment, leading to degraded performance on truly novel targets. Additionally, zero-shot LLM predictions may suffer from hallucination and poor calibration, particularly for properties that require precise quantitative reasoning beyond the model's pretraining distribution—a concern that intersects directly with the calibration challenges discussed in the context of uncertainty quantification. Benchmarking efforts employing rigorous scaffold-based splits and standardized evaluation protocols are critical for fairly assessing progress in this area [46], as naive random splits can overestimate generalization performance by allowing similar scaffolds to appear in both training and test sets; this same rigor underpins the standardized ADMET benchmarking protocols described in the following section. Overall, few-shot and zero-shot learning frameworks represent a rapidly advancing frontier that promises to substantially reduce the experimental burden of molecular property characterization—including the specific ADMET, solubility, and toxicity prediction tasks examined next—and accelerate the early stages of drug discovery.

### 3.7 Prediction of Specific Molecular Properties: ADMET, Solubility, and Toxicity

## 3.7 Prediction of Specific Molecular Properties: ADMET, Solubility, and Toxicity

Accurate prediction of specific molecular properties—particularly ADMET (Absorption, Distribution, Metabolism, Excretion, and Toxicity) profiles, aqueous solubility, and toxicity—is central to the drug discovery pipeline. Failures in these properties account for a substantial fraction of late-stage clinical attrition, making early and reliable in silico prediction indispensable for reducing costs and accelerating development timelines. Building on the few-shot and zero-shot learning paradigms discussed in the preceding section, property-specific prediction models further benefit from transfer learning and multi-task pretraining strategies that address the pervasive challenge of sparse labeled data across diverse molecular endpoints.

### ADMET Property Prediction

ADMET prediction encompasses a broad suite of pharmacokinetic and pharmacodynamic properties that collectively determine whether a candidate molecule is likely to behave safely and effectively in a biological system. Machine learning methods for ADMET prediction have evolved from classical QSAR models relying on hand-crafted molecular descriptors and fingerprints to modern deep learning architectures that learn representations directly from molecular graphs or SMILES strings [22; 27]. Graph neural networks (GNNs) have demonstrated particular effectiveness in this domain due to their ability to capture local chemical environments and long-range atomic interactions without requiring explicit feature engineering. Multi-task learning strategies, which simultaneously optimize models across multiple ADMET endpoints, have further improved performance by leveraging shared chemical information across related properties and mitigating the effect of sparse labeled data for individual endpoints [22]. Transformer-based molecular language models pretrained on large unlabeled chemical corpora have also shown competitive performance when fine-tuned on ADMET benchmarks, benefiting from rich contextual representations of molecular substructures.

A notable challenge in ADMET modeling is the heterogeneity of experimental assay conditions across datasets, which introduces noise and reduces generalizability—a concern closely related to the assay heterogeneity issues highlighted in the context of few-shot learning. Multi-task frameworks that account for assay-specific variability have been proposed to address this issue, enabling models to disentangle intrinsic molecular properties from measurement artifacts [27]. Platforms such as the Therapeutic Data Commons (TDC) have been instrumental in standardizing ADMET benchmarking by providing curated datasets with consistent evaluation protocols, including scaffold-based splits that assess genuine generalization to structurally novel molecules—the same rigor advocated for evaluating few-shot and zero-shot approaches.

### Aqueous Solubility Prediction

Aqueous solubility is a fundamental physicochemical property that directly influences drug absorption and bioavailability. Low solubility is a major cause of compound attrition in early drug discovery, making accurate prediction critical for prioritizing synthetic efforts. Classical approaches relied on physicochemical descriptors such as logP, polar surface area, and hydrogen bond donors and acceptors, but these hand-crafted features fail to capture the complex structural determinants of solubility in diverse chemical spaces [22].

Modern deep learning approaches for solubility prediction have leveraged attention-based graph neural network architectures that can identify key molecular substructures contributing to solubility, combining predictive accuracy with interpretability. Bayesian and uncertainty-aware GNN formulations are particularly valuable in this context, as they provide calibrated confidence estimates that allow practitioners to distinguish reliable predictions from uncertain extrapolations for structurally unusual compounds. These uncertainty-aware models are especially important in active learning workflows where solubility predictions guide experimental prioritization [22]—a connection that becomes central to the virtual screening pipelines discussed in the following section. Benchmark evaluations on standard datasets such as ESOL and FreeSolv have demonstrated that attention-based GNNs consistently outperform classical fingerprint-based regression models, with ensemble methods providing additional gains through variance reduction.

### Toxicity Prediction

Toxicity prediction represents one of the most challenging molecular property prediction tasks due to the diversity of toxicological mechanisms, the extreme imbalance of toxic versus non-toxic compounds in screening datasets, and the complexity of translating in vitro signals to in vivo outcomes. Machine learning models for toxicity prediction span a range of architectures, from classical random forests and support vector machines trained on molecular fingerprints to deep ensemble predictors and few-shot learning frameworks designed to handle data scarcity for rare toxicity endpoints [22; 27].

Ensemble-based methods have proven particularly effective for toxicity prediction, as aggregating predictions from diverse model architectures reduces variance and improves calibration—critical factors when model outputs are used to make compound selection decisions with safety implications. Few-shot learning approaches, including meta-learning frameworks such as Model-Agnostic Meta-Learning (MAML) adapted for molecular graphs, address the practical scenario where only a small number of labeled toxic compounds are available for a given endpoint, enabling generalization from related toxicity tasks [22]. This directly extends the meta-learning strategies outlined in the previous section to the specialized demands of toxicological endpoints. Multi-task learning further amplifies these benefits by exploiting the correlational structure among different toxicity endpoints, such as those in the Tox21 and ToxCast datasets, which include assays measuring nuclear receptor binding, stress response pathways, and cytotoxicity across multiple cell lines.

Explainability is especially important in the toxicity domain, where regulatory compliance and mechanistic understanding are essential. Attribution methods such as gradient-based saliency maps, attention visualization, and GNN explanation techniques can identify the molecular substructures most responsible for predicted toxicity, providing actionable insights for medicinal chemists to guide structural modification and toxicity mitigation [78; 82]. These machine-learned structure-toxicity relationships serve as a bridge between computational predictions and experimental validation, reinforcing scientific trust in model-guided compound optimization.

### Integrated Multi-Property Optimization

Beyond individual property prediction, the simultaneous optimization of ADMET profiles alongside binding affinity and drug-likeness is a key challenge in lead optimization. Multi-task deep learning frameworks that co-predict diverse molecular properties within a shared representation learning architecture have shown promise in this regard, enabling holistic evaluation of compound candidates across the full spectrum of drug discovery criteria [22; 27]. The integration of uncertainty quantification into these multi-property models ensures that predictions are accompanied by reliable confidence measures, guiding experimental prioritization and reducing costly failures in subsequent development stages. Such multi-property optimization frameworks naturally feed into virtual screening workflows, where property predictions must be evaluated rapidly and at scale across large compound libraries.

Overall, the field of specific molecular property prediction has matured considerably, with deep learning models now routinely achieving performance that rivals or exceeds traditional cheminformatics approaches on established benchmarks. Nevertheless, challenges remain in generalizing to structurally novel chemical spaces, handling noisy and imbalanced experimental data, and ensuring that in silico predictions translate reliably to in vivo outcomes—areas that continue to motivate active methodological development and that set the stage for the large-scale virtual screening pipelines examined in the following section.

### 3.8 Virtual Screening Workflows and High-Throughput Applications

## 3.8 Virtual Screening and High-Throughput Compound Prioritization

Virtual screening (VS) workflows leverage computational methods to rapidly evaluate large compound libraries and prioritize candidates for experimental validation, dramatically reducing the cost and time associated with early-stage drug discovery. Building directly on the multi-property prediction frameworks and uncertainty-aware models discussed in the preceding section, machine learning has become deeply integrated into these pipelines, enabling both structure-based and ligand-based screening at scales previously unattainable with traditional physics-based methods alone. The property predictions generated by ADMET, solubility, and toxicity models serve as critical filters within these workflows, ensuring that computationally prioritized compounds meet pharmacokinetic and safety criteria before progressing to experimental testing.

### Structure-Based and Ligand-Based Screening Pipelines

In structure-based virtual screening, molecular docking is combined with machine learning rescoring functions to improve hit rates against known protein targets. Traditional docking programs generate binding poses and estimate affinities using physics-based scoring functions, but these functions often lack accuracy. Deep learning models trained on structural data from databases such as PDBbind can re-rank docked poses more accurately, filtering out false positives and elevating true binders in ranked compound libraries. The integration of multimodal molecular representations—combining SMILES sequences, molecular graphs, and fingerprint vectors—into unified scoring frameworks has further enhanced the discriminative power of these pipelines [92].

Ligand-based virtual screening exploits chemical similarity and learned property models to identify compounds with profiles resembling known active molecules. Classical molecular fingerprints such as ECFP and MACCS keys remain widely used for similarity searches, but learned representations derived from chemical language models and graph neural networks have demonstrated superior discriminative performance, particularly in scaffold-hopping scenarios where structurally distinct molecules share biological activity [115]. This advantage of learned representations over classical fingerprints mirrors the broader trend observed in ADMET and solubility prediction, where deep learning architectures consistently outperform hand-crafted descriptor-based approaches on structurally diverse compound sets. Multi-modal models that combine SMILES encoders, fingerprints, and graph convolutional networks can capture complementary chemical information, improving both accuracy and robustness against noisy or sparse data [92].

### High-Throughput Screening and Scalability

A central challenge in high-throughput virtual screening is scalability: commercial and enumerated chemical libraries now contain hundreds of millions to billions of compounds, making exhaustive scoring computationally prohibitive even with fast machine learning models. Deep fusion approaches that combine multiple molecular representations within a single inference pass can achieve substantial throughput while maintaining predictive quality [116]. Pre-trained molecular language models such as MoLFormer, trained on over one billion SMILES sequences, enable rapid embedding generation that supports large-scale screening with minimal computational overhead [91]. The efficiency of SMILES-based Transformer models is particularly relevant in this context: direct inference from SMILES strings, without expensive graph conversion steps, can achieve 3–14× speedups compared to graph-based models, significantly accelerating throughput for billion-scale compound databases [88]. These scalability gains are especially consequential when molecular property filters—such as the ADMET and toxicity models described in the preceding section—must be applied across the full breadth of a screening library before experimental resources are committed.

### Active Learning and Bayesian Optimization Approaches

Rather than exhaustively scoring entire compound libraries, active learning frameworks iteratively select the most informative compounds for evaluation, enabling efficient exploration of large chemical spaces with minimal experimental or computational budget. Bayesian optimization integrates probabilistic property prediction models—such as the uncertainty-aware ensemble models and Bayesian deep learning approaches highlighted in the solubility and ADMET prediction context—with acquisition functions that balance exploration and exploitation. These well-calibrated uncertainty estimates, which were identified as critical for guiding experimental prioritization in property prediction workflows, play an equally pivotal role in active learning-driven screening: they allow the selection of the next batch of compounds most likely to yield high-affinity hits, achieving sample efficiency several orders of magnitude better than random screening. Such approaches are particularly valuable when experimental throughput is limited or when screening against novel targets with sparse prior data.

The combination of pre-trained molecular representations with active learning frameworks has demonstrated particular promise for few-shot and low-data settings. Pre-trained models such as SMILES Transformer [89] transfer rich chemical knowledge from large unlabeled corpora to downstream screening tasks, substantially improving generalization when only a handful of labeled actives are available—a common scenario in early-stage drug discovery campaigns, and one that directly parallels the few-shot learning challenges addressed in Section 3.6.

### Practical Applications: COVID-19 and Emerging Disease Targets

The COVID-19 pandemic provided a high-profile test case for large-scale ML-driven virtual screening. Researchers rapidly deployed machine learning models to screen vast libraries of approved drugs and novel compounds against SARS-CoV-2 targets, including the main protease, RNA-dependent RNA polymerase, and spike protein. Multi-modal deep learning models combining molecular graphs and SMILES representations were applied to large compound databases to rapidly prioritize candidates for experimental testing [92]. Drug repurposing campaigns leveraged pre-trained molecular embeddings and similarity-based screening to identify existing approved drugs with potential anti-COVID-19 activity, enabling rapid progression to experimental validation without the lengthy timelines of de novo discovery.

Multimodal fusion models demonstrated that incorporating multiple representations—SMILES-encoded vectors, ECFP fingerprints, and molecular graphs processed by Transformer-Encoder, BiGRU, and GCN architectures—improved accuracy, reliability, and noise resistance in screening applications, outperforming single-modality baselines across diverse molecular property datasets [92]. These findings highlight that the complementary information captured by different molecular modalities is particularly valuable when navigating the diverse chemical structures encountered in large-scale screening campaigns.

### Computational Feasibility and Future Outlook

Computational feasibility remains a primary concern as screening libraries grow in size. Hierarchical screening protocols—where fast, approximate models perform an initial coarse filtering followed by more accurate but computationally expensive models for the top-ranked subset—provide a practical path to billion-scale virtual screening. Efficient SMILES-based Transformer models can serve as high-throughput first-pass filters, leveraging their direct inference from string representations to rapidly process massive compound collections [88], while more sophisticated graph-based or structure-based models are reserved for the refined candidate subset. The continued development of scalable pre-training strategies on large molecular databases [91], combined with active learning-driven experimental prioritization, is expected to make end-to-end AI-guided virtual screening campaigns increasingly feasible and impactful across a wide range of therapeutic targets. As these pipelines mature, the interpretability of model predictions—determining not just which compounds rank highest but why specific structural features drive their prioritization—becomes the natural next concern, motivating the discussion of explainability methods in the following section.

### 3.9 Interpretability and Explainability in Property Prediction Models

## 3.9 Interpretability and Explainability in Property Prediction Models

As machine learning models grow increasingly complex in their architecture and predictive capacity, the ability to interpret and explain their outputs has become a critical concern in drug discovery. Medicinal chemists require more than accurate predictions—they need mechanistic insight into which structural features, substructures, or physicochemical properties drive a model's decisions. This need for interpretability is closely intertwined with the benchmarking challenges discussed in the previous section: just as standardized evaluation protocols are essential for comparing model performance, principled explainability methods are essential for validating that high-performing models capture chemically meaningful patterns rather than dataset artifacts. Interpretability thus serves as a bridge between black-box predictive systems and actionable, hypothesis-driven medicinal chemistry, complementing rigorous benchmarking as a pillar of trustworthy model development.

### Gradient-Based Attribution Methods

Gradient-based attribution techniques constitute one of the foundational classes of explainability methods for neural network-based property prediction. Methods such as saliency maps, integrated gradients, and SmoothGrad assign importance scores to individual input features—whether atoms, bonds, or fingerprint bits—by computing partial derivatives of the model output with respect to the inputs. In the context of molecular graph neural networks, these gradients can be backpropagated to individual nodes and edges, highlighting atoms or bonds that most influence the predicted property. This capability is particularly valuable for elucidating structure-activity relationships (SAR), enabling chemists to identify pharmacophoric elements responsible for binding affinity or toxicity. Importantly, gradient-based explanations can serve as diagnostic tools for identifying spurious correlations in training data, directly addressing reproducibility concerns that arise in standard benchmarking contexts.

### Attention Visualization

Transformer-based models and graph attention networks inherently produce attention weights as a byproduct of their operation, and these weights can be visualized to provide interpretable insights. In molecular property prediction, attention maps reveal which atomic neighborhoods or inter-atomic interactions the model prioritizes when computing a prediction. For SMILES-based language models, attention patterns across token positions can highlight reactive functional groups or scaffold motifs correlated with specific biological activities. Such visualizations are intuitive for medicinal chemists and support rapid hypothesis generation, particularly when exploring unexplored regions of chemical space. The self-attention mechanism in models trained on SMILES representations [117] naturally lends itself to this form of interpretability, as attention weights across sequence positions correspond to chemically meaningful token interactions. These attention-based explanations also enable practitioners to assess whether model behavior is consistent across different data splits—a key consideration when evaluating model robustness within benchmarking frameworks.

### Hierarchical Graph Explainability

Graph neural networks (GNNs) for molecular property prediction benefit from hierarchical explainability approaches that go beyond node-level attribution. Methods such as GNNExplainer, PGExplainer, and SubgraphX identify critical subgraphs or molecular fragments that causally determine the model's output. These techniques decompose the molecular graph into substructures—rings, chains, functional groups, and pharmacophoric motifs—and assign importance scores to each. The hierarchical nature of such explanations aligns well with how medicinal chemists conceptualize molecular structure: not just as isolated atoms, but as assemblages of functional units. Topological fingerprint approaches, such as those based on multiparameter persistent homology [118; 119; 120], offer complementary interpretability by revealing the topological features—connected components, cycles, and higher-order structures—that persist across multiple scales and chemical parameters, providing a comprehensive characterization of molecular topology.

### Machine-Learned Pharmacophores

An emerging and particularly chemically meaningful form of interpretability involves extracting machine-learned pharmacophore models from trained property prediction networks. Pharmacophores abstract molecular recognition into spatial arrangements of features such as hydrogen bond donors/acceptors, hydrophobic regions, and charged groups. When GNNs or other deep learning models are trained on binding affinity or bioactivity data, post-hoc analysis techniques can extract implicit pharmacophore hypotheses encoded in the learned representations. The Pharmacoprint framework [121] exemplifies this direction, encoding the presence, types, and spatial relationships between pharmacophore features as high-dimensional fingerprints that are both predictively powerful and interpretable. By coupling such representations with feature selection and dimensionality reduction, the most informative pharmacophoric bits can be identified, directly supporting rational drug design.

### Fingerprint-Level Interpretability

Classical molecular fingerprints, despite their fixed and sometimes opaque nature, offer a degree of inherent interpretability through bit decomposition. Extended-connectivity fingerprints (ECFPs) map each bit to a specific circular substructure centered on a given atom at a particular radius, enabling direct structural assignment of active fingerprint features [122; 123]. Methods that improve fingerprint vectorization, such as Sort & Slice [122], also improve interpretability by removing ambiguous bit collisions inherent in hash-based folding, ensuring that each active bit corresponds to a unique, identifiable substructure. Fragment-based fingerprints generated through tools like SPECTRe [124] provide explicit enumeration of all molecular substructures, facilitating transparent analysis of which chemical building blocks are most predictive. Similarly, topological fingerprints derived from k-mer and fragment-based representations [115] improve discriminative power while retaining structural interpretability through their substructure-counting basis. These fingerprint-level explanations are closely connected to benchmarking considerations, as the choice of atom featurization and fingerprint representation can substantially affect model performance across standard evaluation datasets.

### Integration with Multi-Modal Models

Multi-modal property prediction models that combine molecular images, SMILES strings, graphs, and fingerprints introduce additional complexity for interpretability, yet also offer richer explanatory possibilities. Attention-based fusion mechanisms in such architectures can reveal which modality—and which specific features within that modality—is most informative for a given prediction. For example, models that augment molecular images with binary vector representations such as Morgan fingerprints and MACCS keys [125] allow class activation mapping on the image component alongside bit-level analysis of the vector component, providing complementary structural and visual explanations. Similarly, architectures like CheMixNet [116] that jointly process SMILES sequences and molecular fingerprints can be interrogated through both sequence-level attention and fingerprint feature importance to yield multi-perspective structural explanations. Understanding which modality dominates in such fusion models also has direct implications for benchmarking, as it can illuminate why certain architectures perform better on particular datasets or evaluation metrics.

### Role in Scientific Trust and Regulatory Compliance

Beyond scientific insight, interpretability is increasingly recognized as essential for building trust in AI-driven drug discovery workflows and for meeting regulatory expectations. Regulatory agencies such as the FDA and EMA are beginning to scrutinize AI models used in pharmaceutical research, demanding transparency and justifiability in model predictions. Explainable AI (XAI) methods that clearly map model outputs to specific molecular structural features not only facilitate peer review and reproducibility—echoing the reproducibility challenges highlighted in benchmarking—but also help identify potential biases, spurious correlations, or overfitting to dataset-specific artifacts. When models identify chemically implausible or counterintuitive features as predictive, these anomalies serve as diagnostic signals that prompt re-examination of training data quality, label accuracy, and model architecture. Uncertainty-aware explainability, as explored in frameworks combining gradient boosting with Bayesian uncertainty estimation [120], further strengthens this trust by distinguishing confident, structurally grounded predictions from uncertain extrapolations, enabling more informed decision-making in prioritizing compounds for experimental validation.

### Outlook

The field of interpretable molecular property prediction is rapidly maturing, moving from post-hoc explanation of opaque models toward the co-design of inherently interpretable architectures. Future directions include the development of concept-based explanation methods that align with established chemical concepts, causal attribution frameworks that distinguish predictive correlations from mechanistic relationships, and interactive visualization platforms that empower non-expert users to interrogate model decisions. The integration of interpretability with standardized benchmarking protocols represents a particularly important frontier: as the community converges on consensus evaluation standards, embedding explainability assessments alongside predictive performance metrics will be essential for ensuring that reported advances reflect genuine chemical understanding rather than dataset-specific optimization. As ML models are deployed in increasingly high-stakes drug discovery decisions, the symbiosis between predictive power and mechanistic transparency will be a defining criterion for model adoption in pharmaceutical research.

### 3.10 Benchmarking Platforms and Evaluation Standards for Property Prediction

## 3.8 Benchmarking and Evaluation Protocols for Molecular Property Prediction

Rigorous benchmarking is essential for fairly evaluating and comparing molecular property prediction models, yet the field faces persistent challenges related to reproducibility, standardization, and meaningful model comparison. Establishing robust evaluation frameworks is not merely a technical formality—it is a prerequisite for distinguishing genuine advances in molecular representation learning from artifacts of experimental design. Several established platforms and datasets have emerged as community standards, each with distinct characteristics, strengths, and limitations, and understanding these resources is foundational before turning to the interpretability considerations that follow.

**MoleculeNet** remains one of the most widely adopted benchmarking suites for molecular property prediction, aggregating datasets spanning quantum mechanics (QM7, QM8, QM9), physical chemistry (ESOL, FreeSolv, Lipophilicity), biophysics (PCBA, MUV, HIV, BACE), and physiology (BBBP, Tox21, ToxCast, SIDER, ClinTox). Numerous studies evaluate their models against MoleculeNet benchmarks, enabling cross-method comparisons across diverse property prediction tasks [94; 96; 102; 104; 111].

**Therapeutic Data Commons (TDC)** has emerged as a more recent and comprehensive benchmarking resource, offering standardized datasets and evaluation protocols across the entire drug discovery pipeline, including ADMET prediction, drug-target interaction, and drug combination tasks. TDC provides community-accepted data splits, leaderboards, and unified evaluation frameworks that address many shortcomings of earlier ad hoc comparisons.

**PDBbind** serves as the standard benchmark for structure-based property prediction, particularly binding affinity estimation between protein-ligand complexes. It provides experimentally determined binding affinities paired with three-dimensional structural data, enabling evaluation of scoring functions and structure-based deep learning models. **ToxCast** and **Tox21** are widely used for toxicity prediction benchmarking, providing high-throughput screening data across hundreds of biochemical assays, with Tox21 specifically providing 12 toxicology endpoints used in multiple machine learning challenges.

**Data Splitting Strategies** critically influence reported performance and comparability across methods. Random splits, while simple, allow chemically similar molecules to appear in both training and test sets, leading to artificially inflated performance estimates. Scaffold-based splits—which partition molecules according to their core chemical scaffolds—provide a more realistic assessment of a model's ability to generalize to structurally novel compounds, more faithfully mimicking real-world drug discovery scenarios where predictions must be made for chemically diverse candidates [96; 104; 126]. Time-based splits are also employed when temporal generalization is of interest, though they are less common in standard benchmarks.

**Evaluation Metrics** vary by task type and require careful selection to reflect scientific relevance. For binary classification tasks such as toxicity prediction or activity classification, the area under the receiver operating characteristic curve (AUC-ROC) is most commonly reported, though the area under the precision-recall curve (AUPRC) is particularly important for highly imbalanced datasets where positive examples are rare [127; 128]. For regression tasks such as binding affinity or solubility prediction, mean absolute error (MAE), root mean square error (RMSE), Pearson correlation coefficient, and the concordance index (CI) are standard metrics, with the CI being particularly informative for ranking-based applications such as virtual screening.

**Challenges in Reproducibility and Fair Comparison** are pervasive in the field. Different studies often use inconsistent data preprocessing pipelines, different random seeds, different scaffold split implementations, and different train-validation-test proportions, making direct comparison of reported results unreliable. Furthermore, many works report performance only on selected datasets or metrics, cherry-picking favorable results. The choice of atom featurization and molecular representation can also substantially affect performance independently of model architecture [107], yet these details are often underreported. These reproducibility concerns motivate the interpretability methods discussed in the following section, as transparent models whose predictions can be traced to specific structural features are inherently more auditable and easier to validate across different experimental settings.

Pretraining and multi-task strategies further complicate fair benchmarking, as models pretrained on large external datasets benefit from information not available to simpler baselines trained only on the benchmark data [110; 103; 109; 105]. Standardized protocols that specify whether external pretraining data is permissible, and which pretrained weights are used, are necessary for meaningful comparisons.

Emerging large-scale benchmarks such as **PCQM4Mv2** from the Open Graph Benchmark Large-Scale Challenge (OGB-LSC) and **OPV** and **OCELOTv1** for organic semiconductor property prediction [106] extend the evaluation landscape to larger molecular datasets and more challenging property prediction tasks. These benchmarks help assess scalability and generalization of novel architectures beyond the relatively small datasets in MoleculeNet.

Collectively, the community has made significant progress toward standardized evaluation, but ongoing efforts are needed to ensure that reported improvements reflect genuine advances in molecular representation learning and prediction capability rather than artifacts of evaluation choices. Establishing consensus on split strategies, metrics, and pretraining protocols, alongside open-source evaluation pipelines, remains a critical priority for advancing the rigor and reproducibility of machine learning for molecular property prediction. As discussed in the following section, these benchmarking considerations are deeply intertwined with interpretability: principled explainability methods are essential for validating that high-performing models capture chemically meaningful patterns rather than dataset-specific artifacts, and the two pillars together form the foundation of trustworthy model development in drug discovery.

## 4 Molecular Generation and Optimization

### 4.1 Overview of Molecular Generation and Lead Optimization

## 4.1 Overview of Molecular Generation and Lead Optimization

De novo drug design and lead optimization represent two of the most challenging and consequential stages in the modern drug discovery pipeline. The fundamental goal of de novo molecular generation is to construct entirely novel chemical entities with desired pharmacological properties—such as high binding affinity, favorable ADMET profiles, drug-likeness, and synthetic accessibility—without necessarily relying on prior knowledge of known active compounds. Lead optimization, by contrast, begins from an existing molecule with demonstrated biological activity and seeks to improve its properties through systematic structural modifications, aiming to increase potency, selectivity, and pharmacokinetic performance while reducing toxicity [5; 6].

A central challenge in both tasks is the astronomical size of drug-like chemical space, which is estimated to contain between 10²³ and 10⁶⁰ theoretically synthesizable small molecules [16]. Exhaustive enumeration or brute-force screening of this space is computationally infeasible, making intelligent, goal-directed search strategies essential. Traditional medicinal chemistry approaches rely heavily on expert intuition and iterative experimental cycles, a process that is both time-consuming and expensive [1]. The advent of deep generative models has introduced powerful data-driven alternatives capable of navigating this vast chemical landscape more efficiently and systematically [2].

Deep generative approaches for molecular design can be broadly taxonomized along several dimensions. Based on the underlying generative framework, these methods include variational autoencoders (VAEs), which learn continuous latent spaces for molecular interpolation and optimization; generative adversarial networks (GANs), which pit a generator against a discriminator to produce realistic molecular structures; normalizing flows, which model exact likelihood distributions over molecules; recurrent neural networks (RNNs) and language models, which generate molecules as sequential strings such as SMILES; and diffusion models, which iteratively denoise random inputs into valid 3D molecular structures [83; 2]. Reinforcement learning (RL) provides a complementary optimization paradigm, enabling goal-directed generation by rewarding molecules that satisfy multiple property constraints simultaneously.

Beyond the generative framework, molecular generation methods can also be classified by the molecular representation they operate on—string-based (e.g., SMILES, SELFIES), graph-based (2D molecular graphs), or 3D geometry-based (atomic coordinates)—and by whether they are ligand-based, operating independently of any specific protein target, or structure-based, explicitly conditioning generation on the 3D geometry of a binding pocket [129; 130]. This representational dimension is of foundational importance, as the choice of molecular representation shapes the quality, validity, diversity, and synthesizability of generated molecules in ways that are deeply intertwined with the choice of generative architecture—a relationship that the following subsection examines in detail. A key practical consideration that cuts across all these categories is synthesizability: generative models must not only produce chemically valid and drug-like molecules, but also candidates that can realistically be synthesized in a laboratory setting [84]. Addressing the tension between molecular novelty, diversity, desired property optimization, and synthetic accessibility remains a central open challenge in the field [85].

In the following subsections, we first examine the landscape of molecular representations employed by generative models—spanning SMILES strings, molecular graphs, 3D coordinate-based encodings, and fragment-based decompositions—before turning to each generative paradigm in detail. For each paradigm, we discuss architectural innovations, representative methods, benchmark performance, and the strategies employed to balance these competing objectives in practical drug discovery workflows.

### 4.2 Molecular Representations for Generative Models

The choice of molecular representation is a foundational decision in the design of generative models for drug discovery, as it fundamentally shapes the quality, validity, diversity, and synthesizability of the molecules produced—a concern introduced in the overview above and one that runs throughout all the generative paradigms discussed in this section [2]. Different representations encode distinct aspects of molecular structure and chemistry, and each comes with its own trade-offs in terms of expressiveness, computational tractability, and compatibility with downstream generative architectures such as VAEs, GANs, RNNs, and diffusion models.

**SMILES-Based Representations**

SMILES (Simplified Molecular Input Line Entry System) strings remain among the most widely adopted representations in generative models due to their compact, linear encoding of molecular topology and their natural compatibility with sequence-based architectures such as recurrent neural networks (RNNs), long short-term memory networks (LSTMs), and Transformer-based language models [83; 131]. By treating a molecule as a sequence of characters, models can leverage the rich ecosystem of natural language processing techniques, including transfer learning and fine-tuning on large chemical corpora, to learn the syntactic and semantic rules of chemical space [131]. However, a well-known limitation of SMILES is that minor perturbations in the string—such as a single character substitution—can lead to chemically invalid or structurally very different molecules, resulting in high rates of syntactically invalid outputs from naive generative models. Alternatives such as SELFIES (Self-Referencing Embedded Strings) have been proposed to address this validity issue by construction, guaranteeing that any generated string decodes to a valid molecule. As discussed in the following subsection on VAEs, this invalidity problem is particularly consequential for decoder networks that must map continuous latent vectors back to discrete molecular strings.

**Molecular Graph Representations**

Molecular graphs, where atoms are represented as nodes and bonds as edges, offer a more chemically natural and information-rich representation. Graph-based generative models—including graph variational autoencoders, graph generative adversarial networks, and graph normalizing flows—can directly operate on this representation, preserving the topological structure of molecules without the ambiguity and invalidity issues inherent to string encodings [2; 85]. The expressiveness of graph representations is further enhanced by incorporating rich atom and bond features such as atomic number, hybridization state, aromaticity, and bond order, enabling models to learn fine-grained structure-activity relationships [19]. Graph-based representations have proven particularly effective in molecular property prediction and optimization tasks, as they naturally support message-passing and attention mechanisms that aggregate local and global chemical information [78]. These properties make graph representations a natural fit for the VAE-based architectures described in the following subsection, where graph neural network encoders and decoders have demonstrated improved validity and diversity relative to their string-based counterparts.

**3D Atomic Density Grids and Coordinate-Based Representations**

For structure-based drug design, where the three-dimensional geometry of both the ligand and the protein binding pocket must be considered, 3D representations become essential. Atomic density grids represent molecules as volumetric data by discretizing 3D space into voxels and assigning atomic properties to each voxel, enabling the use of 3D convolutional neural networks for both generation and scoring [41]. While expressive in capturing spatial arrangements, voxel-based representations are computationally expensive due to their cubic scaling with resolution and suffer from rotation sensitivity unless explicit equivariance is enforced. Point cloud representations, which directly encode 3D atomic coordinates and associated features, offer a more memory-efficient alternative. Equivariant neural network architectures, such as SE(3)- and E(3)-equivariant networks, have been developed to process such representations while respecting the physical symmetries of molecular geometry, significantly improving generalization and physical plausibility [41]. These 3D coordinate-based representations are directly relevant to the 3D VAE architectures and diffusion-based models covered in subsequent subsections, which rely on atomic density grids or equivariant encodings to generate conformationally realistic molecular structures.

**Fragment-Based Representations**

Fragment-based representations decompose molecules into chemically meaningful substructures—such as rings, functional groups, and BRICS fragments—and represent molecules as compositions or assemblies of these building blocks [2]. This approach aligns closely with established medicinal chemistry practices in fragment-based drug discovery, enabling generative models to propose molecules that are more likely to be synthesizable and drug-like, since they are constructed from fragments known to possess favorable properties [84]. Fragment-based generative models can operate at a coarser granularity than atom-level models, reducing the effective search space and improving the chemical diversity and synthetic accessibility of generated candidates. Scaffold-based and R-group decoration strategies are further variants that constrain generation around known pharmacophoric cores while diversifying peripheral substituents—a strategy that is directly relevant to the lead optimization workflows discussed in the overview and returned to throughout this section.

**Topological and Descriptor-Based Features**

Beyond direct structural representations, topological data analysis (TDA) and classical molecular descriptors provide complementary perspectives on molecular structure. Persistent homology and other TDA-derived features encode multi-scale topological properties of molecules that are invariant to geometric perturbations, offering robust representations for learning tasks where global molecular shape is important. Physicochemical descriptors—such as molecular weight, lipophilicity (logP), hydrogen bond donor/acceptor counts, and polar surface area—have long served as features in QSAR models and remain useful auxiliary inputs in hybrid generative models that incorporate property constraints during generation [83].

**Impact of Representation Choice on Generation Quality**

The choice of molecular representation profoundly influences the quality and characteristics of molecules produced by generative models, and understanding these trade-offs is essential context for evaluating the architectures described in the subsections that follow. SMILES-based models tend to generate high volumes of molecules rapidly but may exhibit lower chemical validity rates without validity constraints. Graph-based models generally achieve higher validity and better capture of local chemical environments but may require more complex architectures and training procedures. 3D representations enable target-aware generation that accounts for binding pocket geometry but are more computationally demanding and require conformer generation as a preprocessing step. Fragment-based representations tend to yield more synthesizable and drug-like outputs, but may limit chemical novelty to combinations of known fragments [84]. In practice, many state-of-the-art generative frameworks combine multiple representations—for instance, pairing SMILES strings with molecular graphs or integrating 2D topological features with 3D geometric information—to capture complementary aspects of molecular structure and improve the overall quality and diversity of generated drug candidates [2; 19]. With this representational landscape established, the following subsections examine each major generative paradigm in turn, beginning with variational autoencoders, which have been among the most widely applied frameworks for exploiting these representations in continuous latent space optimization.

### 4.3 Variational Autoencoders for Molecular Generation

Variational autoencoders (VAEs) represent another foundational deep generative architecture for de novo molecular generation and lead optimization in drug discovery, complementing the graph-based and fragment-based representations discussed previously. By learning a continuous, low-dimensional latent space from molecular data, VAEs enable principled sampling, smooth interpolation between chemical structures, and gradient-based property optimization—capabilities that are particularly valuable in navigating the vast and complex chemical space inherent to drug design. Whereas GAN-based approaches rely on adversarial training dynamics that can suffer from mode collapse and training instability, VAEs offer a more stable optimization landscape through their evidence lower bound (ELBO) objective, though they introduce their own trade-offs in terms of reconstruction fidelity and latent space regularity.

**Standard and Conditional VAEs**

In the standard VAE framework, an encoder network maps input molecules—typically represented as SMILES strings or molecular graphs—to a probabilistic latent distribution, while a decoder reconstructs valid molecular structures from sampled latent vectors. The resulting continuous latent space allows for interpolation between known compounds and random sampling to generate novel molecules. Conditional VAEs extend this paradigm by conditioning both the encoder and decoder on desired molecular properties, such as binding affinity or drug-likeness, thereby enabling targeted generation of molecules with specific pharmacological profiles [30]. This conditional generation framework is central to goal-directed drug design, where the aim is not merely novelty but the achievement of specific therapeutic objectives—a key concern shared across all generative paradigms discussed in this section.

**Three-Dimensional and Graph-Based VAE Architectures**

A significant advancement in VAE-based molecular generation has been the extension to three-dimensional molecular representations, directly addressing the need for structure-aware generation highlighted in the discussion of molecular representations. Rather than relying on flat SMILES encodings, 3D VAE models use atomic density grids or volumetric representations to encode the full spatial geometry of molecules [30]. These models jointly represent drug-like molecules and their conformations in a shared latent space, enabling the exploration of conformational diversity alongside chemical diversity. A novel fitting algorithm converts continuous grid-based latent representations back to discrete molecular structures, addressing the inherent challenge of translating probabilistic latent samples into chemically valid outputs.

Graph-based VAEs offer another powerful extension by operating directly on molecular graphs, where atoms and bonds are represented as nodes and edges. By leveraging graph neural network encoders and decoders, these architectures can capture the topological and chemical structure of molecules more faithfully than string-based approaches, leading to improved validity and diversity of generated compounds—consistent with the advantages of graph representations identified in the preceding discussion of molecular encodings.

**Integration with Active Learning and Multi-Objective Optimization**

VAEs have demonstrated particular utility when coupled with active learning frameworks for iterative lead optimization. In such workflows, the VAE's continuous latent space is explored using active learning strategies that iteratively query informative regions guided by molecular metrics such as drug-likeness, synthesizability, similarity to known actives, and docking scores [59]. This approach addresses a key limitation of standalone generative models—low affinity towards biological targets and poor ADME/PK properties—by incorporating molecular modeling simulations in a final selection step. Applications to CDK2 and KRAS model systems have demonstrated that VAE-based active learning workflows can generate chemically viable molecules with high predicted target affinity, uncovering novel scaffolds significantly dissimilar from known inhibitors [59].

**Adversarially Regularized and Hybrid Architectures**

Beyond standard VAEs, adversarially regularized autoencoders combine the reconstruction objectives of autoencoders with adversarial training to enforce a more structured latent space, improving the quality and diversity of generated molecules. This hybrid approach bridges the VAE and GAN paradigms, drawing on the complementary strengths of both frameworks. Hybrid VAE-diffusion models represent an emerging direction that integrates the continuous latent space of VAEs with the powerful denoising capabilities of diffusion models, potentially combining the sampling efficiency of VAEs with the high-fidelity generation of diffusion-based approaches—foreshadowing the diffusion model architectures discussed in subsequent sections.

**Challenges and Considerations**

Despite their strengths, VAE-based molecular generation faces several persistent challenges. Ensuring the chemical validity of decoded molecules from arbitrary latent space samples remains non-trivial, particularly for SMILES-based decoders where minor perturbations can yield invalid strings—an issue that equally affects the GAN-based approaches described in the previous section and motivates the use of alternative representations such as SELFIES and molecular graphs. The quality, validity, diversity, drug-likeness, synthesizability, and solubility of generated molecules must all be simultaneously considered [27], and no single VAE variant has yet fully maximized all these properties. The fidelity of property-guided optimization also depends critically on the quality and coverage of training data, as well as the expressiveness of the property predictors used to guide latent space exploration.

Overall, VAEs occupy a central role in the molecular generation landscape, providing a principled and flexible framework for exploring chemical space, optimizing lead compounds, and integrating multi-objective constraints. Their ability to produce smooth, navigable latent representations of chemical space makes them particularly well-suited for iterative lead optimization workflows, while their compatibility with graph-based and 3D molecular representations positions them as an indispensable complement to the broader suite of generative architectures—including the GAN-based and diffusion-based approaches covered across this section—in the modern computational drug discovery toolkit.

### 4.4 Generative Adversarial Networks for Drug Design

Generative adversarial networks (GANs) have emerged as a powerful paradigm for de novo molecular generation, leveraging the adversarial interplay between a generator that produces novel molecular structures and a discriminator that evaluates their chemical realism. The classical GAN framework, originally designed for image synthesis, was adapted to the molecular domain by operating on SMILES string representations, enabling the generation of syntactically valid chemical notations through sequence-based generators [40]. However, raw SMILES-based GANs suffer from the fundamental challenge that small perturbations in the latent space can produce chemically invalid strings, motivating the development of more structured generative frameworks—a limitation shared with other sequence-based approaches and one that underlies much of the subsequent architectural innovation described across this section.

A pivotal advance came with the introduction of ORGAN (Objective-Reinforced Generative Adversarial Network), which augments the standard GAN training objective with a reinforcement learning reward signal encoding desired molecular properties such as drug-likeness, synthetic accessibility, and target affinity [40]. By combining adversarial training with property-specific rewards, ORGAN allows directed exploration of chemical space toward pharmacologically relevant regions, addressing a key limitation of purely unsupervised generative approaches and foreshadowing the property-conditioned generation strategies that also underpin the VAE and normalizing flow architectures discussed in adjacent sections.

To overcome the expressiveness constraints of linear string representations, molecular graph GANs were developed that operate directly on graph-structured molecular representations [2]. In these architectures, both the generator and discriminator operate on atom-bond graphs, enabling the model to respect the intrinsic combinatorial structure of molecules and generate compounds with valid valence and connectivity—an approach that aligns with the broader shift toward graph-based molecular encodings motivated in the preceding discussion of molecular representations. Multi-property optimization frameworks further extended graph-based GANs by incorporating multiple discriminator heads or reward functions that simultaneously penalize undesirable pharmacokinetic profiles, toxicity, or poor synthetic accessibility, enabling constrained multi-objective molecular design [27].

Despite their promise, GAN-based molecular generation faces several well-documented training challenges. Mode collapse—wherein the generator learns to produce a narrow distribution of structurally similar molecules rather than exploring diverse chemical space—is a persistent issue that limits scaffold diversity and the novelty of generated candidates [2]. This same limitation reappears in the VAE and normalizing flow frameworks discussed in surrounding sections, underscoring that chemical diversity remains an open challenge across generative paradigms. Training instability arising from the adversarial minimax objective can cause generator and discriminator losses to oscillate without convergence, particularly when operating on discrete molecular graph structures where gradient signals are difficult to propagate. Techniques such as Wasserstein GAN objectives, gradient penalty regularization, and spectral normalization have been applied to stabilize training, though their effectiveness varies across molecular generation benchmarks [40].

Recent advances have explored quantum GANs and hybrid quantum-classical architectures as a frontier approach for molecular generation, motivated by the potential of quantum computing to represent and sample from exponentially large chemical spaces more efficiently than classical systems [2]. In these hybrid frameworks, quantum circuits serve as the generator component, parameterized by trainable rotation gates that produce quantum states encoding molecular features, while a classical neural network acts as the discriminator. Although currently limited to small molecular systems due to the qubit constraints of near-term quantum hardware, quantum GANs represent a promising direction for exploring chemical diversity in regimes where classical generative models face combinatorial barriers.

Overall, GANs have contributed substantially to the generative chemistry toolkit by enabling property-conditioned, multi-objective molecular generation across both string and graph representations [40; 2]. The adversarial training paradigm, while powerful, introduces trade-offs in training stability and mode diversity that have motivated the complementary approaches of VAEs—which offer a more stable optimization landscape through continuous latent space learning—and normalizing flows, which provide exact likelihood computation. Continued progress in stabilizing adversarial training, improving chemical validity guarantees, and integrating quantum computational resources will be essential to fully realize the potential of GAN-based approaches in practical drug discovery pipelines.

### 4.5 Normalizing Flows for Molecular Generation

Normalizing flows represent a distinct class of generative models that learn invertible transformations between a simple base distribution and a complex target distribution, enabling exact likelihood computation—a significant advantage over the variational autoencoders and generative adversarial networks discussed in preceding sections, which rely on approximate inference or adversarial training objectives respectively. In the context of molecular generation, this property allows for principled probabilistic modeling of chemical space and more reliable density estimation over molecular structures, complementing the strengths and addressing some of the limitations inherent to GAN-based approaches.

Autoregressive flow models have been among the most prominent normalizing flow architectures applied to molecular design. GraphAF, for instance, combines autoregressive generation with normalizing flows operating directly on molecular graphs, generating atoms and bonds sequentially while maintaining exact likelihood estimation throughout the generation process. This autoregressive factorization naturally enforces chemical validity constraints by conditioning each generation step on previously constructed molecular substructures, substantially reducing the proportion of chemically invalid outputs compared to one-shot generation approaches. This graph-based formulation echoes the motivation behind molecular graph GANs, but achieves chemical validity through architectural constraints rather than adversarial training dynamics.

Variational mixtures of normalizing flows extend the expressiveness of standard flow models by combining multiple flow components within a variational framework, enabling the model to capture multimodal distributions over chemical space and generate structurally diverse molecular libraries. The mixture formulation improves coverage of the chemical space, addressing the same mode collapse issues that were identified as a persistent challenge for GAN-based molecular generators, where the distribution of generated molecules can become overly concentrated around a narrow subset of structures.

A key practical advantage of normalizing flow models for molecular generation is their support for efficient parallel training, as the invertible architecture allows gradients to propagate directly through the exact log-likelihood objective without the need for stochastic approximations. Furthermore, by conditioning the flow on target property values, these models can be adapted for goal-directed generation, producing molecules with desired pharmacological profiles in a manner analogous to the property-conditioned generation pursued by ORGAN and graph-based GANs. Despite these strengths, normalizing flows for molecular generation face challenges in scaling to large and structurally complex molecules, as the requirement for invertible architectures constrains model expressiveness and can increase computational overhead compared to non-invertible generative approaches [27; 19].

The progression from GAN-based methods to normalizing flows, and onward to the sequence-based language model approaches discussed in the following section, reflects the broader evolution of deep generative modeling for drug discovery—each paradigm offering distinct trade-offs between chemical validity, diversity, computational tractability, and the capacity for property-conditioned generation.

### 4.6 Recurrent Neural Networks and Language Models for Molecule Generation

## 4.6 Sequence-Based and Language Model Approaches

Recurrent neural networks (RNNs) and language model-based approaches represent some of the earliest and most influential deep learning methods for de novo molecular generation, capitalizing on the sequential nature of SMILES string representations. Building upon the generative modeling paradigms introduced in preceding sections—including VAEs, GANs, and normalizing flows—these sequence-based methods offer a fundamentally different perspective by framing molecular generation as a sequence modeling problem analogous to natural language generation, where a model learns the statistical grammar of valid chemical structures from large molecular datasets and then samples novel sequences token by token.

The foundational idea behind RNN-based molecular generation is to train a character-level or token-level language model—typically implemented using long short-term memory (LSTM) networks or gated recurrent units (GRUs)—on large databases of SMILES strings to learn the syntactic rules and chemical patterns governing valid molecular structures [40]. Once trained, the model can be sampled to produce novel SMILES sequences that, with high probability, correspond to chemically valid and drug-like molecules. This approach offers significant advantages in terms of simplicity, scalability, and the ability to generate highly diverse molecular libraries. In contrast to graph-based generative models such as GraphAF or molecular graph GANs, which explicitly encode chemical validity through architectural constraints or adversarial training, RNN-based models rely on learned sequence statistics to implicitly capture the rules of chemical syntax.

A particularly impactful development within this paradigm is the use of transfer learning and fine-tuning strategies to steer generative models toward biologically relevant chemical spaces. In the transfer learning framework, a general-purpose RNN is first pretrained on a large, diverse corpus of drug-like molecules to capture broad chemical knowledge, and then fine-tuned on a smaller, focused dataset of molecules with desired biological activity or target specificity [40]. This strategy enables the model to rapidly adapt its generation distribution toward a focused chemical library while retaining the structural diversity and validity learned during pretraining—a crucial advantage in drug discovery settings where labeled data for specific targets is scarce.

Sequence-to-sequence architectures extend this framework by conditioning the generative process on input molecular structures or biological contexts. Such models can be trained to perform tasks such as scaffold hopping, bioisosteric replacement, and lead optimization, where an input molecule is transformed into an optimized analogue with improved properties. These conditional generation approaches leverage encoder-decoder architectures inspired by neural machine translation, treating molecular optimization as a form of molecular "translation" between chemical languages—a conceptual parallel to the property-conditioned generation strategies employed by ORGAN and graph-based GANs in earlier sections.

Beyond standard fine-tuning, curiosity-driven exploration methods have been proposed to overcome the tendency of RNN-based generators to concentrate on high-probability but structurally repetitive chemical regions. By incorporating intrinsic motivation signals or novelty bonuses into the training objective, these methods incentivize the model to explore underrepresented regions of chemical space, thereby improving molecular diversity in de novo drug design. This is particularly important for avoiding mode collapse—a common failure mode in generative models where the distribution of generated molecules becomes overly concentrated around a small subset of structures, mirroring the challenges identified for GAN-based molecular generators and normalizing flow mixture models in previous sections.

The deep learning methods survey landscape also encompasses the integration of RNN-based generation within broader drug discovery workflows [132]. Molecular representations used in these approaches, including canonical SMILES, SELFIES, and augmented SMILES with enumeration-based data augmentation, directly influence the quality, validity, and diversity of generated molecules. SELFIES (Self-Referencing Embedded Strings), in particular, has emerged as a robust alternative to SMILES by guaranteeing 100% chemical validity of generated strings through grammar-enforced constraints, addressing one of the key limitations of vanilla SMILES-based RNN generators and offering a complementary solution to the architectural validity constraints employed by graph-based flow models.

More recently, Transformer-based language models have substantially advanced the capabilities of sequence-based molecular generation. By replacing recurrent architectures with attention mechanisms that capture long-range dependencies within molecular sequences, Transformer models can model complex structural relationships more effectively than LSTMs, particularly for longer molecules or those with intricate ring systems. Large-scale pretraining of chemical language models on millions of SMILES strings has produced powerful molecular representations and generative priors that can be fine-tuned for a wide range of downstream generation and optimization tasks [133].

Overall, RNN-based and language model approaches for molecular generation constitute a mature and versatile class of methods in the de novo drug design toolkit.
