Their ability to leverage transfer learning, handle diverse chemical spaces through sequence modeling, and integrate with reinforcement learning reward signals for property optimization has established them as foundational components of modern computational drug discovery pipelines. While these sequence-based methods excel at generating chemically valid and diverse molecular libraries from one-dimensional string representations, the three-dimensional geometric fidelity required for structure-based drug design calls for more spatially aware generative frameworks—a gap addressed by the diffusion-based approaches for 3D molecular generation discussed in the following section.

### 4.7 Diffusion Models for 3D Molecular Generation

## 4.7 Diffusion Models for 3D Molecular Generation

Building upon the sequence-based and language model approaches discussed in the previous section, diffusion models represent a complementary and increasingly influential paradigm for molecular generation—one that operates not on linear string representations but directly in three-dimensional coordinate space. Diffusion models offer principled probabilistic frameworks that iteratively denoise random noise into structured molecular geometries, and their capacity to generate geometrically precise 3D structures makes them particularly well-suited for structure-based drug discovery tasks. Unlike earlier generative approaches such as VAEs and GANs, diffusion models define explicit forward and reverse Markov processes over continuous atomic coordinates, enabling high-fidelity generation of three-dimensional molecular structures with accurate bond lengths, angles, and torsional configurations.

A central architectural challenge in 3D molecular diffusion is ensuring that generated structures respect the physical symmetries of molecular systems. Molecules have no preferred orientation or position in space, meaning that a valid generative model must be equivariant under rotations, translations, and reflections—collectively described by the E(3) or SE(3) symmetry groups. To address this, researchers have developed E(3)-equivariant diffusion models that apply equivariant graph neural networks within the denoising network, ensuring that the learned score function transforms consistently with rigid-body transformations of the molecular frame. These equivariant architectures operate directly on atomic point clouds, diffusing both atomic coordinates and atom type embeddings jointly, enabling coherent joint generation of molecular topology and 3D geometry [41; 2].

Structure-based drug design has particularly benefited from conditional diffusion models that incorporate three-dimensional protein pocket information as a conditioning signal during the reverse diffusion process. Rather than generating molecules in isolation, these pocket-conditioned models learn to generate ligands that are geometrically and chemically complementary to the binding site of a target protein. Architectural innovations in this space include multi-level diffusion schemes that simultaneously operate at different molecular scales—atom-level coordinates, residue-level pocket descriptors, and molecular graph topology—allowing the model to capture hierarchical binding interactions between the ligand and the protein environment [41].

Latent diffusion models represent another important advancement, in which the diffusion process is performed in a compressed latent representation space rather than directly in atomic coordinate space. This approach dramatically reduces the dimensionality of the diffusion process, enabling faster training and inference while preserving the essential chemical and geometric properties of molecules. For structure-based generation, latent representations of protein pockets can be pre-computed and used to condition the latent diffusion process, significantly accelerating inference without requiring repeated protein structure encoding at every denoising step [2].

Score-based generative models, which learn the gradient of the log-probability density of molecular structures, provide a closely related framework to diffusion models and have been applied to both unconditional and conditional 3D molecular generation. Score matching objectives train a neural network to estimate the score function at multiple noise levels, enabling annealed Langevin dynamics sampling that progressively refines noisy molecular configurations into valid low-energy structures. When conditioned on protein binding pocket descriptors, score-based models can generate ligand poses that satisfy both geometric constraints imposed by the pocket shape and chemical valency rules [41].

An interesting direction for improving generalization in 3D molecular diffusion is zero-shot generation via similarity kernels, where the model leverages known molecular structures as soft conditioning signals through similarity-based kernels rather than requiring explicit property labels or binding pocket structures. This approach allows the diffusion model to generate molecules that share structural characteristics with a reference set without direct supervision, enabling exploration of chemically novel regions of 3D space that are nonetheless functionally related to known bioactive compounds.

The integration of protein language model embeddings into structure-based diffusion models offers another promising strategy for accelerating inference. By replacing computationally expensive multiple sequence alignment-based protein representations with fixed embeddings from large pre-trained protein language models, diffusion-based molecular generators can rapidly condition on protein target information at inference time without the need for costly structure retrieval or MSA computation, broadening the applicability of structure-based diffusion methods to targets with limited structural data [41; 2].

Benchmarking 3D diffusion models for drug design typically involves evaluating generated molecules on metrics including binding affinity estimates via molecular docking, drug-likeness (QED), synthetic accessibility (SA), geometric validity of 3D conformations, and diversity of generated structures [2]. Despite impressive progress, key challenges remain, including ensuring synthesizability of generated 3D structures, improving sampling efficiency for large and flexible binding pockets, and bridging the gap between computationally predicted binding poses and experimentally validated binding modes. The continued development of equivariant architectures, multi-level conditioning strategies, and latent diffusion frameworks is expected to further advance the capability of diffusion-based models as central tools in structure-based de novo drug design. These strengths in geometry-aware generation naturally complement the goal-directed optimization perspective offered by reinforcement learning methods, which we discuss in the following section.

### 4.8 Reinforcement Learning for Molecular Optimization

## 4.8 Reinforcement Learning for Molecular Optimization

Building upon the diffusion-based approaches for 3D molecular generation discussed in the previous section, reinforcement learning (RL) offers a complementary and powerful paradigm for goal-directed molecular generation and optimization. While diffusion models excel at generating geometrically precise 3D structures by learning the underlying distribution of molecular configurations, RL-based methods instead explicitly optimize molecular structures with respect to task-specific reward signals, enabling targeted exploration and exploitation of chemical space in pursuit of high-affinity binders, drug-like candidates, and molecules with favorable ADMET profiles. This distinction between distribution learning and reward-driven optimization makes RL particularly well-suited for scenarios where specific molecular objectives can be quantified and incorporated into a training signal.

### Policy Gradient Methods and Sequence-Based Optimization

Policy gradient methods represent one of the foundational RL approaches applied to molecular optimization. In these frameworks, a generative model—typically a recurrent neural network or transformer trained on SMILES or SELFIES representations—serves as the policy, with its parameters updated to maximize expected cumulative rewards. The REINFORCE algorithm and its variants have been widely employed to fine-tune pre-trained chemical language models toward specific molecular objectives. A notable example is the application of best-of-batch policy gradient methods to transformer architectures for molecular optimization [134], where the authors demonstrate that combining a custom context-free grammar for SMILES with additional masking logic to enforce chemical validity constraints, coupled with RL-based fine-tuning, achieves significant improvements over state-of-the-art baselines. This approach uses substantially fewer model steps per atom than earlier methods, enabling the generation of larger, more complex molecules while maintaining chemical validity [134]. The integration of grammar-based constraints with policy gradient optimization illustrates how domain knowledge can be incorporated into RL frameworks to ensure that the generated molecules remain chemically feasible throughout the optimization trajectory.

### Deep Q-Learning and Value-Based Approaches

Deep Q-learning and value-based RL methods offer complementary perspectives on molecular optimization by learning to estimate the expected future reward associated with taking specific molecular editing actions from a given state. In molecular graph-based formulations, the state corresponds to a partially constructed or existing molecular graph, while actions correspond to atomic or bond modifications such as atom substitution, bond addition or deletion, ring formation, or functional group attachment. The Q-network, parameterized by a graph neural network, learns to assign value estimates to state-action pairs, guiding the agent toward high-reward molecular configurations. These approaches are particularly well-suited for scaffold optimization and lead modification tasks, where a starting molecule is iteratively refined through a sequence of chemically meaningful edits. The discrete and combinatorial nature of molecular graph editing aligns naturally with the action space formulation of Q-learning, enabling systematic exploration of structurally related chemical neighborhoods around promising lead compounds.

### Actor-Critic Architectures for Molecular Design

Actor-critic methods combine the strengths of policy gradient and value-based approaches by simultaneously learning a policy (actor) that selects molecular editing actions and a value function (critic) that estimates the quality of the current molecular state. This architecture reduces the variance of policy gradient updates through the use of the learned baseline provided by the critic, leading to more stable and sample-efficient training. In the context of molecular optimization, actor-critic methods have been applied to both SMILES-based sequential generation and graph-based molecular editing, with the critic typically implemented as a graph neural network that processes the partial or complete molecular structure. The actor-critic framework naturally supports continuous and discrete action spaces, enabling the optimization of molecules with respect to multi-dimensional reward signals that capture binding affinity, synthetic accessibility, and physicochemical properties simultaneously.

### Multi-Objective Reward Design

A central challenge in applying RL to drug discovery is the design of reward functions that accurately capture the multi-faceted nature of drug-likeness. Effective drug candidates must simultaneously satisfy numerous criteria including high binding affinity to the target protein, favorable pharmacokinetic properties, low toxicity, acceptable solubility, and practical synthetic accessibility. Multi-objective reward design in RL-based molecular optimization typically involves constructing composite reward functions that combine individual property scores—such as QED for drug-likeness, synthetic accessibility scores, docking scores for binding affinity, and ADMET predictions—into a single scalar reward through weighted summation, geometric averaging, or Pareto-based multi-objective optimization strategies. The relative weighting of different objectives critically influences the exploration-exploitation trade-off and the distribution of generated molecules across the Pareto frontier of optimal property combinations. Adaptive reward shaping techniques, where reward weights are dynamically adjusted during training to encourage exploration of underrepresented regions of chemical space, have been explored to mitigate mode collapse and improve the diversity of optimized molecular populations.

### Curriculum Learning in RL-Based Optimization

Curriculum learning strategies have been incorporated into RL-based molecular optimization frameworks to address the challenge of sparse rewards and difficult credit assignment in the molecular design problem. By progressively increasing the difficulty of the optimization task—starting with simpler property objectives or more lenient reward thresholds and gradually introducing more stringent requirements—curriculum learning enables the RL agent to develop a foundational understanding of chemical space structure before tackling the most challenging optimization objectives. This progressive training paradigm helps avoid the common failure mode where the agent receives uniformly low rewards throughout training, preventing effective learning of productive molecular editing strategies. Curriculum learning has been particularly beneficial in multi-step molecular generation tasks where the reward signal is only received upon completion of a full molecular structure, making it difficult for the agent to learn which intermediate actions contributed to the final outcome.

### GFlowNet-Based Methods for Diverse Molecular Generation

Generative Flow Networks (GFlowNets) represent a particularly promising recent development in RL-based molecular optimization that addresses a fundamental limitation of traditional RL approaches: the tendency to focus excessively on a narrow set of high-reward molecules at the expense of chemical diversity. GFlowNets are trained to generate molecular structures with probabilities proportional to their associated reward values, rather than deterministically maximizing reward. This probabilistic formulation naturally encourages the exploration of diverse high-reward molecular configurations, producing a distribution over molecules that is rich in both quality and variety—a critical requirement for practical drug discovery applications where chemical diversity among lead candidates is essential for downstream development. The GFlowNet framework has been applied to fragment-based molecular generation, where molecules are constructed by sequentially adding molecular fragments, with the flow network learning to assign appropriate generation probabilities to fragment attachment actions based on the expected downstream reward. This approach enables the systematic exploration of synthesizable chemical spaces by constraining the fragment vocabulary to chemically accessible building blocks, thereby ensuring that generated molecules can be practically synthesized. The connection between GFlowNets and active learning frameworks further enables the integration of experimental feedback into iterative molecular design loops, where newly acquired experimental data is used to refine reward models and guide subsequent rounds of molecule generation.

### Integration with Synthesizability Constraints

A critical practical consideration in RL-based molecular optimization is ensuring that generated molecules are not only computationally predicted to have desirable properties but can also be practically synthesized in the laboratory. Reaction-based RL approaches address this challenge by defining the molecular action space in terms of known chemical reactions and commercially available building blocks, ensuring that all generated molecules correspond to feasible synthetic routes. By incorporating synthetic accessibility scores, retrosynthesis prediction models, or explicit reaction-based generation mechanisms into the reward function or action space definition, RL frameworks can be constrained to explore only synthetically accessible regions of chemical space. This integration of synthesizability constraints with property optimization represents an important advance toward practically useful RL-based molecular design, bridging the gap between computational generation and experimental validation in drug discovery workflows. As we will see in the following section, this concern for synthesizability is equally central to fragment-based and 3D structure-based generative methods, which address it through a complementary strategy of anchoring generation to known chemical fragments and protein binding pocket geometry.

### Navigating Chemical Space with RL

The combination of policy gradient methods, deep Q-learning, actor-critic architectures, carefully designed multi-objective reward functions, curriculum learning strategies, and GFlowNet-based generative models provides a comprehensive toolkit for RL-based molecular optimization. These approaches collectively enable the systematic navigation of vast chemical spaces toward novel, synthesizable drug candidates with optimized binding affinity, drug-likeness, and ADMET profiles. The flexibility of the RL framework in accommodating diverse molecular representations—including SMILES strings [134], molecular graphs, and fragment-based representations—and its natural compatibility with iterative experimental feedback loops position RL as a cornerstone methodology in AI-driven drug discovery pipelines. However, a key limitation shared by many RL-based approaches is that optimization proceeds primarily in abstract chemical space, without explicit consideration of the three-dimensional binding geometry that ultimately governs molecular recognition. This gap motivates the fragment-based and 3D structure-based generative methods discussed in the following section, which directly incorporate protein pocket geometry into the molecular design process to produce candidates with inherently improved structural complementarity to the target binding site.

### 4.9 Fragment-Based and 3D Structure-Based Generative Methods

## 4.9 Fragment-Based and 3D Structure-Based Generative Methods

Fragment-based and 3D structure-based generative methods represent a compelling paradigm in computational drug design, wherein molecular generation is explicitly guided by the three-dimensional geometry of protein binding pockets rather than relying solely on ligand-space exploration. These approaches address a fundamental limitation of the reinforcement learning and purely ligand-centric generative models discussed in the previous section: generated molecules may be chemically diverse and drug-like in isolation, yet fail to complement the spatial, electrostatic, and steric requirements of the target binding site. By anchoring generation to protein structure, fragment-based and 3D structure-based methods aim to produce molecules with inherently improved binding affinity, selectivity, and specificity—properties that are difficult to encode exclusively through reward functions operating in abstract chemical space.

### Fragment-Based Molecular Generation

Fragment-based drug discovery (FBDD) has long been a productive experimental strategy in medicinal chemistry, and its computational analog has been progressively empowered by deep learning. In computational fragment-based generation, molecules are assembled incrementally—either atom-by-atom or fragment-by-fragment—within or around a defined protein pocket. This incremental construction allows the model to make locally informed decisions at each growth step, respecting geometric constraints imposed by the surrounding protein environment.

Early fragment-based computational approaches relied on docking fragments from curated libraries and then linking or merging them using classical cheminformatics rules. Modern deep learning methods have substantially advanced this paradigm by learning the statistical patterns of fragment assembly from large datasets of protein-ligand complexes. Graph-based autoregressive models, for instance, treat molecule construction as a sequential decision process: at each step, a new atom or fragment is appended to the growing molecular scaffold, conditioned on the current partial structure and the protein pocket representation. This conditioning ensures that each appended unit is geometrically and chemically compatible with the binding environment.

Scaffold-based design represents a closely related strategy, where a core molecular scaffold—identified from known actives or fragment screens—is retained and elaborated through R-group optimization or linker design. R-group optimization approaches explore the chemical space around a fixed scaffold by varying substituents at designated attachment points, optimizing for binding affinity, selectivity, and ADMET properties simultaneously. Deep generative models trained on matched molecular pair datasets or structure-activity relationship (SAR) tables can learn the mapping from scaffold-plus-context to optimal R-groups, dramatically accelerating lead optimization campaigns.

### 3D Structure-Based Generative Models

The availability of high-resolution protein-ligand crystal structures and, more recently, AlphaFold-predicted protein structures has catalyzed the development of sophisticated 3D structure-based generative models. These methods operate directly in three-dimensional Cartesian space, generating molecular graphs or point clouds that are spatially positioned within protein binding pockets.

Conditional variational autoencoders (CVAEs) were among the first deep generative architectures adapted for structure-based design. These models encode protein pocket geometry using voxelized 3D grids or graph representations, conditioning the ligand generation decoder on the pocket embedding. The decoder then samples ligand structures from a learned latent distribution conditioned on pocket features, enabling pocket-specific molecule generation.

Autoregressive atom-by-atom generation methods guided by crystallographic pocket data construct molecules sequentially, placing each atom at a 3D coordinate determined by both the partial molecular structure and the pocket environment. These models must simultaneously learn chemical validity constraints (bonding rules, valence requirements) and geometric feasibility (avoiding steric clashes, maintaining reasonable bond lengths and angles). Graph neural networks processing the protein pocket as a heterogeneous graph of amino acid residues and atoms provide rich contextual features that guide each placement decision.

### Diffusion Models for Structure-Based Generation

Diffusion-based generative models have emerged as particularly powerful frameworks for 3D structure-based molecular generation. SE(3)-equivariant diffusion models treat molecule generation as a reverse diffusion process in the joint space of atomic positions and identities, ensuring that generated structures are invariant to global rotations and translations—a critical physical symmetry for molecular modeling. Models such as DiffSBDD learn to denoise randomly perturbed ligand configurations conditioned on the protein pocket, producing fully three-dimensional molecular structures with explicit atomic coordinates.

Shape-conditioned generation represents another important direction, wherein the generative model is conditioned not only on the protein pocket but also on a desired three-dimensional shape or pharmacophore hypothesis. This allows medicinal chemists to encode prior knowledge about the binding mode—such as the requirement to mimic a known pharmacophore or to occupy a specific subpocket—directly into the generation process.

Controllable decomposed diffusion models further extend this paradigm by decomposing the molecular generation process into interpretable sub-problems: generating the molecular scaffold, then iteratively adding fragments or substituents, each step guided by both the protein pocket and previously generated molecular components. This decomposition improves controllability, allowing users to fix certain structural features while optimizing others, and facilitates integration with medicinal chemistry intuition.

### Fragment Linking, Growing, and Merging

Beyond atom-by-atom construction, fragment-based deep generative methods encompass fragment linking (connecting two or more fragments with an appropriate linker), fragment growing (extending a fragment from an attachment point), and fragment merging (combining overlapping fragments into a unified scaffold). Each of these tasks presents unique geometric and chemical challenges. Fragment linking, for example, requires the model to generate a linker of appropriate length and geometry that bridges two fragments positioned in the pocket without introducing steric clashes or unfavorable torsion angles.

Graph-based editing approaches, such as MolEdit3D-style methods, formulate structure-based molecular optimization as a graph editing problem: given an initial molecule docked into a pocket, the model learns to perform local edits—adding, removing, or modifying atoms and bonds—to improve binding affinity or other properties while maintaining pocket complementarity. This iterative refinement strategy bridges generation and optimization, enabling fine-grained control over the molecular modification process.

### In-Pocket 3D Graph Architectures

In-pocket 3D graph architectures explicitly encode the protein-ligand interaction interface as a unified molecular graph, where both protein atoms (or residues) and ligand atoms are nodes, and edges encode spatial proximity, hydrogen bonding, hydrophobic contacts, and other interaction types. Message passing over this joint graph allows the model to learn interaction patterns that correlate with binding affinity and selectivity. Generative models built on such architectures can propose new ligands by conditioning generation on the protein subgraph, effectively learning the chemical grammar of productive protein-ligand interactions from structural databases.

### Synthesizability and Practical Considerations

A persistent challenge in fragment-based and structure-based generative methods is ensuring that generated molecules are not only computationally predicted to bind well but are also synthetically accessible—a concern that is directly complementary to the synthesizability constraints discussed in the context of RL-based optimization. Fragment-based approaches have an inherent advantage here, as molecules assembled from validated synthetic fragments via known chemical transformations (such as amide bond formation, Suzuki coupling, or reductive amination) are more likely to be synthetically tractable than molecules generated without such constraints. Incorporating reaction-based constraints into fragment assembly—by limiting growth steps to those that correspond to known chemical reactions—substantially improves the synthetic feasibility of generated candidates and facilitates downstream experimental validation.

The integration of fragment-based and 3D structure-based generative models with computational docking, molecular dynamics simulations, and experimental fragment screening data represents the current frontier of structure-guided drug design. As protein structure prediction methods continue to mature and large-scale structural databases expand, these generative approaches are poised to become central tools in the rational design of novel therapeutics against both well-characterized and previously undruggable targets. The multi-objective optimization challenges inherent to this domain—balancing structural complementarity with the binding pocket against drug-likeness, synthesizability, and ADMET properties—motivate the comprehensive multi-objective frameworks and active learning integration strategies discussed in the following section.

### 4.10 Multi-Objective Optimization and Synthesizability in Generative Models

### 4.10 Multi-Objective Optimization and Synthesizability in Generative Models

Building directly on the fragment-based and 3D structure-based generative methods explored in the preceding section, it becomes clear that structural complementarity with the binding pocket is a necessary but insufficient condition for practical drug candidates. One of the most critical challenges in de novo drug design is that generative models must simultaneously satisfy multiple, often competing objectives: maximizing binding affinity against a specific target, maintaining favorable drug-likeness (QED), ensuring synthetic accessibility, and exhibiting acceptable ADMET (absorption, distribution, metabolism, excretion, and toxicity) profiles. Optimizing any single objective in isolation frequently produces molecules that fail along other dimensions—a compound with excellent predicted binding affinity may be entirely unsynthesizable or metabolically unstable, rendering it useless in practice. This section discusses the strategies and frameworks that have emerged to address multi-objective molecular optimization while ensuring the practical chemical feasibility of generated candidates, laying the groundwork for the benchmarking and evaluation considerations addressed in the following section.

**Multi-Objective Reward Design and Scalarization**

Early approaches to multi-objective molecular optimization relied on scalarization, where individual objectives are combined into a single scalar reward using weighted sums or product formulations. Reinforcement learning (RL) agents, for instance, are commonly trained with composite reward functions that balance binding affinity scores, QED, and synthetic accessibility (SA) scores computed via tools such as RDKit. While scalarization is computationally straightforward, it requires careful manual tuning of objective weights and can fail to capture the full Pareto frontier of trade-offs between competing properties. More sophisticated multi-objective RL frameworks employ Pareto-based reward shaping, multi-objective Bayesian optimization, or decomposition-based approaches inspired by evolutionary algorithms such as NSGA-II and MOEA/D to explore diverse regions of the Pareto-optimal solution space.

**Synthesizability-Constrained Generation**

A fundamental limitation of many generative models—including the atom-by-atom and fragment-based 3D methods discussed in Section 4.9—is that they operate in a latent molecular space without explicit chemical reaction constraints, producing molecules that are structurally novel but practically unsynthesizable. Synthesizability-constrained generation addresses this by embedding forward synthesis frameworks directly into the generative process. One prominent approach represents molecular generation as a sequence of chemical reactions, where each step applies a known reaction template to existing building blocks drawn from commercially available compound libraries. This reaction-based generative paradigm inherently constrains the chemical space to synthetically accessible regions. Reaction-based reinforcement learning frameworks explicitly model molecular construction as a Markov decision process over reaction steps, where the agent selects reaction types and reactants at each step, ensuring that the final generated molecule possesses a well-defined synthetic route. Such approaches have demonstrated that molecules generated via reaction-based pathways are significantly more amenable to experimental synthesis than those produced by unconstrained atom-by-atom or bond-by-bond generative models.

Fragment-based generative methods offer an alternative route to synthesizability by constructing molecules from validated molecular building blocks or fragments with known synthetic accessibility—directly extending the fragment assembly strategies described in Section 4.9. By restricting generation to combinations of chemically feasible fragments and linkers, fragment-based approaches implicitly encode synthesizability constraints into the generation process. These methods can be further combined with retrosynthetic analysis tools to score and filter generated candidates based on their predicted synthetic feasibility, creating a tighter feedback loop between generation and synthesizability assessment.

**Active Learning Integration**

Active learning provides a powerful mechanism for efficiently navigating the multi-objective optimization landscape by iteratively selecting the most informative molecules for experimental or computational evaluation and using the resulting feedback to update the generative model. In the context of multi-objective drug design, active learning frameworks prioritize candidates that are predicted to improve simultaneously across all objectives, effectively guiding the generative model toward regions of chemical space that are both promising and underexplored. Bayesian optimization, which naturally models uncertainty over objective functions, is particularly well-suited to active learning in multi-objective settings, as it enables principled exploration-exploitation trade-offs when evaluating expensive oracle functions such as molecular docking or ADMET prediction.

Deep generative models integrated with active learning loops—sometimes referred to as closed-loop or model-in-the-loop design cycles—have shown considerable promise in early drug discovery campaigns. In these frameworks, a generative model proposes candidate molecules, a property prediction oracle evaluates their multi-objective scores, and the resulting labeled data is used to retrain or fine-tune both the generative model and the property predictors. This iterative cycle progressively improves the quality and diversity of generated candidates while reducing the number of expensive evaluations required.

**Balancing Diversity and Constraint Satisfaction**

An inherent tension in multi-objective generative modeling is the trade-off between chemical diversity and constraint satisfaction. Highly constrained generation—particularly when enforcing synthesizability via reaction-based frameworks—may limit the diversity of produced molecules, concentrating the search around familiar chemical scaffolds. Strategies to mitigate this include diversity-promoting regularization terms in the generative objective, curiosity-driven exploration bonuses in RL frameworks, and GFlowNet-based methods that sample from distributions proportional to multi-objective rewards, enabling proportional exploration of the entire space of high-reward molecules rather than converging to a single mode. As will be discussed in Section 4.11, this tension between diversity and constraint satisfaction also poses significant challenges for benchmarking, since standard evaluation metrics must capture both the quality and the breadth of the Pareto-optimal solutions found.

**Evaluation and Benchmarking**

Evaluating multi-objective generative models requires metrics that capture both the quality and diversity of the Pareto-optimal solutions found. Standard benchmarks such as GuacaMol and MOSES provide multi-objective optimization tasks with predefined target profiles, allowing systematic comparison of generative approaches. However, as elaborated in the following section, these benchmarks often inadequately capture synthesizability constraints, underscoring the need for richer evaluation frameworks that incorporate retrosynthetic feasibility scores alongside traditional property-based metrics such as QED, SA score, and docking affinity.

In summary, multi-objective optimization and synthesizability represent two of the most practically important and technically challenging frontiers in molecular generative modeling. Progress in this area requires tight integration of reaction-aware generation, multi-objective optimization algorithms, active learning feedback loops, and robust evaluation frameworks that reflect the real-world constraints of pharmaceutical synthesis and development—challenges that motivate the comprehensive benchmarking discussion in the section that follows.

### 4.11 Benchmarking, Evaluation, and Challenges in Molecular Generation

### 4.11 Benchmarking, Evaluation, and Challenges in Molecular Generation

Building on the multi-objective optimization and synthesizability frameworks discussed in the preceding section, rigorous benchmarking and standardized evaluation are essential for measuring progress in molecular generative modeling and enabling fair comparisons across diverse architectures and training strategies. The field has converged on a core set of quantitative metrics that collectively assess the quality, diversity, and drug-likeness of generated molecular libraries. These include **validity** (the fraction of generated strings or graphs that correspond to chemically valid molecules), **uniqueness** (the fraction of valid molecules that are non-duplicate), and **novelty** (the fraction of valid, unique molecules not present in the training set). Beyond these basic measures, the **Fréchet ChemNet Distance (FCD)** has been widely adopted as a distribution-level metric that compares the statistical properties of generated molecules against reference datasets in the latent space of a pretrained neural network, providing a more holistic measure of chemical realism. Additional property-based metrics such as the **Quantitative Estimate of Drug-likeness (QED)**, synthetic accessibility scores (SAS), and **molecular docking scores** are used to assess the pharmaceutical relevance and binding potential of generated candidates, particularly in structure-based generation tasks. Critically, these metrics also serve as the very objectives that multi-objective optimization frameworks—discussed in Section 4.10—seek to jointly maximize, underscoring the deep connection between evaluation methodology and generative model design.

Several benchmarking platforms and datasets have been established to facilitate systematic comparisons. The **MOSES** (Molecular Sets) benchmark provides standardized train and test splits derived from ZINC and ChEMBL, alongside a comprehensive suite of metrics covering distribution learning, property optimization, and conditional generation. **GuacaMol** similarly offers a suite of goal-directed and distribution-learning benchmarks, testing models on their ability to optimize multiple drug-like objectives simultaneously. Notably, both MOSES and GuacaMol provide the multi-objective optimization tasks referenced in Section 4.10, serving as practical testbeds for evaluating the Pareto-optimal generation strategies and reward-shaping approaches described there. For structure-based drug design, the **CrossDocked2020** dataset is widely used to evaluate pocket-conditioned generative models, providing protein-ligand complexes with associated docking scores. The **QM9** dataset serves as a reference for evaluating 3D molecular generative models on quantum chemical properties, supporting comparisons of generated geometry quality [135; 136].

Despite these efforts, significant open challenges remain. **Chemical diversity** is a persistent concern: many generative models tend to produce molecules that cluster around the training distribution or seed compounds, limiting their ability to explore underrepresented regions of chemical space. This is compounded by the challenge of **out-of-distribution (OOD) generalization**, where models trained on known bioactive compounds may fail to generate structurally novel scaffolds with desired biological activity. The problem is particularly acute for structure-based generative models conditioned on protein pockets, where the diversity of generated ligands is inherently constrained by the pocket geometry. These diversity limitations also directly affect the practical utility of multi-objective optimization pipelines, as a lack of scaffold diversity can cause even sophisticated Pareto-based methods to converge prematurely.

**Bias in training and seed molecules** presents another fundamental challenge. Training datasets such as ZINC and ChEMBL reflect historical biases in medicinal chemistry, overrepresenting certain chemical scaffolds, heteroatom distributions, and molecular weight ranges. Generative models trained on such data may inadvertently reproduce these biases, limiting their utility for genuinely novel drug discovery campaigns. Similarly, reinforcement learning-based optimization approaches are sensitive to the choice of seed or starting molecules, and may converge to narrow regions of chemical space through reward hacking—a failure mode that is especially problematic in the multi-objective RL frameworks discussed in Section 4.10.

**Reproducibility** is a significant concern in the field, as many reported results are sensitive to implementation details, random seeds, dataset preprocessing choices, and hyperparameter settings. The lack of standardized open-source implementations and consistent evaluation protocols makes direct comparison across publications difficult. Efforts such as MOSES and GuacaMol have partially addressed this by providing reference implementations and fixed evaluation pipelines, but variability in how models are trained and evaluated persists.

Perhaps the most critical challenge is the **gap between computational generation and experimental validation**. Metrics such as QED, SAS, and even docking scores are imperfect proxies for actual biological activity, and molecules that score well in silico frequently fail in experimental assays due to issues of synthesis feasibility, off-target effects, metabolic instability, and cellular permeability. This disconnect directly motivates the synthesizability-constrained generation and active learning integration strategies outlined in Section 4.10, but closing this validation gap remains an open and active research frontier. Addressing these benchmarking and evaluation challenges—from standardizing diversity metrics to better capturing synthesizability in benchmark tasks—will be essential for translating the impressive in silico capabilities of molecular generative models into tangible advances in drug discovery.

## 5 Drug-Target Interaction, Drug-Drug Interaction, and Knowledge Graph Reasoning

### 5.1 Drug-Target Interaction Prediction: Foundations and Graph-Based Methods

## 5.1 Drug-Target Interaction Prediction: Foundations and Graph-Based Methods

Drug-target interaction (DTI) prediction is a cornerstone problem in computational drug discovery, aiming to determine whether a given small molecule drug binds to a specific biological target protein. Accurately identifying such interactions is critical for hit identification, lead optimization, and drug repurposing, as the interaction between a drug and its target largely determines therapeutic efficacy and potential off-target effects. Given the vast combinatorial space of possible drug-target pairs and the high cost of experimental validation, computational approaches have become indispensable for prioritizing candidates before committing to wet-lab assays [36]. While this section focuses on binary interaction prediction—determining whether binding occurs—the following section extends this foundation to the quantitative task of predicting binding affinity values, forming together a comprehensive treatment of drug-target binding prediction within this survey.

### Foundational Approaches

Early computational DTI methods relied primarily on the assumption that similar drugs tend to bind similar targets. Similarity-based approaches leverage chemical fingerprint similarity between molecules and sequence or structural similarity between proteins to infer interactions by analogy with known drug-target pairs. These ligand-based and target-based similarity kernels were among the earliest systematic tools to scale DTI prediction beyond manual curation, enabling the exploration of large interaction matrices at modest computational cost.

Matrix factorization methods represent a natural extension, decomposing the observed drug-target interaction matrix into low-dimensional latent factor representations for both drugs and targets. By learning compact embeddings that reconstruct known interactions, these approaches can generalize to unobserved pairs through inner product scoring or bilinear models. Regularized least squares and probabilistic matrix factorization variants demonstrated that incorporating multiple heterogeneous similarity measures—chemical, genomic, phenotypic—could substantially improve prediction accuracy over single-modality baselines.

Random walk techniques on heterogeneous biological networks offered another foundational paradigm. By propagating information across drug-drug similarity networks, target-target similarity networks, and the known drug-target bipartite graph simultaneously, random walk methods capture indirect associations and multi-hop relational paths that are invisible to pairwise similarity metrics alone. These approaches were among the first to explicitly leverage the network topology of the pharmacological landscape for DTI inference, foreshadowing the graph-based deep learning methods that followed.

### Graph Neural Network Architectures for DTI

The advent of deep learning and, specifically, graph neural networks (GNNs) marked a transformative shift in DTI prediction methodology. Molecules and proteins both possess inherent graph-structured representations—atoms and bonds for small molecules, residue contact maps or interaction networks for proteins—making GNNs a natural architectural choice for capturing their structural and functional properties.

Graph convolutional networks applied to molecular graphs can learn hierarchical atom-level representations by aggregating neighborhood information across successive message-passing layers, producing molecular embeddings that encode rich chemical context without relying on hand-crafted fingerprints. When combined with sequence-based or structure-based protein encoders, these molecular GNN representations enable end-to-end trainable DTI prediction models that jointly optimize drug and target feature extraction alongside interaction scoring.

Graph attention networks (GATs) extend this framework by assigning learned attention weights to neighboring nodes during message passing, allowing the model to selectively emphasize chemically or biologically relevant structural motifs. In the DTI context, attention mechanisms have proven particularly valuable because they provide a degree of interpretability, highlighting which molecular fragments or protein residues are most predictive of the interaction, thereby supporting mechanistic understanding alongside predictive accuracy [67].

Heterogeneous graph models represent a further advance by constructing unified graphs that jointly encode drugs, targets, diseases, and pathways as distinct node types, with multiple edge types capturing diverse biological relationships such as drug-drug similarity, protein-protein interactions, and known drug-target associations. By operating directly on these heterogeneous biological knowledge networks, such models can leverage relational context far beyond what is available from molecular structure or sequence alone [36]. Message-passing over heterogeneous graphs enables the propagation of biological signals across entity types, allowing the model to implicitly integrate genomic, proteomic, and pharmacological information within a single unified representation learning framework.

### Generalization to Novel Drugs and Targets

A persistent challenge in DTI prediction is generalization to novel entities—drugs or targets not present during training—commonly referred to as the cold-start problem. Similarity-based and matrix factorization methods struggle in this regime because they rely heavily on observed interaction patterns for the entities of interest. Graph neural network architectures partially address this challenge by learning inductive representations grounded in molecular structure or protein sequence, which can be computed for entirely new entities without requiring any prior interaction data.

Nonetheless, true out-of-distribution generalization remains difficult, particularly when novel drugs occupy sparsely populated regions of chemical space or when novel targets lack close homologs with well-characterized interaction profiles [3]. Strategies such as transfer learning from related tasks, incorporation of protein language model embeddings pre-trained on large protein sequence databases, and meta-learning approaches that enable rapid adaptation from few labeled interactions have been explored to address these limitations. The integration of multi-omics and clinical data into DTI frameworks further enriches the feature landscape, providing biological context that supports more robust generalization across diverse drug and target spaces. As discussed in the following section, these same cold-start challenges resurface in the quantitative setting of binding affinity prediction, where analogous transfer learning and pre-training strategies have been developed to improve generalization across novel drugs and targets.

Collectively, the evolution from early similarity-based heuristics through matrix factorization and random walk methods to sophisticated graph attention and heterogeneous graph neural network architectures reflects a broader trend toward increasingly expressive, data-driven, and biologically grounded representations for DTI prediction, with each generation of methods building upon and surpassing the limitations of its predecessors.

### 5.2 Drug-Target Binding Affinity Prediction

## 5.2 Drug-Target Binding Affinity Prediction

Drug-target binding affinity (DTA) prediction is a fundamental quantitative task in computational drug discovery, aiming to estimate the strength of interaction between a small molecule and a protein target. Building directly upon the binary interaction prediction frameworks introduced in the preceding subsection, DTA models extend the problem to a regression setting, learning to predict continuous affinity values—such as IC50, Kd, or Ki—from molecular and protein representations. This quantitative granularity provides more actionable information for drug screening and lead optimization than binary interaction labels alone, making DTA prediction an indispensable complement to the DTI methods already discussed [19]. As will become apparent, many of the architectural innovations developed for DTA—including multi-modal fusion, attention mechanisms, and transfer learning strategies—also inform the broader family of drug-target and drug-drug interaction models addressed in subsequent subsections.

### CNN and Sequence-Based Approaches

Early deep learning approaches to DTA prediction adopted sequence-based representations, encoding drug molecules as SMILES strings and proteins as amino acid sequences. DeepDTA, one of the landmark models in this category, employed parallel convolutional neural network (CNN) blocks to independently learn representations from drug SMILES and protein sequences, subsequently concatenating these embeddings for affinity regression. This architecture demonstrated that end-to-end learning from raw sequence data could outperform traditional descriptor-based methods on benchmark datasets such as Davis and KIBA, establishing foundational evaluation protocols for the field [41].

### Recurrent-Convolutional Hybrid Models

Models like DeepAffinity extended the sequence-based paradigm by combining recurrent neural networks with convolutional architectures to capture both local motif patterns and long-range sequential dependencies within protein and drug sequences. By leveraging LSTM layers alongside CNN modules, such hybrid models improved the modeling of structural contexts that are critical for binding affinity estimation, particularly for proteins with complex secondary and tertiary structural motifs. These approaches highlighted the importance of contextual encoding in sequence representations for quantitative DTA tasks, and established a direct conceptual bridge toward the graph-based architectures that would soon supersede purely sequential encoders [19].

### Graph-Based and Graph-in-Graph Architectures

With the rise of graph neural networks (GNNs)—already demonstrated as powerful tools for binary DTI prediction in the preceding subsection—researchers began formulating drug molecules as molecular graphs for DTA, enabling models to capture topological and chemical features at the atom and bond level. Graph-in-graph (GiG) architectures, such as GEFA, pushed this paradigm further by representing both the ligand as a molecular graph and the protein binding pocket as a residue-level graph, then modeling their interactions through a higher-order graph that encodes inter-graph relationships. This nested graph representation enabled more expressive modeling of protein-ligand contacts and improved binding affinity prediction, particularly in scenarios where structural pocket information was available [41].

### Bilinear Attention and Multi-Modal Fusion

Bilinear attention mechanisms have emerged as a powerful strategy for modeling the pairwise interactions between drug and protein representations. DrugBAN exemplifies this approach by employing bilinear attention networks (BANs) to jointly attend over drug molecular graph features and protein sequence features, generating interaction maps that capture fine-grained correspondence between specific molecular substructures and protein residues. This not only improves affinity prediction accuracy but also provides interpretable attention patterns that highlight functionally relevant drug-protein interaction sites, supporting structure-activity relationship elucidation and facilitating medicinal chemistry insights [78]. These bilinear attention frameworks foreshadow the broader family of multi-modal fusion and cross-modal attention architectures examined in detail in Section 5.3.

### Distance-Aware and Geometry-Informed Graph Attention

Subsequent advances incorporated geometric and spatial information into graph-based DTA models. Distance-aware graph attention networks leverage interatomic distance information, either from crystal structures or predicted conformations, to augment standard message-passing schemes with physically meaningful spatial priors. By encoding distance-dependent attention weights between protein residue nodes or between drug-protein atom pairs, these models improve the fidelity of the learned representations to the underlying physical chemistry of binding interactions. Such geometry-informed approaches are particularly valuable when high-quality structural data is available, bridging the gap between structure-based and sequence-based DTA modeling [41].

### Hierarchical Graph Representation Learning

Hierarchical graph representation learning methods address the multi-scale nature of drug-protein binding by constructing representations at multiple levels of granularity—from atom-level features to fragment-level motifs for drugs, and from residue-level encodings to domain-level structural units for proteins. By aggregating information across these hierarchical levels, such architectures capture both local chemical environments and global structural contexts that collectively govern binding affinity. This multi-scale approach has demonstrated improved performance on diverse benchmark datasets and enhanced generalization to structurally distinct protein families [19].

### Benchmark Datasets and Evaluation Metrics

Standard benchmarking in DTA prediction relies on datasets such as Davis (kinase inhibitor affinities measured as Kd), KIBA (integrated bioactivity scores), and PDBbind (experimentally determined binding affinities from crystal structures). Evaluation metrics commonly include the concordance index (CI), mean squared error (MSE), and Pearson or Spearman correlation coefficients between predicted and experimental affinity values. The CASF benchmark, derived from PDBbind, provides standardized scoring, ranking, and docking power evaluations, enabling systematic comparison of structure-based affinity prediction models under controlled conditions [41].

### Addressing Cold-Start Problems via Transfer Learning

A persistent challenge in DTA prediction—directly analogous to the cold-start problem identified for binary DTI prediction in Section 5.1—is the need to generalize to novel drugs or protein targets with no prior interaction data. Transfer learning strategies have been developed to mitigate this issue by pre-training model components on related tasks with abundant data, such as protein-protein interaction (PPI) prediction or chemical-chemical similarity tasks, before fine-tuning on DTA datasets. Pre-training protein encoders on large-scale PPI networks helps initialize representations that capture biologically meaningful protein features, while pre-training drug encoders on molecular property datasets or chemical interaction data provides generalizable molecular embeddings. These transfer learning approaches significantly improve generalization in low-data regimes and for structurally dissimilar target proteins not well-represented in training data, making them critical for real-world applicability of DTA models [19; 41].

### Integration with Protein Structure Prediction

The availability of high-quality predicted protein structures from models such as AlphaFold2 has opened new opportunities for structure-aware DTA prediction, enabling graph-based and geometry-informed models to operate even in the absence of experimental crystal structures. By incorporating predicted structural information into drug-protein interaction graphs, DTA models can now access three-dimensional pocket geometries for a vastly larger set of protein targets than was previously feasible, expanding the scope of quantitative affinity prediction to previously undruggable or structurally uncharacterized proteins [41].

Overall, the field of DTA prediction has evolved from simple sequence-based CNN models toward sophisticated multi-modal, geometry-aware, and hierarchical deep learning architectures that achieve strong predictive performance on established benchmarks while addressing key challenges of interpretability, generalization, and cold-start prediction for novel drugs and targets. The attention-based and multi-modal fusion strategies that have proven most effective in this quantitative setting are examined in greater depth in the following subsection, where they are developed into unified frameworks that integrate heterogeneous molecular and protein representations for both DTI and DTA prediction.

### 5.3 Multi-Modal Fusion and Attention Mechanisms for DTI and DTA

## 5.3 Multi-Modal Fusion and Attention Mechanisms for DTI and DTA

Building upon the sequence-based, graph-based, and geometry-informed architectures surveyed in the preceding subsection on DTA prediction, a natural evolution in the field has been the development of multi-modal and attention-based frameworks that integrate heterogeneous molecular and protein representations within unified architectures. A central challenge in both drug-target interaction (DTI) and binding affinity (DTA) prediction is the heterogeneous nature of the input data: drugs can be represented as SMILES strings, molecular graphs, or fingerprints, while proteins can be described by amino acid sequences, contact maps, or embeddings derived from protein language models. Multi-modal and attention-based architectures have emerged as powerful frameworks for integrating these diverse representations, enabling models to capture complementary chemical and biological information that single-modality approaches inevitably miss. The insights gained from such fusion strategies also lay important groundwork for understanding more complex multi-drug scenarios, which will be addressed in the following subsection on drug-drug interaction prediction.

### Motivation for Multi-Modal Fusion

Single-modality models often fail to exploit the full richness of molecular information available. For example, SMILES-based sequence models capture global chemical syntax but may miss local topological structure, while graph-based models excel at encoding atom-level interactions but may not capture long-range dependencies effectively. Fusing multiple modalities—such as simultaneously processing SMILES sequences, molecular graphs, protein contact maps, and protein language model embeddings—has therefore become a dominant design principle in state-of-the-art DTI and DTA models.

Multi-modal approaches can be broadly categorized according to their fusion strategy: **early fusion** combines raw feature representations before the main learning components; **late fusion** integrates separately learned representations at the prediction stage; and **attention-based fusion** uses cross-modal attention mechanisms to dynamically weight the contribution of different modalities based on the task context. Each strategy presents distinct trade-offs in terms of model expressiveness, computational cost, and interpretability.

### Multi-View Self-Attention for DTI

One prominent line of work employs multi-view self-attention mechanisms to jointly process heterogeneous molecular representations. [67] proposes a self-attention-based multi-view representation learning framework that simultaneously models compound and target features derived from multiple descriptor types. Evaluated on benchmark kinase datasets, this approach achieves competitive predictive performance while providing biologically meaningful and interpretable drug-target interaction insights. By applying self-attention across multiple views of the same molecule or protein, the model learns which aspects of the chemical or biological representation are most informative for interaction prediction, and the resulting attention weights can be directly related to pharmacologically relevant structural features.

The interpretability afforded by such multi-view attention architectures is particularly valuable in drug discovery contexts, where understanding which molecular substructures or protein residues drive binding is as important as the prediction accuracy itself. This aligns with broader efforts to derive machine-learned pharmacophores and structure-activity relationship insights from attention mechanisms [82].

### Attention-Based Multi-Input Architectures

Beyond self-attention, attention mechanisms have been embedded within broader multi-input deep learning architectures that simultaneously process structural and physicochemical data. [79] proposes a deep learning architecture that concurrently trains on SMILES-derived feature matrices and molecular descriptors, integrating an attention module to weight the contribution of each input type. Applied to EGFR inhibitor prediction, this architecture achieves substantial improvements in MCC and AUC over single-modality baselines, demonstrating that attention-guided fusion of structural and physicochemical representations yields more accurate and interpretable predictions of biological activity.

This paradigm—simultaneously processing multiple molecular feature types through a shared attention mechanism—represents an important generalization of the earlier unimodal CNN and recurrent models discussed in the previous subsection, and its success across benchmark datasets underscores the value of capturing complementary information from different molecular descriptors.

### Multi-Modal Fusion of Drug and Target Representations

More recent DTI and DTA architectures extend multi-modal fusion to incorporate protein-specific representations derived from language models or structural data alongside compound representations. Fusing protein sequence embeddings with molecular graph encodings within cross-attention frameworks enables models to capture intermolecular interaction patterns that neither modality alone can fully represent. Such architectures build on the general principle that heterogeneous molecular representations encode distinct and complementary aspects of molecular identity and function [27].

The integration of protein contact maps—which encode spatial proximity between residues in the folded protein—alongside molecular graph representations of the ligand provides a structure-aware view of the binding pocket that complements sequence-based protein encodings. Combined with molecular fingerprints or SMILES-derived embeddings for the drug, these multi-modal fusion architectures more faithfully model the three-dimensional interaction landscape of protein-ligand binding, directly extending the geometry-informed approaches described in the preceding subsection to a multi-modal setting.

### Early vs. Late Fusion Strategies

The choice between early and late fusion strategies has significant practical implications. Early fusion approaches concatenate raw or lightly processed features from multiple modalities before the main deep learning backbone, allowing the model to learn joint cross-modal representations from the outset. In contrast, late fusion approaches train separate encoders for each modality and combine their outputs—typically via concatenation, element-wise operations, or learned gating—at the penultimate prediction layer. Late fusion offers modularity and the ability to leverage pre-trained modality-specific encoders, whereas early fusion may capture richer cross-modal interactions at the cost of increased model complexity.

[86] illustrates the power of integrating both differentiable end-to-end learned representations and predefined molecular fingerprints through a multi-view architecture trained adversarially, demonstrating that complementary descriptor schemes can be effectively combined to improve performance on clinically relevant DTI benchmarks. This work highlights that early and late fusion strategies are not mutually exclusive: hybrid architectures that leverage both learned and fixed representations can outperform approaches that rely on a single fusion paradigm.

### Cross-Modal Attention and Intermolecular Transformers

Cross-modal attention mechanisms—which compute attention weights between tokens or nodes from different modalities—represent a particularly expressive fusion strategy for DTI and DTA prediction. Intermolecular graph transformer architectures model the pairwise interaction between ligand atoms and protein residues through cross-attention layers, enabling the model to identify which atom-residue pairs contribute most strongly to predicted binding affinity. Such architectures naturally produce interpretable binding site predictions and interaction maps that can guide medicinal chemistry optimization.

The integration of protein language model embeddings—derived from large-scale pre-training on protein sequence databases—further enriches protein representations within these cross-modal frameworks. As pre-trained protein embeddings encode evolutionary and structural information that is difficult to learn from limited labeled DTI data alone [137], their incorporation into multi-modal fusion architectures substantially improves generalization to novel protein targets, partially addressing the cold-start problem highlighted in the preceding subsection on DTA prediction.

### Interpretability via Fusion Architectures

A key advantage of attention-based multi-modal fusion architectures over black-box single-modality models is their enhanced interpretability. Attention weights computed across modalities and across molecular substructures provide a direct window into model reasoning, allowing researchers to identify which molecular features drive predicted interactions. This interpretability is not merely a desirable property for scientific understanding—it is increasingly important for regulatory acceptance and for guiding iterative lead optimization in real-world drug discovery workflows [82; 67].

By visualizing cross-modal attention maps, medicinal chemists can identify which functional groups of a ligand are predicted to interact with specific protein residues, providing actionable guidance for structural modifications that may improve binding affinity, selectivity, or ADMET properties. This closes the loop between computational prediction and experimental design in a way that opaque single-modality models cannot. Notably, the attention-based interpretability frameworks developed here share conceptual foundations with the explainability approaches that also prove essential in drug-drug interaction prediction, as explored in the following subsection.

### Summary

Multi-modal fusion and attention-based architectures represent a leading paradigm for DTI and DTA prediction, offering superior predictive accuracy through the integration of heterogeneous molecular and protein representations, as well as enhanced interpretability through attention-guided identification of key interaction features. From multi-view self-attention models [67] to adversarially trained multi-input architectures [86] and attention-enhanced biological activity predictors [79], the field has demonstrated that combining multiple molecular representations within unified, attention-driven frameworks consistently outperforms single-modality baselines and yields biologically meaningful insights into the molecular determinants of drug-target binding. These principles of multi-modal integration and attention-based reasoning extend naturally to the prediction of drug-drug interactions, where modeling complex pairwise relationships between multiple drug entities presents analogous representational challenges at an expanded scale.

### 5.4 Drug-Drug Interaction Prediction: Methods and Architectures

## 5.4 Drug-Drug Interaction Prediction: Methods and Architectures

Drug-drug interactions (DDIs) occur when the presence of one drug alters the pharmacological effect of another, potentially leading to adverse clinical outcomes or therapeutic failure. Predicting DDIs computationally is therefore a critical component of drug safety assessment and clinical research. Traditional identification through experimental trials and clinical observation is expensive, time-consuming, and incapable of covering the vast combinatorial space of possible drug pairs, motivating the development of machine learning and deep learning approaches to accelerate and scale DDI prediction [66]. As noted at the close of the preceding subsection on multi-modal fusion, the pairwise relational nature of DDI prediction presents representational challenges that are in many ways analogous to—yet more complex than—those encountered in drug-target interaction modeling, requiring architectures that can jointly reason over two drug entities and their broader pharmacological context. The methods surveyed here, in turn, provide an important foundation for the knowledge graph embedding and reasoning approaches discussed in the following subsection, where relational drug-drug and drug-target information is situated within large-scale heterogeneous biomedical graphs.

### Graph Neural Network-Based Approaches

Graph neural networks (GNNs) have emerged as a dominant paradigm for DDI prediction due to their natural ability to model molecular structures and relational biological networks. Molecules can be naturally represented as graphs where atoms serve as nodes and chemical bonds as edges, enabling GNNs to learn expressive molecular representations directly from structural data without relying on hand-crafted features [27]. This graph-based molecular encoding philosophy directly extends the GNN and geometry-informed architectures discussed in previous subsections on DTA prediction to the multi-drug setting. Various GNN architectures have been explored for DDI prediction, ranging from graph convolutional networks to more sophisticated attention-based and hierarchical models.

**Bi-level graph networks** address DDI prediction by simultaneously modeling both the intramolecular graph structure of individual drugs and the intermolecular interaction graph between drug pairs. This dual-level formulation allows the model to capture fine-grained atomic-level features while also leveraging the global relational structure among drugs in a network context, improving both binary interaction detection and multi-typed DDI event classification [66].

**Multi-view graph contrastive learning** approaches construct multiple views of molecular graphs through augmentation strategies such as atom masking, bond dropout, and subgraph removal, and then apply contrastive objectives to align representations across views. This strategy encourages the model to learn robust, invariant molecular embeddings that generalize better to unseen drug pairs and reduces reliance on large labeled datasets [66]. The multi-view philosophy here shares conceptual roots with the multi-modal fusion strategies described in the preceding subsection, extending the principle of integrating complementary representations to the domain of self-supervised molecular learning.

**Hierarchical multi-relational graph representation learning** methods model DDIs as multi-relational link prediction problems over heterogeneous biomedical graphs. These approaches construct graphs where nodes represent drugs and edges encode typed relational interactions, and then use multi-relational GNN variants or knowledge graph embedding techniques to predict the type and probability of interactions between drug pairs. The hierarchical aspect captures information at multiple scales, from atom-level features to substructure motifs to whole-molecule representations, enabling more nuanced interaction modeling [66; 36]. This multi-relational graph perspective directly anticipates the knowledge graph embedding and reasoning frameworks surveyed in the following subsection.

### Siamese and Attention-Based Architectures

**Siamese attention-based neural networks** represent another family of architectures for DDI prediction. These models process two drug molecules through shared-weight encoder branches, leveraging attention mechanisms to identify the most pharmacologically relevant structural features contributing to the interaction. The symmetric architecture is particularly well-suited to DDI prediction since the interaction relationship between two drugs is inherently pairwise and often symmetrical in nature. Attention weights provide an interpretable window into which molecular substructures are driving predicted interactions [78; 138]. This attention-based interpretability connects closely to the cross-modal attention frameworks discussed in the preceding subsection on multi-modal fusion, extending the principle of attention-guided feature identification from single drug-target pairs to drug-drug interaction scenarios.

### Graph Prompt Learning Methods

An emerging direction involves **graph prompt learning** for DDI prediction, which adapts large pretrained graph models to DDI-specific tasks using lightweight prompt mechanisms rather than full fine-tuning. This approach is particularly valuable when labeled DDI data is scarce, as it enables efficient transfer of rich molecular representations learned during large-scale pretraining to the DDI prediction task with minimal additional supervision [66]. The use of large-scale pretraining echoes the incorporation of protein language model embeddings within multi-modal DTI frameworks, underscoring a broader trend toward leveraging pre-trained representations to address data scarcity across drug discovery prediction tasks.

### Integration of Molecular Structure with Pharmacological Features

Beyond purely structure-based models, several methods integrate molecular structural representations with pharmacological, biological, and clinical features to improve DDI prediction. These multi-modal approaches combine graph-based molecular encoders with drug target profiles, side effect fingerprints, anatomical therapeutic chemical (ATC) codes, and pathway information to provide a richer representation of each drug's biological context [66; 36]. Such integration is particularly valuable for predicting DDIs in under-studied drug classes or for drugs with limited structural similarity to known interacting pairs, and it mirrors the multi-modal fusion strategies described in the preceding subsection while scaling them to encompass a broader range of pharmacological data sources.

### Multi-Typed DDI Event Prediction

While early computational DDI models focused on binary interaction prediction (interaction vs. no interaction), recent work has shifted toward predicting specific **multi-typed DDI events**—such as increased or decreased drug bioavailability, altered metabolism, cardiotoxicity risk, or specific adverse event mechanisms. This is formulated as a multi-class or multi-label classification problem over heterogeneous drug interaction graphs, where each edge type corresponds to a distinct interaction mechanism. Models leveraging relational graph convolutional networks and knowledge graph reasoning have demonstrated particular strength in this setting [66; 36]. The multi-relational nature of this problem setting provides a natural bridge to the knowledge graph embedding methods discussed in the following subsection, where typed edge prediction over heterogeneous biomedical graphs is a central technical challenge.

### Synergistic Drug Combination Prediction

A closely related but distinct problem is **synergistic drug combination prediction**, which seeks to identify drug pairs or higher-order combinations that produce super-additive therapeutic effects—an area of particular importance in oncology and infectious disease treatment. Deep learning models for this task must capture complex non-linear interactions between drugs and their cellular targets, and graph-based approaches that model drug combinations within the context of disease-specific molecular networks have shown promising results [66].

### Summary

The landscape of machine learning approaches for DDI prediction has expanded substantially, from early fingerprint-based similarity methods to sophisticated GNN architectures, contrastive learning frameworks, hierarchical multi-relational models, and multi-modal integration strategies [66; 19]. The progression from the attention-based multi-modal fusion approaches of the preceding subsection to the graph-based and multi-relational DDI methods surveyed here reflects a broader trajectory in the field: as prediction targets grow more complex—from single drug-target binding to pairwise drug-drug interactions—so too must the representational and relational sophistication of the underlying models. Despite significant progress, key challenges remain including handling data imbalance and sparsity, generalizing to novel drug pairs outside the training distribution, and providing interpretable mechanistic explanations for predicted interactions. These challenges motivate the turn toward knowledge graph embeddings and structured biomedical reasoning explored in the following subsection, where large-scale relational knowledge bases provide both richer contextual information and improved interpretability for drug discovery tasks.
