### 5.5 Knowledge Graph Embeddings and Reasoning for Drug Discovery

## 5.5 Knowledge Graph Embeddings and Reasoning for Drug Discovery

Building upon the graph-based approaches discussed for DDI prediction, biomedical knowledge graphs (KGs) provide a broader and more powerful framework for integrating heterogeneous information from diverse databases—including DrugBank, PharmGKB, KEGG, and PubMed—into structured relational representations that capture complex biological and pharmacological interactions. By encoding entities such as drugs, genes, proteins, diseases, pathways, and phenotypes as nodes, and their diverse relationships as typed edges, these graphs enable machine learning models to perform multi-relational reasoning over rich prior knowledge for a wide range of drug discovery tasks. As previewed in the following subsection, this relational structure also forms the backbone of multimodal knowledge graph representation learning approaches that further enrich drug and protein embeddings with heterogeneous data modalities.

The construction of large-scale biomedical knowledge graphs has been demonstrated in several drug discovery contexts. For instance, comprehensive KGs integrating 15 million edges across 39 types of relationships connecting drugs, diseases, genes, pathways, and expression data have been built from large corpora of scientific publications to support drug repurposing [52]. Similarly, SARS-CoV-2-specific knowledge graphs encoding interactions among viral baits, host genes, pathways, drugs, and phenotypes have been employed to prioritize repurposable drug candidates [53]. These examples underscore the utility of KGs as integrative platforms for synthesizing multi-source biomedical evidence that would be intractable to analyze through any single data modality alone.

Knowledge graph embedding (KGE) methods aim to learn low-dimensional vector representations of entities and relations such that the geometric structure of the embedding space encodes the relational semantics of the graph. Translation-based methods, such as TransE and its variants, model relations as translations in entity embedding space, imposing that the head entity embedding plus the relation embedding approximates the tail entity embedding. These approaches are computationally efficient and suitable for large-scale biomedical graphs with millions of triples. Factorization-based methods, including DistMult and ComplEx, decompose the three-way interaction tensor of head entity, relation, and tail entity into bilinear forms, capturing more complex relational patterns including symmetry and antisymmetry. Neural network-based KGE methods, such as ConvE and RotatE, leverage convolutional or rotation-based operations to model higher-order interactions, achieving improved expressiveness for heterogeneous biomedical graphs with diverse relation types.

Beyond entity-level embedding, knowledge subgraph learning methods extract local subgraphs surrounding candidate entity pairs and apply graph neural networks to reason over the structural context of those subgraphs. This approach is particularly relevant for the drug-drug interaction (DDI) and drug-target interaction (DTI) prediction tasks discussed in previous sections, where the neighborhood structure of a drug-target or drug-drug pair provides informative contextual cues that complement purely structural molecular representations. Graph summarization techniques such as SumGNN aggregate information from multi-relational subgraphs using hierarchical pooling and relation-specific aggregation, enabling the model to identify the most relevant relational paths for predicting DDI types while providing interpretable reasoning chains. Similarly, KnowDDI and related methods leverage knowledge subgraph representations to perform multi-typed DDI prediction with enhanced biological interpretability by explicitly encoding relevant relational context from the knowledge graph.

Multi-hop reasoning over knowledge graphs extends beyond single-step relation prediction to infer complex relational chains involving multiple intermediate entities and relations. In the drug discovery context, multi-hop reasoning enables models to trace biological mechanisms connecting drugs to disease phenotypes through gene targets, pathway membership, and protein-protein interactions, thereby providing mechanistically grounded predictions rather than purely statistical associations. Path-based reasoning approaches, including random walk methods and neural path encoders, traverse the knowledge graph to generate explanation paths that support drug repurposing and interaction predictions [19].

The integration of deep learning with knowledge graph reasoning has emerged as a particularly fruitful direction for drug repurposing [52; 55]. Network-based deep learning methodologies that operate over knowledge graphs combining drug, disease, gene, and pathway information have demonstrated the ability to identify repurposable drugs validated by transcriptomic and proteomic evidence as well as clinical trial data [52]. Graph-based deep neural networks applied to SARS-CoV-2 knowledge graphs have similarly enabled the identification and validation of drug candidates through genetic profiling and electronic health records [53].

A critical advantage of knowledge graph-based approaches is their capacity for interpretable prediction—a property that connects closely to the challenges of generalization and mechanistic explainability noted as open problems in DDI prediction. By grounding predictions in explicit relational paths and knowledge subgraphs drawn from curated biomedical databases, these methods provide biologically meaningful explanations that can be scrutinized by domain experts, supporting both scientific trust and regulatory compliance [42]. The combination of structured prior knowledge with expressive graph neural network architectures positions knowledge graph embedding and reasoning methods as indispensable tools in the modern AI-driven drug discovery toolkit, particularly for tasks requiring generalization to novel drug-target pairs and interpretable evidence synthesis across heterogeneous biomedical data sources. This foundation naturally motivates the integration of knowledge graphs with multimodal molecular and protein representations, as explored in the following subsection.

### 5.6 Multimodal Knowledge Graph Representation Learning

## 5.6 Multimodal Knowledge Graph Representation Learning for Drug and Protein Embeddings

Building directly upon the knowledge graph embedding and reasoning methods discussed in the preceding subsection, multimodal knowledge graph representation learning has emerged as a powerful paradigm for further enriching drug and protein representations by integrating heterogeneous data sources and diverse entity modalities into unified embedding frameworks. Unlike conventional knowledge graph embedding methods that rely solely on relational structure, multimodal approaches incorporate complementary information from protein sequences, molecular graphs, SMILES strings, 3D structural data, and textual biomedical descriptions to produce more expressive and informative representations—directly addressing the challenge of achieving robust generalization to novel drug-target pairs that was identified as a key limitation of purely relational methods.

A central motivation for multimodal knowledge graph learning in drug discovery stems from the observation that individual data modalities capture only partial aspects of biological and chemical entities. Protein sequences encode evolutionary and functional information, molecular graphs encode topological and chemical bonding patterns, and textual descriptions from biomedical databases encode curated domain knowledge about pharmacological activity and biological function. Integrating these modalities within a unified knowledge graph framework allows models to leverage the complementary strengths of each representation, yielding embeddings that are simultaneously sensitive to molecular structure, biological function, and relational context.

The Otter-Knowledge framework exemplifies this approach by constructing multimodal knowledge graphs that incorporate data from seven diverse public sources, encompassing over 30 million triples, and integrating knowledge graph embeddings with sequence and SMILES-based representations [139]. By augmenting standard sequence and SMILES representations with rich relational knowledge extracted from these multi-source graphs, Otter-Knowledge achieves state-of-the-art results on drug-target binding affinity prediction benchmarks within the Therapeutic Data Commons (TDC) [139]. This work demonstrates that incorporating knowledge about existing relations among molecules and proteins into learned representations can substantially improve predictive performance beyond what is achievable through unsupervised pretraining on sequences or SMILES alone.

Multi-source knowledge graph integration also addresses the challenge of data sparsity that is pervasive in drug discovery settings, where labeled interaction data is often scarce. By jointly embedding entities from multiple knowledge graphs—encompassing drug-protein interactions, pathway memberships, disease associations, and side-effect profiles—these frameworks enable the transfer of relational information across different biological contexts, improving generalization to novel drugs and targets. The release of pretrained models and standardized benchmark tasks by efforts such as Otter-Knowledge facilitates reproducible evaluation and encourages further research into how multimodal knowledge-enhanced embeddings can advance binding affinity and drug-target interaction prediction [139].

More broadly, the integration of biomedical knowledge graphs with molecular and protein representations reflects a growing recognition that effective drug discovery models must transcend single-modality boundaries. Knowledge graphs derived from databases such as DrugBank, KEGG, and PharmGKB encode rich biological and pharmacological relationships that, when combined with learned molecular representations from graph neural networks or language models, yield unified embeddings that capture both structural and relational aspects of drug-target biology. This multimodal integration paradigm provides a natural bridge to the drug repurposing applications discussed in the following subsection, where these same multi-source relational structures—integrating drug, disease, target, and pathway information across diverse biomedical databases—form the backbone of graph-based pipelines for systematically uncovering novel therapeutic indications from heterogeneous biological data.

### 5.7 Drug Repurposing via Graph and Knowledge Graph Methods

## 5.7 Drug Repurposing via Graph and Knowledge Graph Methods

Drug repurposing—the identification of new therapeutic indications for existing, approved drugs—offers a compelling strategy to reduce the time, cost, and risk associated with traditional de novo drug discovery. By leveraging the rich relational structure of biomedical knowledge, graph machine learning and knowledge graph (KG) embedding methods have emerged as powerful computational tools for systematically uncovering novel drug-disease associations from heterogeneous biological data. As discussed in the preceding subsection, multimodal knowledge graph frameworks integrating diverse data sources—such as DrugBank, KEGG, and PharmGKB—can substantially enrich molecular and protein representations; these same multi-source relational structures form the backbone of graph-based drug repurposing pipelines, directly connecting the representation learning advances covered previously to the task of therapeutic indication discovery.

### Foundations and Motivation

The core premise of computational drug repurposing is that drugs, targets, diseases, pathways, and genes are interconnected through a complex web of biological relationships that can be captured and mined algorithmically. Knowledge graphs, which represent biomedical entities as nodes and their interactions as typed edges, provide a natural substrate for this task [36]. By integrating data from sources such as DrugBank, PharmGKB, KEGG, and disease ontologies, these graphs encode rich prior knowledge about pharmacological mechanisms, gene-disease associations, and protein-protein interactions. Graph machine learning methods—including graph neural networks (GNNs), knowledge graph embedding (KGE) models, and heterogeneous network approaches—can then be applied to perform link prediction, inferring missing or novel drug-indication relationships from the observed graph topology and entity representations [72].

### Transductive vs. Inductive Learning Strategies

A fundamental distinction in graph-based drug repurposing is between transductive and inductive learning paradigms. In transductive settings, models are trained and evaluated on a fixed graph where all entities (drugs, diseases, targets) are observed during training; the task is to predict missing links among known nodes. Classical KGE methods such as TransE, DistMult, and ComplEx operate in this regime, learning entity and relation embeddings that generalize across the observed graph structure. These approaches have demonstrated strong performance on established drug repurposing benchmarks but are limited in their ability to handle novel drugs or diseases that were absent during training.

Inductive learning strategies, by contrast, aim to generalize to entirely new entities not seen during training, which is critical for repurposing drugs against emerging or rare diseases. Graph neural network-based approaches are particularly well-suited for inductive settings, as they derive entity representations through aggregation of local neighborhood structure and features rather than relying solely on learned entity-specific embeddings. This capability is especially valuable when dealing with newly approved drugs or newly characterized diseases for which limited interaction data exists [42].

### Few-Shot Link Prediction for Drug Repurposing

A significant practical challenge in drug repurposing is the scarcity of known positive associations for many disease-drug pairs, particularly for rare or emerging conditions. Few-shot link prediction methods address this by learning to generalize from a small number of supporting examples for a given relation type or disease category. Meta-learning frameworks, when applied to drug-repurposing KGs, allow models to rapidly adapt their predictions to new diseases or drug classes with minimal supervised signal. These approaches draw on the broader meta-learning literature to construct episodic training procedures that mimic the few-shot inference scenario encountered in real repurposing campaigns, thereby improving generalization to novel therapeutic contexts [42].

### Heterogeneous Network-Based Methods

Biomedical knowledge underlying drug repurposing is inherently heterogeneous, involving multiple entity types and diverse relation semantics. Heterogeneous graph neural networks and metapath-based approaches are designed to explicitly model this heterogeneity. By defining task-relevant metapaths—sequences of node and edge types that capture biologically meaningful association chains, such as Drug→Target→Disease or Drug→Pathway→Gene→Disease—these methods enable richer relational reasoning than homogeneous GNNs [42]. Heterogeneous network-based repurposing frameworks have been applied to systematically score drug-disease associations by propagating information across multiple biological layers, integrating genomic, proteomic, and pharmacological data within a unified graph representation [72]. This multi-layer integration anticipates the broader heterogeneous data integration paradigm explored in the following subsection, where genomics, proteomics, transcriptomics, and clinical sources are jointly incorporated to further enrich predictive models.

### Application to Emerging Diseases: COVID-19 and Beyond

The COVID-19 pandemic provided a high-profile demonstration of the urgency and utility of computational drug repurposing. With no approved therapeutics available at the outbreak's onset, graph-based and KG-based methods were rapidly deployed to identify existing drugs with potential anti-SARS-CoV-2 activity. Heterogeneous KGs integrating viral protein interactions, human protein-protein interaction networks, drug-target associations, and disease comorbidity data were used to rank candidate repurposed drugs for experimental validation [36; 72]. These efforts highlighted both the promise of graph-based repurposing—enabling rapid hypothesis generation at scale—and its limitations, including the need for high-quality, up-to-date KGs and robust experimental follow-up pipelines.

Beyond COVID-19, graph-based repurposing has been applied to a wide range of diseases including Alzheimer's disease, rare genetic disorders, and various cancers, demonstrating the generality of the approach and its potential to complement traditional drug discovery workflows [15].

### Benchmarking Challenges and Evaluation Protocols

Rigorous and fair evaluation of drug repurposing models presents several non-trivial challenges. A fundamental issue is the construction of reliable negative examples: in most drug-disease KGs, the absence of a known association does not necessarily imply a true negative, as many drug-disease pairs are simply unexplored rather than experimentally disproven. This open-world assumption necessitates careful negative sampling strategies that go beyond random selection.

Biologically-driven negative sampling approaches seek to construct more informative and realistic negative sets by leveraging chemical similarity, target profile dissimilarity, or disease mechanistic knowledge to identify drug-disease pairs that are plausibly non-efficacious. Such strategies reduce the risk of evaluating models on trivially easy negatives and provide a more faithful estimate of real-world repurposing performance [36]. Additionally, data leakage in transductive evaluations—where temporal or structural dependencies between training and test splits inflate apparent performance—is a recognized concern that motivates the adoption of time-stratified or target-stratified evaluation protocols.

Standardized benchmarking platforms and datasets tailored to drug repurposing, integrating multiple biomedical KG sources with curated positive and negative interaction sets, are critical for enabling fair comparison across methods [46]. The field would benefit from community-wide agreement on evaluation conventions, including the choice of negative sampling strategies, graph splitting procedures, and performance metrics that reflect the ultimate goal of identifying experimentally actionable repurposing candidates.

### Summary

Graph machine learning and knowledge graph embedding methods have established themselves as powerful computational tools for drug repurposing, enabling systematic inference of novel drug-indication relationships from heterogeneous biomedical data. The field continues to advance on multiple fronts—including inductive generalization to unseen entities, few-shot adaptation to data-scarce disease contexts, and integration of multi-modal biological knowledge—while grappling with persistent challenges in evaluation rigor, negative sampling, and bridging the gap between computational predictions and experimental validation [27; 42]. These advances set the stage for the subsequent discussion of heterogeneous biomedical data integration, where the graph-based relational reasoning introduced here is extended to encompass genomics, proteomics, transcriptomics, and clinical data sources, enabling machine learning models that operate across the full spectrum of biological scales relevant to drug discovery.

### 5.8 Integrating Heterogeneous Biomedical Data: Genomics, Proteomics, and Clinical Sources

## 5.8 Integrating Heterogeneous Biomedical Data: Genomics, Proteomics, and Clinical Sources

As established in the preceding subsection on drug repurposing via graph and knowledge graph methods, the relational structure of biomedical knowledge graphs provides a powerful substrate for reasoning about drug-disease associations. Extending this graph-based relational reasoning to encompass the full spectrum of biological data modalities—genomics, proteomics, transcriptomics, metabolomics, and clinical records—is essential for building machine learning models that can accurately predict drug-target interactions (DTI) and drug-drug interactions (DDI) with sufficient biological relevance. Single-source models are fundamentally limited in their capacity to capture the multi-scale regulatory and functional relationships that govern drug behavior in living systems, motivating a shift toward heterogeneous data integration frameworks. This multi-modal integration paradigm, in turn, directly motivates the interpretability challenges addressed in the following subsection, where understanding which integrated data sources drive a given prediction becomes critical for scientific trust and clinical actionability.

A central challenge in this integration paradigm is the construction of heterogeneous biological networks that unify entities such as genes, proteins, diseases, pathways, and small molecules within a single graph-based representation—building directly upon the multi-relational knowledge graph architectures introduced for drug repurposing. These networks can encode diverse interaction types—protein-protein interactions (PPIs), gene co-expression links, metabolic pathway memberships, and phenotypic associations—as typed edges in a multi-relational graph, enabling message passing and embedding algorithms to propagate information across biological scales. Genomic features such as mutation profiles and copy number variations can be incorporated as node attributes, while proteomics data derived from mass spectrometry can inform the functional annotation of protein nodes.

Automatic meta-path learning methods have emerged as a principled approach to exploit the rich semantics of heterogeneous biomedical networks. Rather than relying on manually curated meta-paths that define biologically meaningful connectivity patterns—a process that requires significant domain expertise and may miss important relationships—these methods learn to identify informative relational pathways automatically from data. Such approaches leverage attention mechanisms over candidate meta-paths to adaptively weight their contributions to DTI prediction, thereby combining the structural inductive biases of heterogeneous graph models with the flexibility of data-driven feature selection. This is particularly valuable when integrating genomics and proteomics data with pharmacological information, as the optimal relational pathways through the biological network may vary across different drug and target families.

Multi-scale topological analysis of protein-protein interaction (PPI) networks offers another powerful avenue for enriching DTI and DDI models with systems-level biological information. PPI networks encode functional co-dependencies between proteins, and their topological properties—including centrality measures, community structure, and network motifs—are informative for predicting target druggability, polypharmacology, and adverse interaction profiles. Machine learning methods that operate on these network topologies, including graph neural networks that perform multi-hop neighborhood aggregation, can extract representations that reflect the broader functional context of a drug target within the cellular signaling landscape. Integrating such network-derived embeddings with sequence- and structure-based protein representations has been shown to improve generalization, particularly for targets with limited experimental interaction data.

Transcriptomics data, including gene expression profiles measured under drug perturbations or disease conditions, provides a complementary source of information for DTI and DDI prediction. Transcriptional signatures such as those available in the Connectivity Map (CMap) and LINCS L1000 datasets capture the genome-wide cellular response to chemical compounds, thereby encoding indirect functional relationships between drugs and biological systems that may not be apparent from molecular structure alone. Integrating these signatures into machine learning pipelines—for example, by training models to predict drug-induced gene expression changes or by using expression profiles as auxiliary features for interaction prediction—connects chemical representations to phenotypic outcomes and biological pathways.

Pathway databases such as KEGG, Reactome, and GO provide curated biological knowledge about the molecular mechanisms through which genes and proteins interact in functional modules. Incorporating pathway membership as structured prior knowledge into heterogeneous graph construction allows machine learning models to reason about functional relationships at a coarser granularity than individual protein-protein links, capturing the modular organization of biological processes relevant to drug mechanism of action and toxicity. This pathway-level integration is especially important for polypharmacological drug design, where predicting interactions across multiple targets requires understanding the downstream functional consequences of simultaneous target engagement.

Large-scale pre-trained models have proven instrumental in generating informative representations that encode interaction knowledge across biological scales without requiring task-specific supervision. Protein language models such as ESM-2 and ProtTrans, trained on massive databases of protein sequences, produce embeddings that implicitly encode evolutionary, structural, and functional relationships between proteins—including binding site characteristics relevant for DTI prediction. Similarly, chemical language models and graph neural network models pre-trained on large molecular databases provide rich compound representations that can be fine-tuned on downstream DTI and DDI tasks. Crucially, multi-modal pre-trained models that jointly process chemical and biological entities—such as those that simultaneously embed drug molecular graphs and protein sequences within a shared representation space—can capture cross-modal interaction patterns that are missed by unimodal approaches.

The integration of clinical data sources, including electronic health records (EHRs), pharmacovigilance databases, and clinical trial outcome repositories, extends the scope of machine learning models beyond molecular biology to encompass patient-level observations of drug effects. Clinical adverse event databases such as FAERS and SIDER provide real-world evidence of drug-drug interaction outcomes and off-target effects that can serve as training labels for DDI prediction models. Integrating such clinical signals with molecular and network-based features enables models to bridge the translational gap between in vitro molecular interaction measurements and in vivo pharmacological outcomes. However, clinical data integration poses significant challenges related to data heterogeneity, missing values, confounding variables, and privacy constraints, requiring specialized preprocessing pipelines and robust normalization strategies.

Despite significant progress, several open challenges remain in the integration of heterogeneous biomedical data for DTI and DDI prediction. These include the development of principled methods for handling modality-specific missing data, the design of scalable heterogeneous graph models that can incorporate growing multi-omics datasets without proportional increases in computational cost, and the creation of evaluation frameworks that specifically assess the contribution of each data modality to predictive performance. Furthermore, ensuring biological interpretability of integrated models—by linking model predictions back to specific genomic variants, pathway perturbations, or clinical phenotypes—remains a critical requirement for translating machine learning insights into actionable therapeutic hypotheses. This interpretability imperative forms the central focus of the following subsection, which examines attention-based mechanisms, knowledge subgraph visualization, and biologically grounded explanation frameworks developed specifically to make such complex, multi-modal drug interaction predictions transparent and scientifically meaningful. Addressing the broader integration challenges will require close collaboration between computational scientists, bioinformaticians, and clinical researchers to develop machine learning frameworks that are both technically rigorous and biologically meaningful.

### 5.9 Interpretability and Explainability in Drug Interaction Prediction

## 5.9 Interpretability and Explainability in Drug Interaction Prediction

As machine learning models for drug-target interaction (DTI) and drug-drug interaction (DDI) prediction grow increasingly complex—incorporating heterogeneous biomedical data from genomics, proteomics, and clinical sources as discussed in the preceding section—the need for interpretability and explainability has become paramount. In clinical and pharmaceutical contexts, black-box predictions are insufficient; researchers and practitioners require transparent, biologically meaningful justifications for model outputs to build scientific trust, support regulatory compliance, and guide actionable decisions in drug development. This interpretability imperative is especially pressing given the multi-modal and multi-scale nature of modern drug interaction models, where predictions emerge from the complex interplay of molecular, network, and clinical features.

**Attention-Based Interpretability Mechanisms**

Attention mechanisms have emerged as one of the most widely adopted tools for providing interpretability in drug interaction models. By assigning learned weights to different molecular substructures, protein residues, or interaction pairs, attention layers can highlight which features contribute most to a predicted interaction or affinity value. In transformer-based and graph attention architectures, these weights offer a direct, albeit approximate, window into model reasoning. For instance, models predicting drug-target binding affinity can visualize which atoms or functional groups in a ligand receive high attention when interacting with specific protein residues, providing chemists with hypotheses about binding pharmacophores and critical contact points.

Bilinear attention networks, exemplified by architectures such as DrugBAN, extend this concept by computing interaction maps between drug and protein feature vectors. These bilinear attention maps explicitly model pairwise interactions between molecular substructure features and protein sequence segments, generating two-dimensional interaction matrices that can be overlaid on molecular and protein structures to highlight putative binding interfaces. Such visualizations offer intuitive, structure-activity relationship (SAR) insights that align closely with biochemical intuition and experimental data, making them particularly valuable for medicinal chemists seeking to rationalize or optimize binding profiles.

**Knowledge Subgraph Visualization and Graph-Based Explainability**

Knowledge graph-based approaches to DDI and DTI prediction introduce a distinct mode of interpretability through reasoning paths and subgraph visualization. Methods such as SumGNN and KnowDDI leverage the relational structure of biomedical knowledge graphs—the same heterogeneous network representations discussed in the context of multi-omics data integration—to not only make predictions but also identify the most relevant subgraph neighborhoods that support each prediction. By surfacing multi-hop reasoning paths—for example, connecting a drug to a target through shared biological pathways, gene associations, or pharmacological mechanisms—these models produce explanations that are grounded in established biological knowledge, making them far more interpretable than purely data-driven approaches.

Knowledge subgraph learning methods can identify which intermediate entities (e.g., enzymes, transporters, or metabolic pathways) mediate a predicted interaction, enabling users to trace the logical chain from input entities to predicted outcomes. This transparency is especially important in drug repurposing scenarios, where understanding the mechanistic rationale for a predicted new indication can inform experimental follow-up and regulatory submissions. The ability to generate and visualize supporting reasoning chains transforms these models from opaque predictors into interpretable reasoning engines that augment human expertise.

**Graph Pooling Methods for Identifying Synergistic Mechanisms**

For synergistic drug combination prediction, specialized graph pooling methods have been developed to identify the molecular and network-level features that drive synergy. Approaches like SANEpool and related hierarchical graph pooling architectures learn to select and aggregate the most informative subgraph structures within drug-target or drug-drug interaction networks, providing cluster-level explanations of which molecular modules or network communities underlie predicted synergistic effects. These methods can pinpoint, for example, which protein complexes or pathway crosstalk nodes are particularly relevant to a given drug combination's synergistic activity, offering biological hypotheses that can be validated experimentally.

Hierarchical multi-relational graph representation learning methods further contribute to interpretability by decomposing interaction predictions across multiple relation types—such as metabolic interactions, transport interference, and target co-engagement—allowing users to understand not just whether an interaction is predicted, but what mechanistic type it likely represents. This level of mechanistic specificity is critical for clinical decision-making, where distinguishing between pharmacokinetic and pharmacodynamic DDIs has direct implications for patient safety.

**Biologically Grounded Explanation Frameworks**

Beyond attention and graph pooling, several approaches have been developed to generate biologically grounded explanations by integrating domain knowledge into the interpretability pipeline. Models that incorporate pathway information, gene ontology annotations, or protein-protein interaction networks into their architectures can attribute predictions to specific biological processes, making explanations more actionable for domain experts. Multi-scale approaches that operate across molecular, protein, and systems biology levels enable explanations that bridge the gap between molecular features and clinically relevant outcomes—directly complementing the multi-omics integration strategies described in Section 5.8.

Gradient-based attribution methods, such as integrated gradients and saliency maps adapted for molecular graphs, complement attention-based approaches by providing model-agnostic explanations of which input features most influence a prediction. When applied to GNN-based DTI or DDI models, these methods can highlight specific atoms, bonds, or protein residues whose perturbation most dramatically alters predicted interaction strength, offering complementary insights to attention visualization.

**Challenges and Outlook**

Despite significant progress, the interpretability of drug interaction models remains an active and challenging research area. Attention weights, while intuitive, do not always faithfully reflect causal feature importance and can be misleading without careful calibration. Knowledge graph reasoning paths are only as reliable as the underlying knowledge base, which may contain errors, incompleteness, or biases. Graph pooling explanations may identify statistically important substructures without necessarily reflecting true biochemical mechanisms. These interpretability challenges are further compounded in multi-modal settings, where attributing a prediction to specific genomic variants, pathway perturbations, or clinical phenotypes across heterogeneous data sources requires additional methodological care.

Moving forward, developing standardized evaluation frameworks for the quality of explanations—such as alignment with known binding pharmacophores, SAR data, or clinical evidence—will be essential to validate interpretability claims and build genuine scientific trust. Such evaluation frameworks will naturally complement the broader benchmarking challenges for drug interaction prediction discussed in the following section. Integrating interpretability as a first-class design objective, rather than a post-hoc analysis, will drive the development of inherently interpretable architectures that provide transparent reasoning without sacrificing predictive accuracy. As drug interaction models increasingly influence clinical and regulatory decisions, the stakes for rigorous, biologically meaningful interpretability continue to grow.

### 5.10 Benchmarking, Generalization, and Open Challenges in Drug Interaction Prediction

## 5.10 Benchmarking, Generalization, and Open Challenges in Drug Interaction Prediction

Despite remarkable progress in machine learning for drug-target interaction (DTI) and drug-drug interaction (DDI) prediction—spanning attention-based architectures, knowledge graph reasoning, and multi-omics integration as surveyed in the preceding sections—the field faces a number of persistent and interconnected challenges related to benchmarking practices, evaluation rigor, and real-world generalization. Addressing these challenges is essential for translating computational models into reliable tools that can meaningfully accelerate pharmaceutical research, and they represent the natural frontier beyond the interpretability and explainability considerations discussed in Section 5.9.

### Data Leakage and Transductive Evaluation Pitfalls

One of the most critical issues in existing benchmarking practices is data leakage arising from transductive evaluation settings. In many DTI and DDI datasets, models are trained and evaluated on the same set of drugs and targets, where the test set contains entities that have appeared during training. Under such conditions, models can exploit memorized interaction patterns rather than learning generalizable predictive features. Standard random splitting of interaction datasets frequently results in substantial overlap between training and test entities, artificially inflating reported performance metrics. This problem is especially acute for graph-based methods that explicitly leverage topological proximity within the interaction network, where high-degree nodes in training data can enable near-trivial inference for adjacent test nodes. Rigorous benchmarking therefore requires the adoption of more stringent splitting protocols, such as leave-drug-out, leave-target-out, and leave-pair-out evaluations, which better simulate the real-world challenge of predicting interactions for entirely novel compounds or uncharacterized biological targets.

### Out-of-Distribution Generalization to Novel Drugs and Targets

Closely related to the transductive evaluation problem is the fundamental challenge of out-of-distribution (OOD) generalization. In practice, drug discovery requires reliable predictions for new chemical scaffolds and protein targets that are structurally dissimilar to those observed during training. Scaffold-based splitting strategies, analogous to those employed in molecular property prediction benchmarks, reveal that many state-of-the-art DTI and DDI models suffer substantial performance degradation when evaluated on structurally diverse test molecules. The development of models capable of robust OOD generalization remains an open research priority. Approaches that incorporate domain-informed priors, equivariant geometric representations, and causal reasoning frameworks have shown promise in improving generalization, but systematic benchmarking of these strategies across diverse DTI and DDI tasks is still lacking.

### Cold-Start Problems for Unseen Entities

The cold-start problem represents a particularly challenging scenario in DTI and DDI prediction, where predictions must be made for drugs or targets that have no interaction data at all in the training set. Classical matrix factorization and collaborative filtering approaches inherently cannot handle cold-start entities, as they rely on learned entity embeddings that are undefined for unseen nodes. While graph neural network-based and sequence-based deep learning models have greater potential for zero-shot or few-shot generalization through their use of molecular structure and sequence features, achieving reliable cold-start performance remains difficult in practice. Transfer learning from protein-protein interaction networks and chemical-chemical similarity graphs has been proposed as a strategy to alleviate cold-start issues in DTI prediction, but standardized evaluation protocols for cold-start settings are not yet universally adopted in the field.

### Data Imbalance and Sparsity in Interaction Datasets

Drug-target and drug-drug interaction datasets are inherently imbalanced and sparse. Known positive interactions represent only a tiny fraction of all possible drug-target or drug-drug pairs, and the ratio of positive to negative examples is highly skewed. Furthermore, experimentally verified negative interactions are rarely reported in public databases, necessitating the use of assumed negatives that are generated by sampling from unobserved pairs. The choice of negative sampling strategy has a profound impact on model training and evaluation, as naively sampled negatives may be trivially distinguishable from positives based on simple structural or pharmacological features, leading to overly optimistic performance estimates. Biologically informed negative sampling strategies that generate hard negatives based on structural similarity or shared target classes are essential for producing more realistic and challenging benchmarks.

### Need for Standardized Evaluation Protocols and Publicly Available Tools

The lack of standardized evaluation protocols across the DTI and DDI literature makes fair model comparison exceedingly difficult. Different studies use different dataset versions, splitting strategies, negative sampling procedures, and evaluation metrics, rendering direct performance comparisons unreliable. There is a pressing need for community-wide benchmarking platforms analogous to MoleculeNet and the Therapeutic Data Commons for molecular property prediction, which would provide standardized datasets, canonical splits, reference implementations, and leaderboards for DTI and DDI tasks. Such platforms would also facilitate the systematic evaluation of models under multiple splitting regimes, including random, scaffold-based, and entity-based splits, enabling a more comprehensive understanding of model strengths and limitations. Importantly, these platforms should incorporate evaluation dimensions aligned with the interpretability standards discussed in Section 5.9, assessing not only predictive accuracy but also the biological plausibility of model explanations.

### Handling Heterogeneous and Multi-Source Data

DTI and DDI datasets are drawn from heterogeneous sources, including bioactivity databases such as ChEMBL and BindingDB, clinical adverse event databases, and curated knowledge graphs such as DrugBank and PharmGKB. Differences in experimental assay conditions, measurement units, activity thresholds, and biological contexts introduce substantial noise and inconsistency into training data. Integrating multi-source data into unified benchmarks requires careful curation and harmonization, and models trained on heterogeneous data must be robust to label noise and distributional inconsistencies across data sources. This challenge directly mirrors the multi-omics and heterogeneous data integration issues described in Section 5.8, underscoring the need for benchmarks that reflect the full complexity of real-world biomedical data environments. Multi-task learning frameworks that jointly model multiple interaction types and data sources have shown promise in improving robustness, but their generalization under rigorous evaluation settings warrants further systematic investigation.

### Future Directions for Robust Benchmarking

Looking forward, the development of truly robust benchmarking frameworks for DTI and DDI prediction will require coordinated community efforts to establish standardized data splits, evaluation metrics, and negative sampling protocols. Prospective experimental validation of computational predictions, in which model-prioritized drug-target pairs are tested in wet-lab assays, provides the most stringent and scientifically meaningful evaluation of real-world utility. Embedding uncertainty quantification into model outputs is increasingly recognized as essential for supporting informed decision-making in drug discovery, as models that produce well-calibrated confidence estimates enable practitioners to prioritize predictions for experimental follow-up. The evaluation frameworks needed to validate interpretability claims—such as alignment with known binding pharmacophores and structure-activity relationship data, as discussed in Section 5.9—should be treated as integral components of these broader benchmarking efforts rather than as separate concerns. Collectively, addressing these open challenges will be critical for transitioning machine learning models for drug interaction prediction from research benchmarks to reliable components of real-world drug discovery pipelines.

## 6 Structure-Based Drug Design and Protein Structure Prediction

### 6.1 Protein Structure Prediction with Deep Learning

The prediction of three-dimensional protein structures from amino acid sequences has long been considered one of the most fundamental and challenging problems in structural biology. For decades, experimental methods such as X-ray crystallography, cryo-electron microscopy, and nuclear magnetic resonance spectroscopy served as the primary means of elucidating protein structures, yet these approaches remain extraordinarily resource-intensive, time-consuming, and applicable only to a fraction of the known protein sequence space. The advent of deep learning has fundamentally transformed this landscape, culminating in breakthroughs that have dramatically reshaped structure-based drug design workflows.

The most celebrated milestone in this transformation is AlphaFold2, developed by DeepMind, which achieved near-experimental accuracy in protein structure prediction at the CASP14 competition. AlphaFold2 introduced several key architectural innovations that collectively accounted for its remarkable performance. Central among these is the Evoformer module, a specialized attention-based architecture that processes multiple sequence alignments (MSAs) to extract co-evolutionary information from related protein sequences. By attending simultaneously over both the sequence and residue-pair dimensions, the Evoformer builds up rich representations of residue-residue relationships that implicitly encode structural constraints. These representations are then passed to a structure module that employs invariant point attention and geometric reasoning grounded in SE(3)-equivariant frames to iteratively refine backbone and side-chain conformations, ultimately producing highly accurate all-atom structural models.

The integration of AlphaFold2-predicted structures into drug discovery pipelines has been transformative. Prior to AlphaFold2, structure-based drug design was largely limited to proteins with experimentally resolved structures, creating significant blind spots for targets whose structures were difficult or impossible to obtain experimentally. The release of the AlphaFold Protein Structure Database, covering hundreds of millions of protein sequences including the majority of the human proteome and numerous pathogen proteomes, has dramatically expanded the druggable target space. Researchers can now conduct structure-based virtual screening, binding site identification, and molecular docking against protein targets for which no experimental structure previously existed [4]. This capability is particularly significant for emerging infectious diseases, rare genetic disorders, and previously undruggable proteins, enabling drug discovery campaigns against a vastly broader range of biological targets [12].

Following AlphaFold2, a number of related and competing approaches have been developed that extend or adapt the core framework. RoseTTAFold introduced a three-track neural network architecture that simultaneously processes sequence, distance, and coordinate information, enabling bidirectional information flow across all three representations. ESMFold, developed by Meta AI, leverages large-scale protein language models (PLMs) trained on hundreds of millions of protein sequences to generate structural predictions without requiring MSA computation, significantly reducing inference time and computational cost. This MSA-free approach is particularly advantageous for proteins with few known homologs in sequence databases, as well as for high-throughput applications requiring rapid structure prediction across large protein families. The success of ESMFold and related PLM-based approaches highlights the remarkable amount of structural information implicitly encoded in protein sequence distributions learned by transformer-based language models.

Efforts to reduce the computational overhead of structure prediction have motivated further innovations. While AlphaFold2's reliance on deep MSA searches through large sequence databases achieves state-of-the-art accuracy, this step constitutes a major computational bottleneck. MSA-free methods such as ESMFold bypass this requirement entirely, trading a modest reduction in accuracy for orders-of-magnitude improvements in throughput. Hybrid approaches have also been proposed that use lightweight PLM embeddings as substitutes for full MSA representations, enabling faster inference while retaining much of the structural accuracy of MSA-dependent methods. These advances are particularly enabling for large-scale drug discovery applications, such as high-throughput virtual screening campaigns where structures must be predicted for thousands of target variants or homologs [13].

Beyond static structure prediction, several deep learning frameworks have begun to address the inherently dynamic nature of proteins. Proteins are not rigid structures but ensembles of conformations that fluctuate under physiological conditions, and these conformational dynamics are critically relevant to drug binding, allosteric regulation, and selectivity. Models that predict conformational ensembles rather than single structures are an active area of research, with approaches ranging from stochastic sampling within AlphaFold2's framework to generative models trained to capture multi-state protein conformations. Accurate modeling of protein flexibility is expected to become increasingly important for structure-based drug design, particularly for targets characterized by induced-fit binding mechanisms or cryptic allosteric pockets—themes that recur prominently in the context of molecular docking methods discussed in the following section.

The integration of predicted protein structures into downstream drug design tasks encompasses multiple workflow stages. For binding site identification, predicted structures provide the three-dimensional surface representations required by pocket detection algorithms, enabling the identification of druggable cavities even for structurally uncharacterized targets [140]. For molecular docking and scoring, AlphaFold-predicted structures serve as receptor models in docking simulations, though challenges remain regarding the accurate representation of binding-site sidechain flexibility and the confidence calibration of predicted loops and disordered regions. For de novo structure-based molecule generation, predicted pocket structures define the three-dimensional constraints within which generative models design ligands. Collectively, these integrations represent a paradigm shift in structure-based drug discovery, moving from a regime limited by structural data availability to one where deep learning can bridge the gap between sequence and structure for virtually any protein of interest [2].

Despite these remarkable advances, important limitations remain. AlphaFold2 and related models predict single static structures under implicit averaged conditions and do not directly account for binding-induced conformational changes, post-translational modifications, or non-standard amino acid residues. The accuracy of predicted structures varies with the availability of homologous sequences, with orphan proteins possessing few homologs generally receiving less confident predictions. Furthermore, the translation of structurally accurate predictions into functionally accurate drug discovery outcomes requires careful validation, as small errors in binding site geometry can substantially impact docking accuracy and virtual screening enrichment. These concerns about receptor flexibility and binding site accuracy connect directly to the challenges confronted by machine learning-enhanced molecular docking methods, which must grapple with the same structural uncertainties when predicting ligand binding poses. Addressing these limitations through integration of molecular dynamics simulations, experimental validation, and uncertainty-aware modeling frameworks remains an active and important frontier in the field [1].

### 6.2 Machine Learning-Enhanced Molecular Docking

## 6.2 Machine Learning-Enhanced Molecular Docking

Molecular docking—the computational prediction of how a small molecule ligand binds to a protein target—has long been a cornerstone of structure-based drug discovery [4]. Traditional physics-based docking tools such as AutoDock Vina, Glide, and GOLD rely on hand-crafted scoring functions and heuristic search algorithms to sample and evaluate ligand poses within protein binding sites. While widely adopted, these approaches suffer from well-known limitations in scoring accuracy, computational efficiency when applied to large compound libraries, and difficulties in handling receptor flexibility. The emergence of deep learning has catalyzed a new generation of machine learning-enhanced docking methods that aim to overcome these limitations by learning directly from structural data—data whose availability has been dramatically expanded by the AlphaFold-driven structural coverage of the proteome described in the preceding section.

One prominent class of deep learning docking methods employs equivariant neural networks that respect the rotational and translational symmetries of three-dimensional molecular systems. Models such as EquiBind and E3Bind reformulate the docking problem as a direct prediction task, bypassing the expensive conformational search step of traditional docking by regressing binding poses in a single forward pass through an equivariant graph neural network. These end-to-end approaches achieve orders-of-magnitude speedups compared to physics-based tools, enabling rapid screening of large compound libraries [41]. The equivariance property ensures that predictions remain physically consistent regardless of the input orientation of the molecular complex, improving generalization to diverse protein-ligand pairs. This emphasis on physical symmetry in neural network design reflects a broader architectural trend that will recur in the equivariant pocket detection and affinity prediction methods discussed in the following sections.

Generative modeling frameworks have also been applied to the docking problem, most notably through diffusion-based approaches. DiffDock recasts molecular docking as a generative problem over the space of ligand poses, modeling the docking process as a reverse diffusion over translational, rotational, and torsional degrees of freedom. By training a score-based diffusion model on experimentally determined protein-ligand complex structures from the Protein Data Bank, DiffDock learns to generate a distribution of plausible binding poses for a given protein-ligand pair and selects the top-ranked pose using a confidence model. This generative perspective allows the model to capture multi-modal binding pose distributions and has demonstrated competitive or superior performance compared to traditional docking tools on standard benchmarks such as PDBbind [41].

Beyond pose prediction, end-to-end models such as FABind have been developed to jointly predict binding pocket locations and docking poses within a single unified architecture, eliminating the need for separate pocket detection preprocessing steps. By co-training pocket identification and pose prediction objectives, FABind leverages the mutual information between binding site geometry and ligand placement to improve overall docking accuracy, particularly for targets with less well-characterized binding sites. This joint learning paradigm previews a theme developed more fully in Section 6.3, where dedicated binding site identification methods are examined in detail; it is especially relevant when integrating with AlphaFold-predicted structures, where binding site geometry may be less experimentally validated [41].

A significant challenge in molecular docking, both classical and machine learning-based, is the treatment of receptor flexibility. Real protein-ligand binding events frequently involve conformational rearrangements of receptor sidechains and, in some cases, backbone movements, that are poorly captured by rigid receptor docking. This challenge connects directly to the limitation noted in the preceding section regarding AlphaFold2's prediction of single static structures that do not account for binding-induced conformational changes. Several deep learning docking frameworks have begun to incorporate flexible sidechain modeling, either through explicit prediction of sidechain torsion angles conditioned on the docked ligand pose, or by training on ensembles of receptor conformations generated from molecular dynamics simulations. ETDock and related methods incorporate SE(3)-equivariant representations of both the ligand and the flexible protein environment to improve pose accuracy in induced-fit binding scenarios.

Pre-training strategies have emerged as an important tool for improving the generalization of deep learning docking models, particularly given the limited size of experimentally determined protein-ligand complex datasets. One approach involves pre-training on large-scale datasets of computationally generated docking conformations produced by traditional tools, which, despite their inaccuracies, provide rich structural diversity for learning molecular interaction patterns. Self-supervised pre-training objectives such as masked atom prediction and 3D denoising have also been used to initialize encoders before fine-tuning on binding pose prediction tasks [41]. These pre-training strategies help the model learn general principles of protein-ligand interaction geometry, reducing the reliance on scarce experimentally validated training examples and improving robustness to novel protein families.

The integration of machine learning-enhanced docking into large-scale virtual screening workflows has demonstrated practical impact. High-throughput virtual screening campaigns, such as those targeting SARS-CoV-2 protein targets, have employed deep learning rescoring models in combination with traditional docking pre-filters to screen compound libraries of unprecedented size [13; 12]. These hybrid workflows exploit the computational speed of machine learning models to rescore and rerank docking poses at scale, while leveraging the structural interpretability of physics-based methods for initial pose generation. The complementarity of machine learning and physics-based simulation in such pipelines reflects a broader trend toward hybrid approaches that combine the strengths of both paradigms [50].

Despite these advances, machine learning-enhanced docking methods face several open challenges. Generalization to protein families underrepresented in training data remains a concern, as does the handling of allosteric binding sites and highly flexible targets—precisely the scenarios where accurate upstream binding site identification, as discussed in Section 6.3, becomes most consequential. The evaluation of deep learning docking models on realistic prospective screening campaigns, as opposed to retrospective benchmarks such as CASF and PDBbind, remains an area requiring further systematic study. Furthermore, the accuracy of docking outcomes depends critically on the quality of the receptor structure and binding site definition provided as input, establishing a direct dependency on the pocket detection methods examined in the following section. Nevertheless, the rapid progress in equivariant architectures, diffusion-based generative models, and scalable pre-training strategies positions machine learning-enhanced docking as a transformative capability for structure-based drug discovery.

### 6.3 Binding Site Identification and Pocket Detection

## 6.3 Binding Site Identification and Pocket Detection

Accurate identification of druggable binding sites on protein surfaces is a critical prerequisite for structure-based drug design, as the quality of pocket detection directly determines the reliability of downstream docking, virtual screening, and de novo molecule generation tasks. As established in the preceding discussion of machine learning-enhanced molecular docking, the docking search space is fundamentally defined by the identified binding pocket, making pocket detection a foundational upstream step whose errors propagate directly into pose prediction and affinity estimation. Traditional geometric and energy-based approaches such as cavity detection algorithms (e.g., fpocket, SiteMap) have long served this purpose, but they often rely on simplified shape-based criteria and lack the capacity to capture subtle chemical and electrostatic features that define true druggability. The integration of machine learning—and particularly deep learning—into binding site prediction has substantially expanded the accuracy and scope of pocket detection methods, providing richer structural representations that better inform the docking, affinity prediction, and generative design workflows discussed in subsequent sections.

### 3D Convolutional Neural Networks and Voxelized Representations

One of the most influential paradigms in machine learning-based pocket detection involves representing the protein environment as a three-dimensional volumetric grid, where each voxel encodes physicochemical features of the local protein environment such as atom type, charge, hydrophobicity, and hydrogen-bond donor/acceptor status. Deep fully convolutional neural networks operating on such voxelized representations can learn hierarchical spatial features that distinguish genuine binding cavities from non-functional surface depressions.

A notable example in this direction is Kalasanty, a 3D fully convolutional neural network designed for binding site segmentation [81]. Unlike earlier classification-based approaches that assign a single label to a protein, Kalasanty treats pocket prediction as a voxel-level segmentation task, producing dense spatial maps that intuitively delineate the three-dimensional shape and extent of predicted binding cavities. The segmentation paradigm offers several practical advantages: it enables direct visualization of the predicted pocket geometry, supports seamless integration with downstream structure preparation workflows, and naturally accommodates pockets of varying sizes and shapes. Extensive benchmarking on standard datasets including sc-PDB and DUD-E demonstrates that Kalasanty achieves high prediction accuracy and provides interpretable spatial representations suitable for direct incorporation into drug discovery projects [81].

DeepSurf represents another influential 3D CNN-based approach, in which protein surface points are first extracted and then centered local volumetric representations are constructed and fed into a convolutional neural network to classify each surface point as belonging to a binding site or not. By focusing on the molecular surface rather than the entire protein volume, DeepSurf reduces computational redundancy and emphasizes the interface region most relevant to ligand binding. The model was trained on experimentally validated protein-ligand complexes from the sc-PDB database and evaluated using the DCC (Distance from the Center of the pocket to the Center of Closest prediction) and DVO (Degree of Volume Overlap) metrics commonly used in the field.

### Equivariant Graph Neural Networks and Surface Geometry

While voxelized 3D CNN approaches have demonstrated strong performance, they are inherently constrained by grid resolution and may not efficiently capture the continuous, irregular geometry of protein surfaces. Equivariant graph neural network methods address these limitations by representing the protein surface as a graph or point cloud where geometric relationships between surface points are explicitly modeled while preserving physical symmetries such as rotation and translation equivariance. This equivariance property directly parallels advances in equivariant architectures for molecular docking—such as EquiBind and DiffDock discussed in the previous section—reflecting a broader trend toward physically consistent geometric learning across structure-based drug discovery tasks.

EquiPocket exemplifies this direction, employing an E(3)-equivariant graph neural network that models the protein surface geometry to identify binding pockets with improved sensitivity to the spatial arrangement of chemical features. By constructing a graph over surface points annotated with local physicochemical descriptors and learning equivariant message-passing operations, EquiPocket captures the continuous geometry of binding cavities in a manner that is robust to rigid-body transformations of the protein structure. This equivariance property is particularly important for structure-based drug design, where the same binding site should be identified regardless of the arbitrary orientation in which the crystal structure was solved or the model was generated.

### Point Cloud and Hybrid Strategies

Point cloud representations offer an alternative to both voxel grids and surface graphs by treating the set of protein surface atoms or surface vertices as an unordered set of three-dimensional points, each annotated with relevant chemical descriptors. Deep learning architectures adapted from the computer vision literature—such as PointNet and PointNet++—can operate directly on such representations, enabling flexible and computationally efficient pocket detection without requiring fixed-resolution discretization of space.

Hybrid strategies that combine multiple information modalities have also gained traction. These approaches leverage both the chemical richness of physicochemical descriptors (e.g., local electrostatic potentials, hydrophobicity indices, and hydrogen-bonding profiles) and the geometric information encoded in three-dimensional spatial coordinates to jointly assess druggability. For instance, models that integrate protein sequence-derived features—such as evolutionary conservation and predicted secondary structure—with structural surface features can better distinguish allosteric and cryptic sites from obvious orthosteric pockets, thereby broadening the scope of targetable binding sites. Such multi-modal pocket detection strategies are particularly valuable when the identified pockets serve as conditional inputs to structure-based generative models or as search spaces for the graph neural network-based affinity prediction models discussed in the following section.

### Benchmark Datasets and Evaluation Metrics

The evaluation of binding site prediction methods relies on several standardized datasets and metrics. The sc-PDB database, which contains experimentally validated protein-ligand complexes with annotated binding sites, serves as the primary training and benchmarking resource. Additional benchmarks include COACH420 and HOLO4K, which provide diverse test cases covering a wide range of protein families and binding site types. Evaluation metrics typically include the Distance from the Center of the pocket to the Closest prediction (DCC success rate, with a threshold commonly set at 4 Å), the Degree of Volume Overlap (DVO) between predicted and reference pockets, and the Matthews Correlation Coefficient (MCC) for binary pocket/non-pocket surface point classification.

A recurring challenge in fair benchmarking is the potential for data leakage between training and test sets, particularly when protein structures share high sequence or structural similarity. Careful scaffold-level or sequence-identity-based splitting is therefore essential to assess the true generalization capability of pocket detection models to novel protein families—a concern that equally applies to the binding affinity prediction benchmarks discussed in Section 6.4.

### Role in Downstream Drug Discovery Workflows

The practical impact of accurate binding site prediction extends well beyond the pocket detection task itself. In virtual screening pipelines, the identified pocket coordinates and shape define the docking search space, and errors or imprecision in pocket prediction directly propagate into docking accuracy and hit rates—connecting back to the machine learning-enhanced docking methods discussed in the preceding section. This coupling is particularly consequential when using AlphaFold-predicted protein structures for targets that lack experimental structural data, since the predicted structures may present binding cavities that differ subtly from the true holo conformation. Machine learning-based pocket detection methods that can identify functionally relevant cavities in predicted structures—and potentially anticipate induced-fit conformational changes—thus play an enabling role in expanding the druggable proteome.

Similarly, the binding affinity prediction models discussed in Section 6.4—including 3D-CNN scoring functions, physics-informed graph neural networks, and SE(3)-equivariant architectures—all require well-defined pocket geometries as structural inputs. Errors in pocket identification can degrade the quality of the protein-ligand complex representations these models process, leading to systematically biased affinity estimates. Likewise, structure-based de novo generative models that place new molecules within protein pockets require accurate pocket definitions as conditional inputs. Errors in pocket identification can lead generative models to design molecules that do not occupy the biologically relevant binding site, undermining the utility of the generated candidates. The integration of robust, deep learning-based pocket detection as an upstream component of both generative structure-based drug design and affinity prediction pipelines is therefore an important area of ongoing methodological development [27].

In summary, machine learning methods for binding site identification—spanning 3D CNN segmentation approaches such as Kalasanty [81], equivariant graph neural networks such as EquiPocket, and point cloud and hybrid strategies—have substantially advanced the accuracy, interpretability, and scope of pocket detection. As the field moves toward routine use of computationally predicted protein structures and autonomous drug discovery pipelines, the continued development of robust and generalizable pocket detection models will remain a foundational enabler of the docking, affinity prediction, and generative design methods that collectively constitute the structure-based drug design toolkit.

### 6.4 Protein-Ligand Binding Affinity Prediction with Structure-Based Models

## 6.4 Protein-Ligand Binding Affinity Prediction with Structure-Based Models

Having established accurate binding site identification as a foundational upstream step, the logical next challenge in structure-based drug design is to quantify how strongly a candidate molecule binds to the identified pocket. Accurate prediction of protein-ligand binding affinity from three-dimensional structural data is a cornerstone of structure-based drug design, enabling the prioritization of candidate molecules and the rationalization of binding mechanisms. Over the past decade, deep learning has dramatically expanded the toolkit available for this task, moving well beyond classical physics-based scoring functions toward data-driven approaches that can capture complex, non-linear structure–activity relationships directly from atomic coordinates. As will be seen in Section 6.5, the affinity prediction models developed here also directly inform and complement structure-based de novo molecule generation, where the same pocket representations and geometric architectures are repurposed to actively design novel ligands rather than merely evaluate existing ones.

### 3D Convolutional Neural Network Scoring Functions

Early deep learning scoring functions represented protein-ligand complexes as voxelized three-dimensional grids, assigning atom-type and physicochemical features to grid cells and applying 3D convolutional neural networks (3D-CNNs) to learn spatial interaction patterns—an approach conceptually related to the voxelized pocket representations employed by binding site detection methods such as Kalasanty and DeepSurf discussed in the preceding section. Models such as Pafnucy and DeepAtom exemplified this paradigm, demonstrating that 3D-CNNs could learn latent representations of binding pockets and ligand geometries that correlate meaningfully with experimentally measured binding affinities. These approaches benefit from their capacity to process raw structural information without requiring hand-crafted interaction features, though they suffer from computational expense, sensitivity to grid resolution, and a tendency to overfit when training data are limited. Benchmark evaluations on the CASF datasets revealed that while 3D-CNN scoring functions achieve competitive correlation coefficients with experimental affinities, they often struggle to generalize reliably to structurally diverse protein families or to novel scaffolds not well represented in training sets [41].

### Atomic Convolutional Networks

Atomic convolutional networks (ACNNs) addressed some limitations of voxel-based CNNs by operating directly on the pairwise atomic distances within a protein-ligand complex rather than on a discretized grid. By learning radial basis function expansions of inter-atomic distances and applying convolutional filters over neighborhoods defined by atom-type pairs, ACNNs produce rotation- and translation-invariant representations that are more memory-efficient than voxelized grids. Despite these advantages, ACNNs remain sensitive to the completeness and quality of the input structural data—underscoring the importance of accurate pocket identification as discussed in Section 6.3—and their performance on CASF benchmarks indicates that they benefit considerably from large, diverse training corpora [41].

### Physics-Informed Graph Neural Networks

A particularly important advance came with the development of physics-informed graph neural networks (GNNs) that explicitly incorporate knowledge of underlying physical interactions—such as van der Waals forces, hydrogen bonding, hydrophobic effects, and metal coordination—into their message-passing schemes. Models in the PIGNet family represent protein-ligand complexes as molecular interaction graphs in which nodes correspond to atoms and edges encode specific types of physical interactions, allowing the network to learn interaction-type-specific energy contributions. PIGNet and its successor PIGNet2 demonstrated that embedding physical inductive biases into the GNN architecture improves both predictive accuracy and generalization to novel protein-ligand pairs compared to purely data-driven graph approaches, while also enabling more interpretable decomposition of predicted binding energies into physically meaningful components. Evaluations on CASF scoring, ranking, docking, and screening benchmarks confirmed that physics-informed models achieve strong performance particularly in the low-data regime where purely statistical approaches tend to overfit [41].

### Geometry-Complete and SE(3)-Equivariant Graph Neural Networks

Building on the insight that molecular interactions are fundamentally governed by three-dimensional geometry, recent work has developed geometry-complete and SE(3)-equivariant GNN architectures for binding affinity prediction. These equivariant architectures are closely related to the equivariant graph neural networks discussed for pocket detection in Section 6.3 and for molecular docking in Section 6.2, reflecting a broader trend toward physically consistent geometric learning across structure-based drug discovery tasks. Models such as GCPNet incorporate geometric completeness principles to ensure that all relevant three-dimensional structural information—including inter-atomic distances, bond angles, and dihedral angles—is faithfully preserved and processed through equivariant message passing layers. By constructing representations that transform predictably under rotations and translations of the molecular complex, SE(3)-equivariant models achieve superior physical plausibility and generalization compared to models that process fixed Cartesian coordinates. These architectures have shown state-of-the-art performance on CASF benchmarks and are particularly robust when applied to protein-ligand complexes derived from predicted rather than experimentally resolved structures, an important consideration as AlphaFold-predicted structures increasingly populate structure-based drug discovery workflows [41].

### Dynamic Structure-Aware Models

A critical limitation shared by most structure-based affinity prediction models is their reliance on single, static crystal structures that may not capture the conformational flexibility of proteins and ligands in solution. Dynamic structure-aware models such as Dynaformer address this limitation by incorporating molecular dynamics (MD) trajectories into the learning process, allowing the model to account for ensemble effects, induced fit, and conformational sampling that influence binding thermodynamics. By encoding temporal structural information through attention mechanisms operating over trajectory snapshots, such models capture dynamic features—including pocket reshaping, loop flexibility, and water-mediated interactions—that are inaccessible to static structure-based approaches. This trajectory-aware capability also has direct implications for the co-design scenarios explored in Section 6.5, where induced-fit conformational changes present a key challenge for structure-based generative models.
