Benchmark evaluations demonstrate that trajectory-aware models achieve improved correlation with experimentally measured binding free energies, particularly for highly flexible targets and allosteric binding sites [41].

### Multi-Task Bioassay Pre-Training Strategies

The scarcity of high-quality protein-ligand binding affinity data for diverse target families has motivated the development of multi-task and transfer learning strategies for structure-based affinity prediction. Multi-task bioassay pre-training involves simultaneously learning from a broad collection of heterogeneous biochemical assay measurements during pre-training, thereby enabling the model to capture shared structural features of protein-ligand binding that transfer to specific targets with limited data. By leveraging large-scale public bioactivity databases and incorporating both 2D and 3D molecular features, these pre-training strategies substantially reduce the data requirements for accurate affinity prediction on novel targets, addressing the chronic cold-start problem in structure-based drug discovery—a challenge that is equally pertinent to structure-based generative models discussed in Section 6.5 [22; 27].

### Meta-Modeling and Ensemble Approaches

Ensemble and meta-modeling strategies represent another important direction for improving the reliability of binding affinity predictions. By training multiple diverse scoring functions—including 3D-CNN, GNN, and physics-based models—and combining their predictions through learned meta-models or stacking approaches, ensemble methods reduce variance and improve calibration compared to individual models. Meta-modeling frameworks can also integrate complementary information from multiple docking programs and scoring functions, yielding consensus scores that are more robust to the idiosyncratic failures of any single method. Empirical evaluations on CASF benchmarks consistently demonstrate that ensemble approaches outperform the best individual components, particularly in the challenging screening and docking power tasks where structural uncertainty is high [41].

### Benchmarking and Challenges in Generalization

The CASF benchmark suite remains the primary standard for evaluating structure-based binding affinity prediction models, assessing scoring power (correlation with experimental affinities), ranking power (ability to rank compounds by affinity within a target), docking power (ability to identify near-native poses), and screening power (ability to enrich active compounds in virtual screening). Notably, the data partitioning concerns raised in Section 6.3 regarding fair benchmarking of pocket detection models apply equally here: careful sequence- and scaffold-level splitting is essential to ensure that CASF evaluations reflect genuine generalization rather than interpolation within known protein families. While leading deep learning models now achieve Pearson correlation coefficients approaching 0.85 on the CASF-2016 scoring benchmark, substantial challenges remain in generalizing to novel protein families, cryptic binding sites, and structurally diverse ligand chemotypes that are underrepresented in PDBbind training data. Additionally, the quality of input structures—whether experimentally resolved or computationally predicted—profoundly affects model performance, motivating ongoing research into uncertainty-aware and structure-agnostic affinity prediction approaches [41; 27]. Addressing these generalization challenges will require both the development of more diverse and representative training datasets and the design of architectures with stronger inductive biases toward the physical principles governing molecular recognition. These considerations carry forward directly into the structure-based generative design methods examined in the following section, where the same geometric representations and equivariant architectures are leveraged not merely to score candidate molecules but to actively construct novel ones conditioned on pocket geometry.

### 6.5 Structure-Based De Novo Molecule Generation and Protein-Ligand Co-Design

## 6.5 Structure-Based De Novo Molecule Generation and Protein-Ligand Co-Design

Building directly upon the structure-based binding affinity prediction models discussed in the previous section, structure-based de novo molecule generation represents a natural extension of these capabilities: rather than evaluating pre-existing molecules against a target, these approaches leverage the same three-dimensional protein pocket information to actively design novel ligands from scratch. This paradigm shift from ligand-based generative approaches explicitly incorporates the geometric and chemical environment of the target binding site into the molecular design process, enabling the generation of molecules with improved binding affinity, shape complementarity, and selectivity toward specific protein targets. The geometric deep learning architectures discussed in Section 6.6 provide the foundational machinery that makes many of these approaches possible, connecting binding affinity prediction, generative design, and equivariant molecular modeling into a coherent structure-based drug discovery framework.

Early structure-based generative approaches employed conditional variational autoencoders (CVAEs) that encoded protein pocket geometry using atomic density grids or voxelized 3D representations. In these frameworks, the encoder maps a protein-ligand complex into a continuous latent space, while the decoder generates novel ligands conditioned on the pocket representation. Such grid-based methods, while conceptually straightforward, suffer from high computational costs associated with voxelization and struggle to capture fine-grained atomic interactions at the pocket boundary. Subsequent work replaced volumetric grids with graph-based pocket representations, using graph neural networks to encode atomic contacts between protein residues and ligand atoms, yielding richer and more expressive pocket embeddings that better capture local chemical environments—directly echoing the graph-based representations that proved effective for affinity prediction in Section 6.4.

Autoregressive atom-by-atom generation methods emerged as an alternative strategy, building molecules incrementally within protein binding pockets guided by crystallographic structural data. These approaches model the generative process as a sequential decision problem, placing atoms one at a time based on the partial structure and the surrounding pocket context. By conditioning each placement step on the three-dimensional coordinates and chemical identity of neighboring protein atoms, autoregressive models can generate molecules that are geometrically compatible with the binding site. While these methods offer fine-grained control over generated structures, their sequential nature can limit diversity and computational efficiency during sampling.

The advent of diffusion models has significantly advanced structure-based molecular generation. SE(3)-equivariant diffusion models, such as DiffSBDD-style architectures, formulate molecule generation as a denoising process operating directly in three-dimensional Euclidean space. By respecting rotational and translational symmetries through SE(3)-equivariant neural networks—the same class of equivariant architectures that underpin the geometric deep learning models discussed in Section 6.6—these models ensure that generated molecules are physically plausible regardless of the global orientation of the protein-ligand complex. The diffusion process jointly perturbs atomic positions and chemical identities, and the reverse denoising process learns to recover valid molecular structures conditioned on the pocket representation. This class of models has demonstrated substantial improvements in generating molecules with high predicted binding affinity and favorable drug-likeness scores compared to earlier autoregressive and VAE-based counterparts.

Fragment-based and 3D graph editing approaches offer another promising direction. Methods such as MolEdit3D-style architectures decompose molecule generation into editing operations on three-dimensional molecular graphs, including fragment addition, deletion, and modification guided by pocket context. By operating at the fragment level rather than the atom level, these methods leverage chemically meaningful building blocks that naturally satisfy valence constraints and tend to be synthetically accessible. Fragment-based generation also enables scaffold hopping and R-group optimization workflows, where a core scaffold is retained while peripheral groups are redesigned to improve binding interactions or optimize ADMET properties.

In-pocket 3D graph architectures represent a unified approach that jointly encodes protein-ligand contacts within a single heterogeneous graph framework. In these models, protein residues and ligand atoms are represented as nodes in a shared graph, with edges encoding spatial proximity, covalent bonds, and non-covalent interaction types such as hydrogen bonds, hydrophobic contacts, and electrostatic interactions. Message passing over this joint graph allows the model to capture mutual dependencies between ligand structure and pocket geometry, enabling co-design scenarios where both the ligand and flexible protein side chains are simultaneously optimized. Such co-design capabilities are particularly valuable for addressing induced-fit binding scenarios where the protein undergoes conformational changes upon ligand binding—a dynamic behavior that also motivated the trajectory-aware affinity prediction models described in Section 6.4.

The integration of structure-based generative models into drug discovery workflows has demonstrated tangible advantages over purely ligand-based approaches [41]. By explicitly encoding three-dimensional pocket information, these methods generate molecules that are more likely to adopt favorable binding poses, exhibit higher docking scores, and possess improved selectivity profiles. Furthermore, conditioning generation on crystallographic or AlphaFold-predicted pocket structures enables the design of molecules for previously undruggable or structurally uncharacterized targets, substantially expanding the accessible therapeutic space [27].

Despite these advances, several challenges remain. Ensuring that generated molecules are not only geometrically complementary to the pocket but also synthetically feasible represents a critical bottleneck, as highly novel structures may be difficult or impossible to synthesize [1]. Multi-objective optimization frameworks that simultaneously balance binding affinity, drug-likeness, synthetic accessibility, and ADMET properties are therefore essential components of practical structure-based generative pipelines [59]. Combining structure-based generation with active learning and experimental feedback loops further promises to iteratively improve model performance and accelerate the identification of viable drug candidates [34]. These remaining challenges directly motivate the development of more expressive geometric deep learning architectures for molecular interaction modeling, which are examined in detail in the following section.

### 6.6 Geometric Deep Learning for 3D Molecular Interaction Modeling

## 6.6 Geometric Deep Learning for 3D Molecular Interaction Modeling

Building directly upon the structure-based de novo generation and co-design methods discussed in the previous section—which rely heavily on equivariant neural architectures to generate physically plausible molecules within protein binding pockets—this section examines the geometric deep learning foundations that underpin not only those generative approaches but also the broader landscape of 3D molecular interaction modeling in drug discovery. Geometric deep learning has emerged as a foundational paradigm for modeling three-dimensional molecular interactions, offering principled frameworks that respect the physical symmetries inherent in molecular systems. At the core of these approaches lies the concept of equivariance and invariance with respect to geometric transformations: molecules and protein-ligand complexes exist in three-dimensional Euclidean space and their physical properties must remain invariant under rotations, translations, and reflections (the E(3) or SE(3) symmetry groups). Neural network architectures that are explicitly designed to satisfy these symmetry constraints—known as SE(3)- or E(3)-equivariant networks—ensure that their predictions transform consistently with the input geometry, leading to improved generalization, data efficiency, and physical plausibility of learned representations. These same architectural principles directly enable the high-throughput virtual screening pipelines examined in the following section, which leverage geometric and graph-based models to rapidly evaluate millions of compounds against protein targets at scale.

### Foundations of Equivariant Neural Networks for Molecular Systems

The motivation for equivariant architectures in molecular modeling stems from a fundamental limitation of standard deep learning approaches: conventional message-passing graph neural networks (MPNNs) that operate on molecular graphs typically discard explicit 3D coordinate information or handle it in a manner that is not guaranteed to respect geometric symmetries. This can lead to physically inconsistent predictions when molecules are rotated or translated in space. Equivariant neural networks address this by maintaining geometric consistency throughout their computation, ensuring that learned features transform appropriately under the symmetry group of interest.

Graph neural networks operating on molecular and protein-ligand complex graphs represent a natural foundation for these approaches [69; 70]. By encoding atoms as nodes and bonds or intermolecular contacts as edges, these architectures can capture both local chemical environments and global structural patterns. When augmented with geometric equivariance, such graph networks can model directional interactions such as hydrogen bonds, van der Waals contacts, and electrostatic interactions with greater physical fidelity. The application of such geometric graph networks spans a broad range of structure-based drug discovery tasks, including binding affinity prediction, molecular docking pose scoring, de novo molecule generation within protein pockets, and protein-protein interaction modeling [69; 70]—thereby connecting the generative capabilities described in Section 6.5 with the screening workflows that follow in Section 6.7.

### Graph Neural Networks for Protein-Ligand Complex Modeling

A prominent class of geometric deep learning models applies graph neural networks directly to protein-ligand complex graphs, where the complex is represented as a unified heterogeneous graph encoding both intramolecular and intermolecular interactions. In such representations, protein residues or atoms and ligand atoms serve as nodes, while edges encode covalent bonds, non-covalent contacts, and spatial proximity relationships. Message-passing operations then propagate information across these edges to produce expressive representations of the binding interface—precisely the kind of joint protein-ligand encoding that enabled the in-pocket 3D graph architectures and co-design frameworks discussed in Section 6.5.

Physics-informed graph neural networks have demonstrated the value of encoding known physical interaction terms—such as van der Waals forces, hydrogen bonding, and electrostatic contributions—into the graph connectivity and edge features of protein-ligand complex models [141]. This physics-informed design endows geometric GNNs with inductive biases that improve generalization to structurally diverse protein-ligand pairs. The PotentialNet architecture represents an early and influential example of applying staged graph convolutions to protein-ligand complexes, where separate graph convolutional layers operate on covalent molecular graphs and non-covalent interaction graphs to model the multi-scale nature of molecular interactions [142]. This design achieved state-of-the-art performance on binding affinity prediction benchmarks and demonstrated the advantages of learning directly from atomic interaction graphs rather than relying on fixed expert-crafted features.

Hierarchical graph representation learning further enriches geometric modeling of protein-ligand interactions by capturing information at multiple scales—from individual atoms to molecular motifs and whole-molecule or whole-protein representations [65]. Such hierarchical approaches can identify which specific molecular substructures or binding site residues are most critical for the interaction, providing interpretable insights alongside accurate predictions. These multi-scale representations also naturally support the rescoring and ranking tasks central to the high-throughput virtual screening pipelines described in Section 6.7.

### Intermolecular Transformer Architectures

Beyond message-passing GNNs, transformer-based architectures have been adapted to model intermolecular interactions in three-dimensional space. The Intermolecular Graph Transformer (IGT) exemplifies this paradigm by employing a dedicated three-way attention mechanism that explicitly models the intermolecular information between drug molecules and target proteins [68]. By attending simultaneously to intramolecular drug features, intramolecular protein features, and the intermolecular interaction context, IGT captures richer representations of the binding interface compared to models that treat molecular and protein features independently before fusion. This architecture demonstrated significant improvements over prior state-of-the-art methods for both binding activity and binding pose prediction, and exhibited strong generalization to unseen receptor proteins—a critical requirement for practical virtual screening applications such as those discussed in Section 6.7 [68].

Multi-view self-attention mechanisms similarly extend the geometric modeling of drug-target interactions by constructing multiple learned views of molecular representations and attending across these views to capture complementary structural and chemical information [67]. Such attention-based fusion strategies provide not only competitive predictive performance but also interpretable attention maps that highlight the molecular features most relevant to the predicted interaction, bridging the gap between predictive accuracy and mechanistic understanding.

### Integration of Protein Language Model Embeddings

A significant recent development in geometric deep learning for molecular interaction modeling involves the integration of pre-trained protein language model (PLM) embeddings into geometric network architectures. Large-scale protein language models trained on vast databases of protein sequences learn rich, contextually-informed representations of amino acid positions that encode evolutionary, structural, and functional information. When these PLM-derived embeddings are incorporated as node features in geometric graph networks operating on protein-ligand complexes, the resulting models benefit from both the statistical knowledge encoded in protein sequence databases and the geometric reasoning capabilities of equivariant architectures.

Multimodal knowledge graph frameworks further extend this integration by combining protein language model embeddings with molecular graph representations and relational knowledge from biomedical databases, enabling unified models that leverage diverse information sources for binding affinity and drug-target interaction prediction [139]. Such approaches demonstrate that enriching geometric representations with pre-trained linguistic and relational embeddings consistently improves prediction performance on established benchmarks, pointing toward a future where geometric deep learning models are routinely augmented with broad pre-trained knowledge. This integration of sequence-derived and structure-derived information is particularly valuable in the context of AlphaFold-enabled virtual screening workflows discussed in Section 6.7, where predicted structural representations may benefit from complementary sequence-level signals.

### Equivariance, Invariance, and Physical Plausibility

The incorporation of SE(3)- and E(3)-equivariant constraints into deep learning architectures for molecular interaction modeling confers several important practical advantages beyond theoretical elegance. First, equivariant models achieve significantly improved data efficiency compared to non-equivariant baselines, as they need not learn the same interaction pattern redundantly for all possible orientations of a molecular complex in space. This is particularly valuable in drug discovery contexts where experimental 3D structural data—from X-ray crystallography, cryo-electron microscopy, or computational modeling—is often limited relative to the diversity of chemical space of interest. Second, equivariant architectures produce physically plausible predictions that are consistent with known symmetries of molecular systems, reducing the risk of spurious predictions arising from orientation-dependent artifacts. Third, these architectures facilitate the direct incorporation of 3D geometric features such as interatomic distances, bond angles, and torsion angles as equivariant or invariant edge features, enabling richer geometric reasoning about molecular structure than is possible with purely topological graph representations [69; 70]. These same properties—physical consistency, data efficiency, and expressive geometric featurization—are what make equivariant architectures the preferred backbone for both the SE(3)-equivariant diffusion models discussed in Section 6.5 and the deep learning rescoring functions employed in large-scale virtual screening.

### Applications to Docking Scoring and Protein-Protein Interactions

Geometric deep learning models have been extensively applied to improve the scoring of molecular docking poses—a critical step in structure-based virtual screening pipelines. Traditional physics-based scoring functions employed in molecular docking suffer from approximations that limit their accuracy, motivating the development of machine learning-based rescoring approaches. Geometric GNNs trained on experimental protein-ligand complex structures can learn implicit physical interaction terms that complement or supersede those in physics-based functions, leading to improved ranking of docking poses and better discrimination of true binders from decoys [141; 41]. As discussed in Section 6.7, these rescoring capabilities are directly deployed within high-throughput screening pipelines to enrich hit rates across compound libraries of millions to billions of molecules.

The application of geometric deep learning to protein-protein interaction modeling represents an emerging frontier, with equivariant architectures enabling the scoring and design of protein-protein docking poses and the identification of protein-protein interaction inhibitors—an important but challenging class of drug targets. By operating on graphs that represent entire protein complexes with geometric features encoding the three-dimensional arrangement of residues at the interface, these models can capture the complex shape and electrostatic complementarity requirements that govern protein-protein recognition.

### Challenges and Future Directions

Despite substantial progress, several key challenges remain in applying geometric deep learning to 3D molecular interaction modeling. The scarcity of high-quality experimental protein-ligand complex structures limits training data for supervised geometric models, motivating the development of data augmentation strategies, physics-informed regularization, and pre-training schemes on computationally generated structures [141]. The computational cost of equivariant message passing on large protein-ligand complex graphs—particularly when the full protein structure must be encoded—remains a practical bottleneck that motivates the development of more efficient equivariant architectures and hierarchical coarse-graining strategies. This efficiency challenge is particularly acute in large-scale HTVS settings where geometric models must be applied to millions of candidate compounds, as examined in Section 6.7. Furthermore, the evaluation of geometric models on realistic benchmarks that reflect the out-of-distribution challenges encountered in real-world virtual screening—including novel protein fold families, allosteric binding sites, and structurally diverse compound libraries—remains an important area for future benchmarking work [69].

Overall, geometric deep learning provides a powerful and principled framework for modeling three-dimensional molecular interactions that is well-suited to the physical nature of drug-target binding. By combining SE(3)- and E(3)-equivariant architectures with rich molecular and protein representations—including those derived from pre-trained language models and multimodal knowledge graphs—these approaches are driving significant advances in binding affinity prediction, docking pose scoring, and structure-based molecule generation, establishing geometric deep learning as a central methodology in modern computational drug discovery. The representational and predictive capabilities established in this section form the direct foundation for the scalable, high-throughput virtual screening workflows examined in the following section.

### 6.7 High-Throughput Virtual Screening with Structure-Based Models

## 6.7 High-Throughput Virtual Screening with Structure-Based Models

Building upon the geometric deep learning architectures discussed in the previous section—which provide principled frameworks for modeling protein-ligand interactions with physical symmetry constraints—high-throughput virtual screening (HTVS) represents one of the most practically impactful applications of structure-based machine learning in drug discovery. By enabling the rapid computational evaluation of millions to billions of compounds against therapeutically relevant protein targets, HTVS translates the representational power of geometric and graph-based models into large-scale screening campaigns that directly accelerate early-stage drug discovery.

Traditional docking-based virtual screening, while effective, is computationally expensive when applied to ultra-large compound libraries, and classical scoring functions often exhibit limited accuracy in rank-ordering candidate molecules. Machine learning-based structure-based models have emerged as powerful rescoring layers that can be applied downstream of initial docking filters to more accurately identify promising candidates from large-scale libraries [41]. The equivariant and graph neural network architectures described in Section 6.6—including physics-informed GNNs and transformer-based interaction models—serve as natural foundations for these rescoring approaches. Deep fusion and coherent fusion scoring approaches combine multiple heterogeneous feature representations—including protein pocket geometry, ligand topology, and interaction fingerprints—into unified predictive models capable of processing massive compound sets while retaining high discriminative power.

The SARS-CoV-2 pandemic provided a prominent and urgent test case for large-scale structure-based virtual screening pipelines. Researchers rapidly deployed computational campaigns targeting multiple viral protein targets, including the main protease (Mpro), the spike protein receptor-binding domain, and RNA-dependent RNA polymerase, screening libraries of hundreds of thousands to millions of compounds [41]. These campaigns demonstrated that combining physics-based docking with deep learning rescoring could substantially enrich hit rates and identify structurally diverse scaffolds that might be missed by conventional methods alone. The availability of experimentally resolved crystal structures for key SARS-CoV-2 targets accelerated these efforts, enabling the rapid construction of reliable docking setups and the validation of machine learning scoring models.

A transformative development in enabling HTVS against previously undruggable or structurally uncharacterized targets has been the advent of AlphaFold2 and related protein structure prediction models [41]. Prior to these advances, the absence of experimental three-dimensional structures for many therapeutically relevant proteins—including orphan receptors, intrinsically disordered proteins, and understudied kinases—severely limited the applicability of structure-based screening. AlphaFold-predicted structures have opened these targets to computational investigation, directly extending the reach of the geometric deep learning models discussed in Section 6.6 to previously inaccessible protein families. However, challenges remain regarding the accuracy of predicted binding pockets, particularly for flexible or allosteric sites, and the reliability of docking against predicted versus experimentally determined structures.

Scalable screening infrastructure has been another critical enabler of large-scale HTVS. Modern pipelines couple high-performance computing resources, including GPU-accelerated docking engines and distributed cloud-based architectures, with deep learning models to achieve screening throughputs that were previously infeasible [41]. Active learning frameworks have been integrated into these pipelines to prioritize the most informative compounds for docking evaluation, dramatically reducing computational cost while maintaining or improving hit identification rates [59]. These Bayesian optimization approaches iteratively query subsets of a large compound library for docking evaluation and use the resulting scores to train surrogate models that guide subsequent selection, effectively concentrating computational resources on the most promising chemical regions.

Evaluating the real-world pharmaceutical relevance of compounds identified through HTVS requires metrics that go beyond simple docking scores or binding affinity predictions. The delta score framework has been proposed as one such evaluation criterion, assessing how much a machine learning model improves upon the baseline performance of the underlying docking scoring function in identifying pharmaceutically relevant compounds [41]. This metric provides a more meaningful measure of the value added by deep learning rescoring in practical screening contexts, distinguishing genuine improvements from artifacts introduced by model overfitting or dataset biases. Complementary evaluation metrics including enrichment factors, area under the ROC curve, and precision-recall performance at early ranks of the screened library are also standard components of rigorous HTVS assessment frameworks [77].

Despite these substantial advances, several open challenges persist in large-scale HTVS with structure-based models. The accuracy of deep learning scoring functions often degrades when applied to protein targets or chemical scaffolds that are underrepresented in training data, reflecting the broader generalization challenges common to equivariant and graph-based architectures discussed in Section 6.6 [15]. Protein flexibility and induced-fit binding mechanisms remain difficult to model efficiently at scale, and the translation of high-scoring virtual hits into experimentally validated leads still exhibits significant attrition. Efforts to benchmark structure-based HTVS methods on standardized datasets and to develop robust evaluation protocols that reflect prospective rather than retrospective performance are critical priorities for the field [46]. Collectively, however, the convergence of accurate protein structure prediction, scalable docking engines, and deep learning rescoring—grounded in the geometric and equivariant modeling principles established throughout this section—has positioned high-throughput structure-based virtual screening as an increasingly central and productive component of modern computational drug discovery.

## 7 Challenges, Interpretability, and Future Directions

### 7.1 Data Scarcity, Imbalance, and Quality

## 7.1 Data Scarcity, Imbalance, and Quality

One of the most pervasive and fundamental challenges confronting machine learning applications in drug discovery is the scarcity of high-quality labeled experimental data. Generating reliable molecular bioactivity measurements, ADMET profiles, and binding affinity data requires extensive wet-lab experimentation that is both costly and time-consuming. As a result, most publicly available drug discovery datasets contain only hundreds to a few thousand labeled compounds for any given target or assay, far fewer than the millions of examples typically required to train deep learning models to their full potential [1]. This data scarcity problem is particularly acute for novel targets, rare diseases, and emerging pathogens, where historical experimental data is minimal or entirely absent—and notably, these are precisely the contexts where reliable model predictions would be most valuable.

Compounding the scarcity problem is the issue of severe class imbalance. In high-throughput screening campaigns, for instance, the ratio of active to inactive compounds often ranges from 1:100 to 1:10,000. This extreme imbalance means that naive machine learning models tend to be biased toward predicting the majority inactive class, resulting in poor sensitivity for identifying true hits. Similarly, toxicity and adverse drug event datasets frequently exhibit highly skewed label distributions, where toxic compounds represent a minority of the total screened chemical space [143]. Standard evaluation metrics such as accuracy become misleading under such imbalance, and specialized training objectives and evaluation protocols are required.

Beyond scarcity and imbalance, the quality and consistency of annotations in drug discovery datasets present a significant concern. Experimental measurements collected across different laboratories, assay conditions, and time periods are subject to systematic and random noise. Contradictory activity labels for the same compound tested under slightly different conditions are common in aggregated datasets. This label noise can introduce spurious correlations during training, leading to models that memorize artifacts rather than learning generalizable structure-activity relationships. Moreover, data drawn from heterogeneous sources may differ substantially in their chemical space coverage, assay protocols, and measurement units, further complicating model development [36]. These issues of data quality and consistency are intimately connected to the out-of-distribution generalization challenges discussed in the following section, since models trained on noisy or narrowly distributed data are particularly vulnerable to performance degradation when applied to novel chemical matter.

Several strategies have been proposed and investigated to mitigate these interrelated challenges. **Multi-task learning** has emerged as one of the most effective approaches for combating data scarcity. By jointly training models to predict multiple related molecular properties across diverse assays simultaneously, multi-task frameworks enable the sharing of statistical strength across tasks, effectively augmenting the available training signal for each individual task. This is particularly valuable when individual tasks have very limited labeled examples but share underlying physicochemical or biological mechanisms. Physical formula-enhanced multi-task learning represents a particularly innovative direction, in which known physical relationships between pharmacokinetic parameters are encoded directly into the model architecture. This approach facilitates effective knowledge sharing across tasks, reduces data demand compared to standard graph neural networks, and enhances robustness to measurement noise [45]. Such physics-informed constraints act as powerful inductive biases that prevent overfitting when labeled data is scarce, and as we will see in Section 7.2, incorporating domain knowledge in this manner also contributes to improved out-of-distribution robustness.

**Few-shot and zero-shot learning** frameworks directly address the challenge of predicting properties for novel targets or compounds when only minimal experimental data is available. Meta-learning approaches, including model-agnostic meta-learning (MAML) adapted for molecular graphs, enable models to rapidly adapt to new tasks with only a handful of labeled examples by learning generalizable initialization parameters from a distribution of training tasks. Zero-shot learning methods go further by attempting to predict activity for entirely new compounds or targets without any task-specific examples, leveraging structural or semantic embeddings to transfer knowledge from related domains. For example, zero-shot drug response prediction methods have been developed that learn invariant features from prior response data of similar drugs to predict responses for novel, unlabeled compounds, demonstrating meaningful performance improvements in preclinical drug screening scenarios [144]. Similarly, zero-shot 3D drug design approaches have been proposed that decouple the generation process from scarce experimental binding data by leveraging large-scale molecular databases to bridge the design stages [129].

**Data augmentation and synthetic data generation** offer complementary strategies for expanding the effective size of training sets. Molecular data augmentation can be performed by exploiting the symmetry of molecular representations—for instance, generating multiple valid SMILES strings for the same molecule through random permutation, or creating augmented graph views via atom masking, bond deletion, or subgraph removal. Synthetic data generation approaches use generative models or physics-based simulations to produce virtual molecules or molecular conformations that can supplement experimental datasets, though care must be taken to ensure that synthetic data accurately reflects the underlying distribution of real chemical space. Physics-based molecular dynamics simulations have been employed to generate contact matrices and structural data that provide rich training signals for downstream machine learning models without requiring additional experimental measurements [5].

**Transfer learning and pretraining on large unlabeled molecular corpora** have become foundational strategies for addressing labeled data scarcity. By pretraining models on millions of unlabeled molecules using self-supervised objectives such as masked atom prediction, SMILES reconstruction, or denoising, models can acquire rich representations of chemical space that transfer effectively to downstream tasks with limited labeled data. This paradigm mirrors the success of large language model pretraining in natural language processing and has demonstrated substantial improvements on molecular property prediction benchmarks, particularly in low-data regimes. Importantly, the breadth and diversity of the pretraining corpus plays a critical role in determining the degree to which learned representations transfer to structurally novel compounds—a point directly relevant to the out-of-distribution generalization challenges examined in the next section.

Addressing imbalanced class distributions requires specialized training strategies at the loss function level. Techniques such as focal loss, cost-sensitive learning, oversampling of minority classes, and directly optimizing area under the ROC curve (AUC-ROC) have been shown to improve model sensitivity to rare active compounds. Optimizing ROC-based cost functions is particularly well-suited to virtual screening tasks, where class imbalance is severe, decision thresholds vary widely, and performance at high specificity thresholds is of primary practical concern [50].

Finally, rigorous data curation and standardization practices are increasingly recognized as essential prerequisites for robust model development. Efforts to harmonize assay conditions, standardize compound representations, remove duplicate and contradictory annotations, and establish clear data provenance are critical to building drug discovery datasets that support reliable and generalizable machine learning models. Integrated approaches that combine careful data curation with algorithmic strategies for handling scarcity, imbalance, and noise represent the most promising path toward models that can be trusted to generalize across the diverse and challenging settings encountered in real-world drug discovery pipelines [1]. As we discuss in the following section, however, even with carefully curated and balanced training data, the challenge of generalizing reliably to molecules and biological contexts that lie outside the training distribution remains a distinct and formidable obstacle that demands dedicated methodological attention.

### 7.2 Out-of-Distribution Generalization and Covariate Shift

## 7.2 Out-of-Distribution Generalization and Covariate Shift

Building directly on the data scarcity, imbalance, and quality challenges examined in the previous section, a distinct but closely related challenge concerns the ability of trained models to generalize reliably to molecules, targets, and biological contexts that lie outside the distribution of their training data. This problem, broadly referred to as out-of-distribution (OOD) generalization, is particularly acute in the pharmaceutical domain due to the inherently exploratory nature of drug discovery: researchers are most interested in identifying novel chemical matter, probing previously unexplored biological targets, and discovering mechanisms of action that have not been observed before. By definition, the most scientifically and commercially valuable predictions are precisely those that lie furthest from the training distribution, making OOD robustness a fundamental requirement rather than a desirable add-on. Crucially, the strategies introduced in Section 7.1—such as transfer learning, physics-informed multi-task frameworks, and rigorous data curation—lay important groundwork for OOD robustness, but they do not fully resolve it; even well-curated and carefully balanced training sets leave models vulnerable to the distribution shifts that characterize real drug discovery campaigns.

**The Nature of Distribution Shift in Drug Discovery**

Distribution shift in molecular machine learning arises in several distinct but interrelated forms. Covariate shift occurs when the marginal distribution of input features—molecular structures, protein sequences, or physicochemical descriptors—differs between training and deployment, even if the conditional relationship between inputs and outputs remains stable. In practice, drug discovery campaigns routinely encounter this scenario when models trained on historical compound libraries are applied to novel chemical scaffolds, unexplored chemical space regions, or newly emerging therapeutic targets for which little prior data exists.

Scaffold-based distribution shifts represent a particularly well-documented and challenging form of covariate shift in molecular property prediction. Unlike random splits of molecular datasets, scaffold splits partition compounds such that molecules sharing the same core chemical framework (Bemis-Murcko scaffold) appear exclusively in either training or test sets. This more realistic evaluation protocol reveals that models trained with random splits frequently suffer dramatic performance degradation when evaluated on structurally dissimilar test compounds, exposing the extent to which many published models have learned scaffold-specific memorization rather than generalizable structure-activity relationships [19]. The critical insight is that the molecules of greatest pharmaceutical interest—those representing genuine structural novelty—are precisely those for which scaffold-split generalization is required.

Domain gaps present an additional layer of complexity. Models trained on assay data from one biological context, cell line, or experimental platform may fail to transfer when applied to a different context, even for nominally identical molecular properties. This issue is compounded by the heterogeneity of data sources typically aggregated in large-scale molecular databases, where measurements of ostensibly the same property may reflect systematic batch effects, assay-specific biases, or context-dependent biology that the model conflates with molecular features [1]. These domain gaps are intimately related to the label noise and data inconsistency problems discussed in Section 7.1: models trained on noisy or narrowly distributed data are particularly ill-equipped to handle the additional challenge of deployment-time distribution shift.

**Benchmarking OOD Generalization**

Recognizing the inadequacy of random-split evaluations for assessing real-world model performance, several dedicated benchmarking efforts have emerged to systematically characterize OOD generalization in drug discovery contexts. The Therapeutics Data Commons (TDC) provides a standardized collection of drug discovery datasets with carefully designed splitting strategies that simulate realistic deployment scenarios, including scaffold splits, time-based splits reflecting the temporal ordering of compound discovery, and cold-drug/cold-target splits that test generalization to entirely unseen entities [19]. These evaluation protocols consistently reveal that state-of-the-art models suffer substantial performance degradation under OOD conditions compared to their in-distribution performance, highlighting the gap between benchmark results and practical utility—a theme that will recur prominently in the reproducibility and benchmarking discussion of Section 7.3.

The DrugOOD benchmark specifically targets the OOD generalization problem in AI-aided drug discovery by providing a systematic framework for generating and evaluating distribution shift scenarios across multiple drug discovery tasks. DrugOOD introduces a diverse set of OOD challenges including assay-based shifts, scaffold-based shifts, and size-based shifts, enabling principled comparison of OOD robustness across different model architectures and training strategies. Evaluations on DrugOOD reveal that many architectures that achieve strong in-distribution performance suffer disproportionate degradation under OOD conditions, underscoring the need for methods that explicitly account for distribution shift during training and evaluation.

**Methodological Advances for OOD Robustness**

Addressing OOD generalization in molecular machine learning has stimulated a growing body of methodological innovation drawing on diverse perspectives from domain adaptation, causal inference, invariant risk minimization, and Bayesian reasoning.

*Domain-Informed Priors and Physicochemical Constraints.* One principled approach to improving OOD robustness is to incorporate domain knowledge directly into model architectures and training objectives, effectively constraining the hypothesis space to solutions consistent with known chemistry and physics. As noted in Section 7.1, physics-informed multi-task learning frameworks already demonstrate how encoding known physical relationships between pharmacokinetic parameters can reduce data demand and enhance robustness to measurement noise [45]. Extending this philosophy to OOD settings, physics-informed graph neural networks that incorporate known interaction terms such as van der Waals forces, electrostatics, and hydrogen bonding as inductive biases are less susceptible to learning spurious correlations from training data and tend to generalize more reliably to novel protein-ligand complexes [41]. Similarly, equivariant neural network architectures that respect the symmetries of molecular systems—rotational and translational invariance—provide a form of built-in structural prior that reduces the effective degrees of freedom available to the model and improves generalization across conformational and geometric variation.

Multi-task learning frameworks that simultaneously optimize across multiple related molecular property prediction tasks provide an implicit form of regularization that can improve OOD robustness by preventing overfitting to training-set-specific patterns and encouraging the learning of more generalizable molecular representations [1]. When the multiple tasks share underlying structural determinants, multi-task pretraining encourages the model to learn representations that capture fundamental chemical relationships rather than task-specific correlations.

*Domain Adaptation Strategies.* Domain adaptation methods seek to explicitly align the feature distributions of source (training) and target (deployment) domains, reducing the covariate shift that underlies OOD performance degradation. In the molecular context, this has been explored through adversarial domain adaptation, where a domain discriminator is trained alongside the property predictor to encourage the learning of domain-invariant molecular representations. Meta-learning approaches that train models to rapidly adapt to new tasks with limited data—closely related to the few-shot and zero-shot strategies discussed in Section 7.1—provide another avenue for addressing distribution shift, enabling models to leverage structural similarity between related tasks to improve generalization to novel targets and assay contexts [19].

Transfer learning from large pretrained molecular models represents perhaps the most widely adopted practical strategy for addressing domain gaps. Foundation models pretrained on large, diverse collections of molecular data learn rich, transferable representations that capture broad chemical knowledge, and can be fine-tuned on small task-specific datasets with substantially improved OOD generalization compared to models trained from scratch [1]. As emphasized in Section 7.1, the breadth and diversity of the pretraining corpus plays a critical role: models trained on structurally diverse molecular databases show stronger generalization to novel chemical scaffolds, directly mitigating the covariate shift that arises when applying models to unexplored chemical space.

*Causal Reasoning Frameworks.* An emerging and theoretically grounded approach to OOD generalization draws on the framework of causal inference, which distinguishes between stable causal relationships and spurious correlations that may hold in training data but fail to generalize. In drug discovery, many apparent structure-property correlations reflect confounding factors in dataset construction—such as the historical focus of medicinal chemistry on particular scaffold classes or the prevalence of certain assay conditions—rather than genuine causal mechanisms. Models that learn causal rather than associative relationships between molecular features and biological activity are expected to generalize more reliably to novel chemical contexts where the spurious correlations present in training data no longer hold.

Invariant risk minimization (IRM) and related frameworks operationalize this intuition by training models to find representations for which the optimal predictor is invariant across multiple training environments or data partitions, encouraging the capture of stable causal relationships rather than environment-specific correlations. Applied to molecular property prediction, these approaches have shown promise in improving generalization across scaffold classes and biological contexts, though their practical implementation requires careful construction of meaningful training environments that reflect genuine sources of variation [15].

**The Relationship Between OOD Generalization and Molecular Generation**

The OOD generalization challenge has important implications not only for predictive models but also for generative models used in de novo drug design. When generative models propose novel molecules, the property prediction models used to score and filter these candidates are necessarily being applied in an OOD regime—the generated molecules by construction represent novel chemical matter not present in training data. The unreliability of property predictions in this regime means that the success of AI-driven molecular optimization campaigns depends critically on how well the property prediction models generalize to structurally novel candidates [84].

This creates a fundamental tension in AI-driven drug discovery: the goal is to generate genuinely novel molecules with desired properties, but the tools used to assess those properties become increasingly unreliable as the structural novelty of the candidates increases. Addressing this tension requires either developing more reliable OOD property prediction methods, designing generative models that constrain their outputs to regions where predictive models are reliable, or incorporating uncertainty quantification to identify when property predictions are insufficiently trustworthy to guide optimization decisions [78]. This tension between structural novelty and prediction reliability is one that will reappear in Section 7.3's discussion of the gap between computational benchmarks and experimental validation outcomes.

**Evaluation and Reporting Standards**

Beyond methodological advances, improving OOD robustness in practice requires changes in evaluation and reporting standards. The field has historically favored random-split evaluation because it yields more favorable performance numbers and more straightforward comparisons, but this practice systematically overestimates the real-world utility of models. Adopting scaffold splits, temporal splits, and other OOD evaluation protocols as standard practice—as advocated by platforms like TDC—is essential for providing meaningful assessments of model performance and for driving methodological progress toward genuinely more generalizable models [19]. These evaluation and reporting concerns form the foundation of the broader reproducibility and benchmarking challenges examined in Section 7.3.

Additionally, reporting the gap between in-distribution and OOD performance provides crucial diagnostic information about the degree to which a model has learned generalizable chemical knowledge versus training-set-specific memorization. Models that maintain small performance gaps across evaluation protocols provide stronger evidence of generalizable learning and are more likely to deliver value in real-world drug discovery campaigns where the molecules of greatest interest are structurally novel. Equally important, as Section 7.3 will discuss, is the incorporation of uncertainty quantification into these evaluations: calibrated confidence estimates are essential for determining which OOD predictions should be trusted and prioritized for experimental follow-up, and for detecting when model predictions diverge systematically from experimental outcomes in ways that signal meaningful distribution shift.

In summary, OOD generalization and covariate shift represent central unsolved challenges in machine learning for drug discovery, with direct implications for the practical utility of AI-driven predictive and generative methods. Progress requires advances across multiple fronts: more realistic benchmarking protocols, novel architectures incorporating domain knowledge and causal reasoning, and principled uncertainty quantification that signals when model predictions should not be trusted. These requirements connect naturally to the reproducibility and validation challenges examined in the following section, where the question of whether computational results translate meaningfully to experimental outcomes emerges as the ultimate test of OOD generalization in practice [15; 1].

### 7.3 Reproducibility, Benchmarking, and Experimental Validation Gaps

## 7.3 Reproducibility, Benchmarking, and Experimental Validation

The challenges of out-of-distribution generalization discussed in the previous section are deeply intertwined with a broader set of methodological concerns surrounding reproducibility, benchmarking rigor, and the translation of computational predictions into experimentally validated outcomes. Even when models demonstrate strong performance under carefully constructed evaluation protocols, the question of whether these results are meaningful and reproducible across different implementations, datasets, and experimental contexts remains a persistent and pressing concern in machine learning for drug discovery.

Reproducibility remains one of the most pressing challenges in machine learning for drug discovery. Across the literature, inconsistent dataset splits, heterogeneous evaluation metrics, and incompatible experimental protocols make it exceedingly difficult to draw fair and meaningful comparisons between models. A study may report superior performance simply because it uses a more favorable data partition or a less stringent evaluation criterion, rather than because of genuine methodological advances. This problem is compounded by the diversity of molecular property prediction tasks, where different works benchmark on entirely different subsets of datasets, preventing systematic cross-study comparison [28].

A related concern is the inconsistency of evaluation metrics. Some studies optimize for ROC-AUC, while others prefer AUPRC, concordance index, or root mean squared error, depending on the task formulation [50]. Without agreed-upon standards, claims of state-of-the-art performance are difficult to contextualize. The diversity of molecular representations—ranging from SMILES strings, molecular graphs, and fingerprints to 3D conformer-based descriptors—further complicates comparison, as models differ not only in architecture but also in the fundamental nature of their inputs [22; 113].

Benchmark platforms such as MoleculeNet, Therapeutic Data Commons (TDC), and DOCKSTRING have been proposed to address these issues by providing standardized datasets, splits, and evaluation protocols [25]. As discussed in the context of OOD generalization, the choice of splitting strategy is critical: platforms like TDC that offer scaffold splits, temporal splits, and cold-target splits provide a far more realistic picture of model performance than those relying exclusively on random partitions. DOCKSTRING in particular argues that docking-based benchmarks more faithfully represent real-world drug design objectives compared to simpler physicochemical property benchmarks, which may not reflect the complexity of target-specific binding [25]. Similarly, large-scale empirical studies have demonstrated that the best-performing method is often dataset-dependent—classical methods such as Gaussian Processes sometimes outperform deep learning on QSAR tasks, while graph neural networks and language models may excel on ADMET datasets—highlighting that no universal solution exists and that thorough benchmarking is indispensable [28].

The meta-learning perspective on QSAR benchmarking further reinforces this point: across more than 2,700 QSAR problems, no single method consistently dominates, suggesting that algorithm selection and meta-learning strategies can outperform fixed architectures by significant margins [21]. This comprehensive scale of comparison is rarely achieved in individual publications, and the field would benefit greatly from adopting similarly rigorous large-scale evaluation norms.

Reproducibility in generative modeling presents its own unique difficulties. Metrics such as validity, uniqueness, novelty, and drug-likeness are frequently reported, but their implementation details vary across codebases and experimental setups. The benchmark proposed in [145] specifically illustrates how graph-based generative models often fail to produce high-docking-score molecules when evaluated under realistic conditions, exposing optimistic biases in how generative models are commonly assessed. The introduction of the delta score [26] similarly highlights that even when generated molecules achieve high docking scores, they may not satisfy realistic pharmaceutical requirements—underscoring the inadequacy of relying solely on docking metrics as proxy measures of drug candidate quality. This observation connects directly to the tension identified in Section 7.2 between the goal of generating structurally novel molecules and the reliability of the property prediction models used to evaluate them: in both cases, computational surrogates can paint an overly optimistic picture that fails to hold under rigorous scrutiny.

Beyond computational benchmarking, the persistent gap between in silico predictions and experimental wet-lab validation remains a fundamental unresolved challenge. Predicted binding affinities, ADMET properties, and molecular generation outputs are typically assessed using computational surrogates such as docking scores or physics-based scoring functions, which themselves carry significant uncertainty and systematic errors [24]. The translation from high-performing computational results to experimentally validated hits and leads is far from guaranteed, and many promising AI-generated or AI-selected compounds fail in downstream biological testing due to off-target effects, poor solubility, metabolic instability, or other unanticipated liabilities [27].

Closed-loop validation strategies, where computational predictions are systematically tested against experimental outcomes in iterative cycles, represent a promising direction for closing this gap [34; 146]. In RECOVER, for example, sequential model-based optimization guided wet-lab experiments for drug combination synergy, demonstrating that iterative ML-guided experimentation can efficiently enrich for biologically active combinations while substantially reducing the experimental burden compared to exhaustive screening [146]. Such frameworks exemplify how tighter integration between computational predictions and experimental feedback loops can help ground AI-driven drug discovery in real-world biological outcomes. Crucially, these iterative cycles also provide a natural mechanism for detecting and correcting OOD failures: when model predictions diverge systematically from experimental results on novel chemical matter, the discrepancy itself signals a distribution shift that can inform model retraining and improved uncertainty quantification.

Open-source pipelines and software tools play an essential role in improving reproducibility. Frameworks that standardize data preprocessing, model training, and evaluation workflows reduce idiosyncratic implementation differences that obscure true model performance. Uncertainty quantification is another critical component of reliable validation; without calibrated confidence estimates, it is impossible to determine which predictions should be trusted and prioritized for experimental follow-up [28]. Ensemble methods and Bayesian approaches provide practical avenues for quantifying predictive uncertainty, and their integration into benchmarking standards is increasingly recognized as necessary for responsible deployment of ML models in drug discovery pipelines [23].

In summary, the field urgently requires community-wide adoption of standardized benchmarking practices, transparent reporting of evaluation protocols, uncertainty-aware model validation, and systematic experimental testing of computational predictions. These requirements are closely aligned with the need for interpretable and explainable models—the subject of the following section—since models whose predictions can be explained and audited are inherently more amenable to rigorous validation and reproducibility assessment. Only by closing these reproducibility and validation gaps can machine learning for drug discovery fulfill its transformative potential and earn the trust of the broader pharmaceutical research community.

### 7.4 Explainable AI and Interpretability in Drug Discovery

## 7.4 Explainable AI and Interpretability in Drug Discovery

As machine learning models grow increasingly complex and are deployed in high-stakes pharmaceutical decision-making, the demand for explainability has become a central concern in AI-driven drug discovery. The reproducibility and validation challenges discussed in Section 7.3 are closely intertwined with interpretability: models whose predictions can be explained and audited are inherently more amenable to rigorous benchmarking, cross-laboratory validation, and experimental follow-up. Explainable artificial intelligence (XAI) encompasses a broad family of techniques designed to make model predictions interpretable and transparent to human experts, enabling scientists and regulators to understand, validate, and trust the outputs of otherwise opaque deep learning systems [78; 138].

### 7.4.1 The Need for Interpretability in Drug Discovery

The pharmaceutical research context imposes unique requirements on AI models. Unlike many consumer applications, drug discovery decisions carry significant scientific, clinical, and regulatory consequences. A predictive model that identifies a promising lead compound or flags a potential toxicity risk must ideally provide a rationale that can be scrutinized by medicinal chemists and regulatory bodies. Without interpretability, it is difficult to determine whether a model has learned genuinely meaningful structure-activity relationships (SAR) or is instead exploiting spurious correlations in training data—precisely the kind of artifact that undermines out-of-distribution generalization and benchmarking validity as discussed in the preceding sections [60]. The reproducibility crisis in machine learning further underscores the importance of transparency: models that cannot explain their predictions are harder to audit and validate across laboratories [1].

Moreover, interpretability is essential for closing the loop between computational predictions and experimental design. Medicinal chemists need to understand which molecular substructures or physicochemical features drive a predicted biological activity in order to rationally guide synthesis and optimization campaigns [22]. XAI methods thus serve as a bridge between statistical learning and domain knowledge, enabling AI tools to function as genuine scientific collaborators rather than black-box oracles. This bridging role complements the uncertainty quantification methods discussed in the following section, which address the related but distinct need for calibrated confidence estimates alongside interpretable predictions.

### 7.4.2 Model-Agnostic Interpretability Methods

Model-agnostic XAI methods are applicable to any predictive model regardless of its internal architecture, making them broadly useful across the diverse array of ML approaches employed in drug discovery. Among the most widely used are perturbation-based methods such as LIME (Local Interpretable Model-agnostic Explanations) and SHAP (SHapley Additive exPlanations), which attribute predictions to individual input features by systematically varying input values and measuring the impact on model output. In the molecular property prediction context, these methods can highlight which molecular fragments, atoms, or physicochemical descriptors are most influential in driving a given prediction [138].

These approaches are particularly valuable when the underlying model is a complex ensemble or a neural network whose internal representations are not directly interpretable. By generating local explanations around individual molecules of interest, model-agnostic methods support medicinal chemists in forming hypotheses about SAR, identifying potentially problematic functional groups for toxicity, and prioritizing molecular modifications during lead optimization [78].

### 7.4.3 Gradient-Based Attribution Methods

Gradient-based attribution techniques are model-specific methods that exploit the internal computational graph of differentiable models—particularly deep neural networks—to assign importance scores to input features. Methods such as saliency maps, integrated gradients, GradCAM, and DeepLIFT compute how the model's output changes with respect to each input dimension by backpropagating attribution signals through the network. In the context of molecular inputs represented as atomic features or fingerprint bits, gradient-based methods can highlight individual atoms or bonds whose modification would most strongly influence a predicted property such as binding affinity or toxicity [78; 138].

These methods have been applied to convolutional neural networks operating on molecular images, as well as to sequence-based models trained on SMILES representations. In SMILES-based models, gradient attribution can identify specific tokens or subsequences that contribute most to predictions, providing chemists with intuitive visualization of SAR across molecular series. Importantly, gradient-based methods are computationally efficient compared to perturbation-based alternatives, making them well-suited for high-throughput drug discovery workflows.

### 7.4.4 Attention Mechanisms as Interpretability Tools

The transformer architecture's attention mechanism has been widely adopted as a built-in source of interpretability in molecular property prediction models. Attention weights, which quantify the degree to which different parts of the input are consulted when generating a prediction, can be visualized to identify which atoms, bonds, or molecular fragments a model focuses on during inference [79]. In multi-modal models that simultaneously process SMILES sequences and molecular descriptors, attention maps can reveal how different types of chemical information are combined to generate predictions.

However, the interpretation of attention weights as direct proxies for feature importance has been the subject of debate. Attention distributions do not always correspond to gradient-based importance and can be misleading in highly contextualized transformer models. Nevertheless, in practice, attention-based visualization remains one of the most accessible and widely deployed tools for communicating model behavior to non-expert stakeholders, including medicinal chemists and regulatory scientists [78; 138].

### 7.4.5 GNN Explanation Techniques

Graph neural networks (GNNs) have become the dominant architecture for molecular property prediction due to their ability to operate directly on molecular graph representations. Explaining GNN predictions presents unique challenges, as the input is structured rather than tabular, and the relevant explanatory unit may be a subgraph, a set of atoms, or a particular bond rather than an individual feature [42].

Several GNN-specific explanation methods have been developed to address this challenge. GNNExplainer identifies minimal subgraphs that are most responsible for a given prediction by optimizing a mutual information objective between the original prediction and the subgraph-conditioned prediction. Similarly, PGExplainer learns a parameterized mask over the graph structure, enabling edge-level explanations at scale. Hierarchical graph explainability methods provide multi-level explanations that identify not only individual atoms and bonds but also higher-order molecular motifs—such as functional groups or ring systems—that are collectively responsible for driving predictions [78]. Such motif-level explanations are particularly valuable in medicinal chemistry, where structure-activity relationships are often framed in terms of functional group contributions.

In the context of drug-target interaction prediction, GNN explanation methods have been used to identify potential binding site residues and pharmacophoric elements, providing mechanistically interpretable predictions that align with known structural biology [42].

### 7.4.6 Machine-Learned Pharmacophores and SAR Elucidation

Beyond post-hoc explanation of individual predictions, XAI methods are increasingly being used to distill machine-learned models into interpretable chemical rules and pharmacophore hypotheses. By aggregating explanation maps across a set of active compounds, researchers can identify consistent molecular patterns that the model associates with activity, effectively automating the generation of pharmacophore models from data-driven predictions [78; 138].

These machine-learned pharmacophores can be compared to experimentally derived pharmacophoric features to validate model understanding and identify cases where the model has learned chemically meaningful patterns versus dataset artifacts. This alignment of computational interpretations with known medicinal chemistry knowledge is critical for building scientific trust in AI-driven discovery tools and ensuring that generated hypotheses are actionable in a laboratory setting. Notably, this validation process also provides a form of indirect reproducibility check, linking the concerns of Section 7.3 back to interpretability: explanations that consistently align with established SAR across different datasets and implementations lend additional credibility to model predictions.

### 7.4.7 Interpretability in Toxicity and ADMET Prediction

Toxicity prediction represents one of the most consequential applications of XAI in drug discovery, as false negatives—failure to flag a toxic compound—can have severe clinical consequences. Explainability methods applied to toxicity models can identify structural alerts: specific molecular substructures or reactive features associated with adverse effects. These alerts can be communicated directly to medicinal chemists to guide synthetic modifications that mitigate toxicity while preserving potency [138; 15].

Similarly, for ADMET property prediction, gradient and attention-based methods can reveal which molecular regions influence absorption, distribution, metabolism, or excretion profiles, enabling targeted lead optimization strategies. The integration of XAI into ADMET modeling pipelines supports more rational decision-making at the hit-to-lead and lead optimization stages, reducing the likelihood of late-stage clinical failures due to poor pharmacokinetic properties [22]. As will be discussed in Section 7.5, uncertainty quantification methods for ADMET prediction are most actionable when paired with interpretable explanations, since decision-makers need to understand not only how uncertain a prediction is but also which molecular features are responsible for that uncertainty.

### 7.4.8 Regulatory Compliance and Scientific Trust

The practical deployment of AI in pharmaceutical research is increasingly subject to regulatory scrutiny. Regulatory agencies including the FDA and EMA have expressed interest in frameworks for the validation and qualification of AI/ML-based tools in drug development. Interpretability is a key component of these frameworks: a model whose predictions can be explained and validated against known chemistry is far more likely to gain regulatory acceptance than an opaque black box [138; 1].

XAI methods thus serve a dual function: they support scientific discovery by providing actionable insights to researchers, and they support regulatory compliance by enabling documentation and audit of model behavior. The development of standardized protocols for interpretability validation—analogous to those used for validating experimental assays—represents an important frontier for the field [60]. These regulatory considerations connect directly to the calibration challenges discussed in Section 7.5: regulators require not only that model predictions be explainable, but also that their associated confidence estimates be formally justified and well-calibrated.

### 7.4.9 Visualization Tools and Interactive Platforms

Interactive visualization platforms represent an important complement to algorithmic XAI methods, enabling researchers to explore model predictions and explanations in an intuitive, hands-on manner. Tools that integrate molecular structure visualization with prediction and explanation outputs allow medicinal chemists to interrogate model behavior dynamically, modify molecular structures, and observe how predicted properties and explanations change in response [147]. Such platforms lower the barrier to adoption of AI tools by non-computational scientists and facilitate productive collaboration between computational and experimental researchers.

The development of open-source, user-friendly software platforms that integrate property prediction, molecular generation, and XAI—such as TorchDrug [46]—has been instrumental in democratizing access to interpretable AI tools in drug discovery. As these platforms mature and incorporate state-of-the-art explanation methods, they will play an increasingly important role in translating AI research into practical pharmaceutical impact, and in supporting the transparent, reproducible workflows emphasized throughout this section.

### 7.4.10 Open Challenges in XAI for Drug Discovery

Despite significant progress, numerous challenges remain in the application of XAI to drug discovery. A fundamental difficulty is the evaluation of explanation quality: unlike model predictions, which can be validated against experimental measurements, explanations are inherently subjective and may be assessed through consistency, fidelity, and alignment with domain knowledge rather than objective ground truth [138]. Different explanation methods applied to the same model can yield conflicting attributions, making it difficult to determine which explanations to trust.

Furthermore, the relationship between model interpretability and model accuracy is not straightforward. Highly accurate deep learning models often achieve their performance through complex, high-dimensional representations that resist simple explanation, while more interpretable models such as decision trees or linear models may sacrifice predictive performance. Striking the right balance between interpretability and accuracy for specific drug discovery applications remains an active area of research [78; 1].

Finally, most current XAI methods operate at the level of individual predictions or molecular features, but drug discovery scientists often require explanations at the level of mechanisms, pathways, and biological hypotheses. Developing XAI approaches that can generate and validate higher-level biological explanations—connecting molecular features to protein binding mechanisms, off-target effects, and clinical outcomes—represents a major open challenge that will require tight integration of machine learning with domain knowledge and causal reasoning [60; 42]. Addressing this challenge is also a prerequisite for the well-calibrated, trustworthy uncertainty estimates that are the subject of the following section, since uncertainty quantification is most scientifically meaningful when it can be grounded in mechanistic understanding rather than purely statistical signal.

### 7.5 Uncertainty Quantification and Calibration

## 7.5 Uncertainty Quantification and Calibration

Uncertainty quantification (UQ) has emerged as a critical component of reliable machine learning workflows in drug discovery.
